At 128K, the router preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 (vs. SSA-style top-k at 0.09 on the former) while the fused selection-plus-kernel pipeline runs at 0.62x and 0.80x dense wall time.

</details>


### [79] [SHIFT: Survival Prediction from Incomplete and Heterogeneous Genomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07725)
*Muhammet Sami Yavuz,Ayhan Can Erdur,Sabri Mustafa Kahya,Benedikt Wiestler,Jana Lipkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SHIFT is a missingness-aware transformer for survival prediction from incomplete genomic data, enabling cross-cohort generalization without test-time imputation by using per-feature representations and masks, plus variable-rate training masking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-institution sequencing panel differences cause structural missingness that hampers transfer of genomic survival models; existing work either restricts genes, excludes incomplete profiles, or imputes at test time, reducing robustness.

Method: Propose SHIFT: a transformer-based survival model that treats each genomic feature separately, applies masked self-attention with a feature-availability mask; training uses variable-rate feature masking to simulate heterogeneous missingness; evaluated on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with multi-cohort external validation across panel-mismatched settings; uses a single model across varying feature sets.

Result: SHIFT demonstrates strong generalization and favorable performance compared with standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches across several cohorts with severe panel mismatch; including incomplete cohorts during development improves external performance.

Conclusion: Missingness-aware modeling is a practical strategy for robust multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology; SHIFT enables use of incomplete data without imputation and supports integrating partially observed cohorts in model development.

Abstract: Genomic prediction models often fail to transfer across institutions because sequencing panels differ across sites, creating structural feature missingness at deployment. Existing approaches to this challenge typically restrict analysis to genes shared across cohorts, exclude patients with incomplete profiles, or rely on test-time imputation, all of which can reduce robustness and limit the use of multi-center data. We propose Survival prediction Handling Incomplete Features using Transformer (SHIFT), a missingness-aware survival model that directly predicts from incomplete genomic inputs without test-time imputation. SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately and uses masked self-attention, along with a feature-availability mask, so that predictions are based only on observed inputs. Further, we introduce variable-rate feature masking during training to improve robustness to heterogeneous missingness patterns. We evaluate the approach on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with external validation across multiple cohorts, including a challenging setting with severe cross-cohort panel mismatch. Across these settings, SHIFT shows strong generalization and compares favorably with standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches, while using a single model across differing feature sets. We also find that incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during development can improve performance on external data, suggesting that partially observed cohorts need not be excluded from model building. These results support missingness-aware modeling as a practical strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology.

</details>


### [80] [Jet-Long: Efficient Long-Context Extension with Dynamic Bifocal RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07740)
*Haozhan Tang,Zerui Wang,Yuxian Gu,Song Han,Han Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Jet-Long is a tuning-free zero-shot method that dynamically adapts the long-context window by combining a RoPE-faithful short window with an adaptive long-range window, enabling near-base fidelity at short inputs and clean extrapolation at long contexts, with high throughput and strong downstream performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot long-context extension for open-weight LLMs in long-context deployments; fixed rescaling factors harm fidelity at short contexts or fail at long contexts; need a dynamic, tuning-free solution that preserves base-model behavior.

Method: Bifocal attention: a local RoPE-faithful window plus a long-range window; adaptive rescaling factor tied to current sequence length; inclusion-exclusion attention merge; on-the-fly RoPE rotation; CuTe kernel; prefill and generation performance; generalizes to Jet-Nemotron; hyperparameter-resilient.

Result: On Qwen models up to 128K context: improvements over RULER by 4.79/2.18/2.03 pp at 1.7B/4B/8B; best HELMET-RAG accuracy; lowest PG-19 perplexity; prefill throughput up to 1.39x FA2 on H100; generation overhead ≤ 4%; flexible to hybrid attention without retraining.

Conclusion: Jet-Long delivers tuning-free, adaptive long-context extension for open-weight LLMs with strong quantitative gains and practical efficiency; approach generalizes beyond standard transformers to hybrid attention architectures; highly deployment-friendly due to hyperparameter resilience.

Abstract: Modern LLMs are increasingly deployed in long-context applications such as retrieval-augmented generation, repository-level coding, and agentic workflows whose accumulated reasoning and tool traces routinely push the input an order of magnitude past the pretraining window, making zero-shot context extension the dominant deployment path for open-weight checkpoints. Most existing zero-shot methods fix a single rescaling factor up front, so an aggressive factor sacrifices short-context fidelity while a conservative one breaks down at long contexts. We propose Jet-Long, a tuning-free zero-shot method that pairs a local RoPE-faithful window with a long-range window whose rescaling factor adapts dynamically to the current sequence length, recovering the base model exactly at short inputs while extrapolating cleanly at long ones. An inclusion-exclusion attention merge and an on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation make the bifocal construction essentially free at inference; fused into a single CuTe kernel, long-context prefill reaches up to $1.39\times$ FA2 throughput on H100 (approaching the Hopper-only FA4), and single-batch generation incurs $\le 4\%$ overhead at every length. On Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B up to 128K context, Jet-Long leads RULER by $+4.79$/$+2.18$/$+2.03$~pp over the strongest baseline at 1.7B/4B/8B, achieves the best overall accuracy on HELMET-RAG (a benchmark identified by HELMET as the most efficient predictor of downstream long-context performance) and attains the lowest PG-19 perplexity. Jet-Long also generalizes to hybrid attention architectures such as Jet-Nemotron for further long-context improvement without retraining, and remains hyperparameter-resilient for ease of deployment.

</details>


### [81] [Architecture Generalization with MetaNCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07743)
*Meet Barot,Daniel Berenberg,Sina Khajehabdollahi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaNCA learns local update rules to self-organize neural network weights via a Weight Transformer with linear attention, generating MLP, CNN, and ResNet weights for MNIST and CIFAR-100 up to 2M parameters; it generalizes to unseen architectures, with diversity in meta-training improving generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To emulate biology-inspired self-organization and local information processing by transferring the strength of neural cellular automata to neural network weight configuration, enabling adaptable, robust, and backpropagation-free weight generation.

Method: Introduce Meta Neural Cellular Automata (MetaNCA): a rule network that iteratively updates the weights of a target network using only local interactions on the computation graph. A Weight Transformer with linear attention aggregates signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. The rule network is meta-trained across a distribution of architectures; at inference, it generates weights for a new architecture without backpropagation.

Result: MetaNCA successfully generates task-network weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks of ~2 million parameters. It generalizes to architectures not seen during meta-training, and architectural diversity in the meta-training phase strengthens this generalization.

Conclusion: Local-rule-based weight self-organization via MetaNCA is a viable approach for diverse architectures and datasets, enabling backpropagation-free weight generation and highlighting the benefit of architectural diversity during meta-training for improved generalization.

Abstract: Self-organization is an emergent property of life, driven by the collective behavior of individual components acting on local information. Biological neurons, through local interactions transmitted through synapses, are able to learn efficiently and can adapt their connections over an organism's lifespan. Motivated by these desirable properties of adaptability and local interaction, neural cellular automata (NCA) models have been successful at learning morphogenesis solely through local update rules, demonstrating stability over many updates and robustness to perturbations. In this work, we introduce Meta Neural Cellular Automata (MetaNCA), a framework that learns local rules which self-organize the weights of artificial neural networks. A learned rule network iteratively updates the weights of a task network using only local interactions on the computation graph. We propose a novel Weight Transformer architecture for the local rule network, which uses linear attention to aggregate signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. Once trained, the rule network generates task networks of diverse architectures without backpropagation. We show that MetaNCA generates weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks of 2 million parameters. We further show that MetaNCA generalizes to architectures not seen during meta-training, and that architectural diversity in the training phase strengthens this generalization.

</details>


### [82] [LiST: Lipschitz Scaling Training for Robust and Calibrated Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07745)
*Arthur Chiron,Franck Mamalet,Thomas Massena,Thomas Deltort,Mathieu Serrurier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LiST introduces Lipschitz Scaling Training to jointly optimize accuracy, robustness, and calibration by tuning the global Lipschitz constant; it connects Lipschitz constraints with Temperature Scaling to select calibrated operating points on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front and constructs a calibrated Pareto frontier with a margin-based loss, reintegrating calibration data for better sample efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Achieve reliable neural networks that are simultaneously accurate, robust to perturbations, and well-calibrated. Lipschitz constraints improve robustness but control of the accuracy-robustness trade-off via L is poorly understood, and calibration underexplored in Lipschitz regimes.

Method: Establish a theoretical and empirical link between the Lipschitz bound L and Temperature Scaling calibration. Propose Lipschitz Scaling Training (LiST) that iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant to reach a target calibrated operating point on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front. Introduce a margin-based loss to enable construction of a fully calibrated Pareto front. At convergence, reintegrate calibration data into training to improve sample efficiency without sacrificing calibration.

Result: LiST demonstrates competitive accuracy and robustness on CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny-ImageNet compared to constrained and unconstrained baselines and remains calibrated out-of-the-box. It enables a fully calibrated Pareto front and, by reusing calibration data, improves sample efficiency.

Conclusion: Calibration is a principled criterion for selecting operating points on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front in Lipschitz-constrained models. LiST provides a practical training framework to achieve calibrated trade-offs, preserving calibration while balancing accuracy and robustness, with demonstrated effectiveness on standard datasets and publicly available code.

Abstract: While accuracy, robustness, and calibration are all essential for reliable neural networks, they are often studied separately; developing models that satisfy all three simultaneously remains a central challenge. Lipschitz-constrained models guarantee robustness by design, yet the manual selection of the Lipschitz constraint L governs the resulting accuracy-robustness trade-off, and their calibration properties remain largely underexplored. In this work, we highlight a theoretical and empirical link between the enforced Lipschitz constraint and Temperature Scaling, a state-of-the-art calibration method. Specifically, we find that for a given training scheme, there exists a non-trivial value L* that yields an out-of-the-box calibrated network, and that calibration acts as a principled criterion to select a well-defined operating point on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front. Leveraging these insights, we introduce Lipschitz Scaling Training (LiST), a novel training paradigm that iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant to reach this operating point. Through a margin parameter in the training loss, LiST further enables the construction of a fully calibrated Pareto front, allowing users to navigate the accuracy-robustness trade-off while remaining calibrated throughout. At convergence, LiST also enables the reintegration of calibration data into training, improving sample efficiency without sacrificing calibration. We validate LiST on CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny-ImageNet, demonstrating competitive accuracy and robustness against constrained and unconstrained baselines, while remaining calibrated out of the box. Code is available at GitHub.

</details>


### [83] [Selective Left-Shift: Turning Test-Time Compute and Difficulty-based Curation into Training Data for Low-Resource Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07748)
*Didula Samaraweera,Anjana Supun,Srinath Perera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-phase pipeline to boost Small Language Models on low-resource programming languages by shifting syntax learning offline, then fine-tuning on synthetic data and using RL with verifiable rewards; achieves substantial gains in Julia and even Ballerina with reduced data and cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRPLs suffer from data scarcity and the computational trilemma: supervised fine-tuning data bottleneck, expensive inference-time scaling, and limited gains from learning from scratch. Need to decouple syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning to improve SLMs.

Method: Phase 1: offline data synthesis with iterative compiler/test feedback to generate verified training examples (left-shifted compute). Phase 2: supervised fine-tuning on synthetic/verified data to embed strong syntactic priors. Phase 3: reinforcement learning with verifiable reward grounded on language-agnostic I/O tests, with SFT prior constraining exploration away from syntax errors.

Result: On Qwen3-8B, pass@1 gains: Julia: +7.6 (MultiPL-E) and +14.2 (Agnostics LiveCodeBench). Data efficiency: use 1/3 data and 1/6 cost vs prior SOTA. Generalizes to Ballerina with 49.7% Pass@1 on MultiPL-E. Ablations show both SFT and execution-grounded rewards are needed for stable training.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline effectively decouples syntax and reasoning, yielding data- and cost-efficient improvements for LRPLs, with demonstrated generalization to a non-pretrained language.

Abstract: Large Language Models achieve strong code generation for high resource languages like Python and Java but suffer sharp performance drops on Low-Resource Programming Languages~(LRPLs) such as Julia. Improving Small Language Models~(SLMs) for these languages faces a trilemma: Supervised Fine-Tuning~(SFT) is bottlenecked by data scarcity, inference-time scaling is too expensive for deployment, and Reinforcement Learning from scratch yields near zero advantages. We propose a three-phase pipeline that resolves this trilemma by decoupling syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning. First, we \emph{left-shift} inference-time compute to an offline data synthesis engine that uses iterative compiler and test feedback to generate verified training examples. Second, we fine-tune an SLM on this synthetic, verified data to embed strong syntactic priors. Third, we apply Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward~(RLVR) grounded by language-agnostic Input/Output tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration away from syntax errors. Applied to Qwen3-8B, our pipeline improves pass@1 by up to +7.6 points on MultiPL-E and +14.2 points on the Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia compared to SOTA results. Furthermore, the pipeline only used $\frac{1}{3}$ data and $\frac{1}{6}$ cost over the previous state-of-the-art. We further demonstrate that the pipeline generalizes to Ballerina achieving 49.7\% MultiPL-E Pass@1, a language with near-zero pretraining representation. Ablations confirm that both the SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

</details>


### [84] [A Transdiagnostic Space of Disorder Like Phenotypes in Reinforcement Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07753)
*Hari Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recasts disorder modelling as dose-controlled appraisal perturbations in an appraisal-guided PPO, yielding monotone, graded disorder effects across seven conditions, predictable comorbidity patterns, and cross-domain transfer to a pixel-based env without the appraisal critic; demonstrates controllable affective phenotypes and treatment-like retractions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a testbed for computational psychiatry and illuminate failure modes of affective control by parameterizing psychiatric-like states as manipulable appraisal signals, enabling principled, preregistered assays and reproducible dose–response analysis across domains.

Method: Treat appraisal weights as seven knobs corresponding to anxiety, mania, OCD checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, and PTSD within an appraisal-guided PPO. Define preregistered assays for each symptom. Run >1000 RL trials (10 seeds, 4 controls) with 95% CIs. Map disorders in a two-step process: in gridworld with an appraisal critic; test persistence via ablations and graded exposure; validate cross-domain transfer by re-implementing three knobs in MiniWorld with a standard CNN agent (no appraisal critic).

Result: All seven disorders show a graded, monotone dose–response; controls do not reproduce these effects. Disorders self-organize into a two-dimensional affective space (mania ≈ anxiety). Removing a knob can remit reward-distorting disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which respond to graded exposure. Two knobs interact nonadditively, yielding testable comorbidity predictions. Three disorder knobs transfer to MiniWorld, with cross-assay dissociation observed across domains, indicating generality beyond the specific RL setup and abolition of the appraisal critic.

Conclusion: The framework yields a controllable space of affective phenotypes via appraisal-knob manipulation, enabling modeling of both disorder and treatment within a unified parameterization. The approach supports cross-domain predictions (comorbidity, persistence, and transfer) and generalizes beyond grid-worlds and the appraisal critic, strengthening the computational psychiatry testbed for affective disorders.

Abstract: Modelling psychological disorders in artificial agents offers both a testbed for computational psychiatry and a lens on the failure modes of affective control. Prior work induces one or two disorders in a reinforcement learning (RL) agent by hand-tuned reward shaping, labels the behaviour post hoc, and reports single runs. We recast disorder modelling as dose-controllable manipulation of cognitive appraisal signals in an appraisal-guided PPO agent, expressing seven disorders (anxiety, mania, obsessive-compulsive checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, and post-traumatic stress) each as a single knob grounded in a computational psychiatry account, with each symptom measured by a preregistered assay mapped to a recognised paradigm. Across more than a thousand runs (10 seeds, four controls, 95% confidence intervals) every disorder shows a graded, monotone dose-response that no control reproduces. Beyond these induced effects, three findings emerge that were not written into the reward: the disorders self-organise into a two-dimensional affective space in which mania mirrors anxiety; removing a knob remits reward distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which instead recover under a graded exposure curriculum; and two simultaneous knobs interact nonadditively, yielding testable comorbidity predictions. Appraisal weights thus parameterise a controllable space of affective phenotypes in which the same knobs that induce a disorder can model its treatment. We also show that three disorder knobs (depression, addiction, anxiety) transfer to a three-dimensional pixel environment (MiniWorld) with a standard convolutional agent and no appraisal critic, with cross-assay dissociation confirmed across both domains, indicating the framework is not specific to grid worlds or to PPO's appraisal critic.

</details>


### [85] [Image classification via a quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07754)
*Kumari Jyoti,Rohith Babu,Apoorva D. Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hybrid classical-quantum inspired mixture of experts for image classification using amplitude encoding, local unitary convolution, and stabiliser-code feature extraction; multiple experts processed same image; classical fully connected network for final prediction; on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST, joint expert analysis outperforms individual experts and halves failure rate; moderate GPU overhead; potential execution on a quantum processor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve pattern recognition by leveraging quantum-inspired processing and ensemble learning to capture diverse representations while retaining a predominantly classical training pipeline.

Method: Amplitude encoding of images; convolution using local unitary operations; multiple experts with different parameters processing the same image; feature extraction using quantum stabiliser codes; classical fully connected neural network combines features for final image classification.

Result: Joint analysis from multiple experts outperforms individual experts and reduces the failure rate by about a factor of two on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST benchmarks; overhead on GPUs is moderate; the quantum part can be executed on a quantum processor in principle.

Conclusion: The proposed hybrid classical-quantum inspired framework with a mixture of experts is a practical and potentially scalable approach that can yield significant performance gains; it serves as a bridge toward quantum-aware hardware implementations and warrants further exploration and ablation studies.

Abstract: Pattern recognition problems arise in a variety of physical image processing situations, and convolutional neural networks are a popular scheme for the required feature extraction and classification tasks. The classical networks use diffusion-based smearing and block-wise pooling to downsample the image data and capture important structural features. In this work, we propose and demonstrate a more efficient quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts. It is a hybrid classical-quantum framework. The quantum part consists of amplitude encoding of the images, convolution using local unitary operations, multiple experts processing the same image with different parameters, and feature extraction using quantum stabiliser codes. The classical part then jointly processes the features extracted by different experts using a standard fully connected neural network for image class prediction. Using MNIST and Fashion-MNIST datasets as benchmarks, we demonstrate that the joint expert analysis outperforms the individual expert one, as well as reduces the failure rate of image class prediction by around a factor of two. The overhead of our quantum-inspired strategy is only moderate on GPU workstations, which makes our proposal a practical alternative to existing classical schemes. We also point out how the quantum part of our framework can be executed on a quantum processor.

</details>


### [86] [The Importance of Encoder Choice:A Tabular-Image Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07756)
*Ilia Koloiarov,Diego Coello de Portugal Mecke,Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi,Tom Hanika,Lars Schmidt-Thieme*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Evaluates state-of-the-art tabular encoders as image-tabular multimodal encoders and addresses label-dependency in In-Context Learning to show encoder quality is a key driver in multimodal performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular data are challenging for deep learning and are often encodied by plain MLPs in multimodal setups. The paper hypothesizes that stronger tabular encoders could unlock better image-tabular fusion and seeks to benchmark them.

Method: Systematically evaluate several state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in the image–tabular multimodal setting. Tackle the challenge that In-Context Learning (ICL) models require labels to process instances by proposing solutions that align training and test embeddings across models, and run experiments across multiple tabular encoder families.

Result: Findings suggest encoder choice substantially impacts multimodal performance; state-of-the-art tabular encoders can serve as effective image–tabular encoders and can outperform plain MLP baselines when properly integrated, with viable strategies to mitigate ICL labeling constraints.

Conclusion: Encoder design is a critical factor in multimodal learning; tabular encoders are viable and valuable components for image–tabular fusion. Future work should refine cross-embedding techniques for ICL-based models and extend evaluation to broader tabular modalities and datasets.

Abstract: Multimodal learning usually requires a dedicated encoder per modality. When a tabular modality is involved, prior work has been mostly using a \emph{plain MLP} as the encoder. Yet if it were a strong encoder, the tabular domain would not be ``the last unconquered castle for deep learning''. This study evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in the image-tabular setting for the first time. An obstacle stands out. In-Context Learning models, among the best performing methods in the tabular domain, require labels to process instances, making it non-trivial to embed training and test instances the same way. We addressed this problem across multiple models of this family. With this study, we would like to highlight the importance of encoder factor in the multimodal learning.

</details>


### [87] [Scalable and Trustworthy Earth Observation Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07758)
*Syed Usama Imtiaz,Mitra Nasr Azadani,Nasrin Alamdari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey of remote-sensing foundation models (RSFMs): domain constraints drive design, no universal best, and evaluation is inconsistent; emphasizes physics-informed, modality-aware transfer and trustworthy representations; includes case studies on harmful algal bloom prediction and adaptive station selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EO data are large, multimodal, and label-sparse; measurement physics and operational decision constraints require domain-specific adaptation of foundation models.

Method: Comprehensive literature synthesis of RSFM design (pretraining objectives, architectures, adaptation) and trust requirements; two illustrative case studies: physics-informed spectral targeted masking for algal bloom prediction and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection.

Result: No geospatial foundation model is universally superior; inconsistent evaluation persists; current RSFM landscape is heterogeneous in objectives and modalities; case studies demonstrate practical domain-guided design and highlight the need for trustworthy evaluation.

Conclusion: Future RSFMs should be evaluated not only on benchmark accuracy but also on modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations to support trustworthy EO decisions; standardized but diverse benchmarks and transparent evaluation are needed.

Abstract: Foundation models (FMs) have transformed machine learning from isolated task-specific model development toward general-purpose models pretrained on broad data and adapted to multiple downstream tasks. Earth observation (EO) is an important domain for this paradigm because satellite and airborne archives are large, high-revisit, and increasingly multimodal, while reliable field labels are often sparse. Remote sensing foundation models (RSFMs) cannot be transferred reliably/optimally without domain-specific adaptation. This is because EO data are governed by measurement physics and operational decision constraints. This chapter reviews the design principles arising from these domain-specific constraints. It first defines the FMs paradigm in remote sensing (RS), then synthesizes the current model landscape, pretraining objectives, architecture designs, downstream adaptation and trustworthiness requirements. The chapter also incorporates recent benchmark evidence showing that no single geospatial foundation model is universally best and that inconsistent evaluation remains a major issue to fair comparison and reliable deployment. In addition, two brief environmental monitoring case studies; physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection to illustrate the FMs domain-guided principles in practice. This chapter posits that next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations for trustworthy EO decisions.

</details>


### [88] [Trustworthy Machine Learning through the Lens of Combinatorial Optimization: Survey and Research Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07762)
*Thibaut Vidal,Julien Ferry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey of combinatorial optimization (CO) methods for trustworthy ML, showing how CO can provide global guarantees, certificates, and clear trade-offs across training and post-training tasks (interpretability, explanations, robustness, fairness, privacy, and compression).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Move beyond empirical performance to principled, certifiable reasoning about ML models. Address transparency, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability by leveraging CO formulations and exact or certificate-based guarantees.

Method: Literature synthesis at the intersection of CO and trustworthy ML. Taxonomy of tasks (interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, privacy attacks/ protections) and corresponding CO formulations. Compare CO approaches with gradient-based methods, highlighting certificates and explicit trade-offs. Discuss scalability, solver progress, and hybrid algorithms.

Result: CO formulations enable global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit trade-offs in trustworthy ML tasks. Benefits include interpretable models, verifiable robustness and fairness analyses, and privacy-attack mitigations, alongside model compression. However scalability remains a challenge; advances in solvers and hybrids show promise for practical deployment.

Conclusion: CO is poised to play an increasing role in the design, training, auditing, and certification of trustworthy ML systems. Continued solver and hybrid-method research will be critical to address scalability and integration with ML pipelines.

Abstract: Modern machine learning (ML) increasingly relies on complex models whose behavior is difficult to characterize beyond empirical performance metrics. Across a wide range of tasks, including prediction, generation, and decision-making, models with similar empirical performance can exhibit markedly different properties in terms of their transparency, interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability. This survey highlights how optimization- and certification-oriented reasoning can provide a useful framework for reasoning about such differences, supporting tasks ranging from model training and selection to auditing and certification. We review and synthesize recent advances at the intersection of combinatorial optimization (CO) and trustworthy ML, covering both training and post-training tasks, including interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks and protections. Across these domains, CO formulations offer additional capabilities over purely heuristic approaches, e.g., gradient-based ones, notably global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit treatment of trade-offs. While scalability remains an important challenge, continued progress in solvers and hybrid algorithms suggests a growing role for CO in the design and deployment of trustworthy ML systems.

</details>


### [89] [Unlocking Temporal Generalization in Hamiltonian Video Dynamics Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07763)
*Eli Laird,Corey Clark*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hamiltonian Generative Networks (HGN) struggle to generalize to variable temporal resolutions in dissipative, externally forced environments due to latent growth and integrator truncation; the paper proposes fixes and strategies that yield stable predictions across a wide range of time steps and offers guidance for continuous-time video generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable true temporal generalization of continuous-time physical models to support hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and multi-timescale simulations/games; current HGNs assume fixed step size, hindering cross-timescale queries.

Method: Diagnose failure modes in non-conservative settings: (1) latent magnitude growth from an unconstrained action–force map, (2) global truncation error from an under-resolved integrator. Implement targeted fixes for each mechanism and validate across temporal resolutions beyond training; provide a detailed analysis and recommendations for continuous-time video generation.

Result: Demonstrate stable dynamics prediction at temporal resolutions far outside training; achieve improved temporal robustness; deliver concrete strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation.

Conclusion: Targeted interventions can restore temporal generalization in HGN, enabling reliable predictions across multiple time scales; guidelines and strategies are provided to extend continuous-time dynamics learning to video generation tasks.

Abstract: World models are typically trained to predict discrete-time physical dynamics with a fixed step size baked into the model weights, preventing prediction at variable temporal resolutions. This matters for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and scientific or game-engine applications that must query the same dynamics at multiple timescales. Hamiltonian Generative Networks (HGN) offer a principled path forward, grounding predictions in a continuous-time energy function that is, in principle, independent of the observation frame rate. In practice, however, their temporal generalization breaks down in non-conservative settings. We show that in externally forced, dissipative environments, HGN rollouts at step sizes beyond the training regime fail due to distinct failure modes, including latent magnitude growth driven by an unconstrained action-force map, and global truncation error accumulation from an under-resolved integrator. We identify a targeted fix for each mechanism and demonstrate stable dynamics prediction at temporal resolutions well outside the training distribution. In a detailed analysis, we recommend several strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation.

</details>


### [90] [Principled Analysis of Deep Reinforcement Learning Evaluation and Design Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07769)
*Ezgi Korkmaz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling laws in deep reinforcement learning reveal non-monotonic relationships between data scale, model capacity, and performance, challenging the validity of conclusions drawn under canonical evaluation paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To interrogate core assumptions about how data, compute, and model size interact to determine RL performance and to assess whether standard benchmarks reliably reflect progress.

Method: Theoretically derive scaling laws for RL and perform large-scale empirical experiments across diverse data regimes under canonical RL evaluation designs to examine how rankings vary with data, compute, and capacity.

Result: Asymptotic performance does not monotonically track data regimes; canonical-design-inspired research can lead to incorrect conclusions; results highlight the importance of scaling, capacity, and complexity in deep RL.

Conclusion: Calls for a reassessment of RL evaluation practices and scaling analyses, emphasizing non-monotone effects of data and capacity on performance and the need for robust paradigms that account for scaling, capacity, and complexity.

Abstract: Starting from the utilization of deep neural networks to approximate the state-action value function that led to winning one of the most challenging games, to algorithmic advancements that allowed solving problems without even explicitly stating the rules of the challenge at hand, reinforcement learning research has been the center of remarkable scientific progress for the past decade. In this paper, we focus on the key ingredients of this research progress and we analyze the canonical evaluation and design paradigms in reinforcement learning. We introduce the theoretical foundations of scaling laws in reinforcement learning and show that the asymptotic performance of reinforcement learning algorithms does not have a monotone relationship between performance rankings and data-regimes. We conduct large-scale experiments and our results demonstrate that a line of reinforcement learning research under the canonical design and evaluation paradigms resulted in incorrect conclusions. Our analysis and results provide a core analysis on scaling, capacity and complexity of deep reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [91] [Graph-Regularized Deep Learning for EEG-Based Emotion Recognition with Psychologically-Grounded Label Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07773)
*Dongyang Kuang,Zizheng Ma,Yushan Zhang,Xiaocong Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Graph-regularized learning for EEG emotion recognition that encodes emotional interdependencies via a graph; introduces Graph Label Smoothing, Graph Laplacian commuting distance, and Sliced Wasserstein Distance; tested across AudioTransformer, Conformer, DCGNN on SEED-IV/SEED-V with improvements in accuracy and reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emotions are psychologically interdependent; conventional DL treats classes as independent; leveraging structured emotion topology should improve accuracy and reduce implausible misclassifications.

Method: Construct emotion graph with nodes as emotion classes; edges reflect proximity per dimensional theories; apply three regularizations of increasing complexity to backbone models; evaluate across three architectures on SEED-IV/SEED-V.

Result: Consistent improvements; best +5.42 percentage points in accuracy; 39% fewer psychologically implausible misclassifications; architecture-agnostic benefits; code to be released.

Conclusion: Graph-regularized emotion topology can raise upper bound of EEG emotion recognition performance beyond standard approaches; framework is flexible across architectures.

Abstract: EEG-based emotion recognition is critical for mental health monitoring and affective brain-computer interfaces, yet existing deep learning approaches often treat emotion classes as isolated labels, ignoring their psychological interdependencies. We propose a graph-regularized learning framework that conceptualizes emotions as nodes in a graph where edges encode proximity based on dimensional emotion theories. We adapt three complementary regularization strategies--Graph Label Smoothing (intuitive soft labeling), Commuting distance on graph via Graph Laplacian (spectral graph theory), and Sliced Wasserstein Distance (optimal transport on graph)--ordered by increasing computational complexity. These strategies penalize model predictions that deviate from the established emotion topology. Our framework is evaluated across three representative backbone architectures: AudioTransformer (pure transformer), Conformer (CNN-transformer hybrid), and DCGNN (causal graph neural network), demonstrating architecture-agnostic benefits. Experiments on SEED-IV (4 classes) and SEED-V (5 classes) datasets show consistent improvements: best case up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications. Ultimately, our framework help raise the upper bound of performance achievable with standard approaches. Code will be released.

</details>


### [92] [A law of robustness for two-layer neural networks with arbitrary weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07778)
*Yitzchak Shmalo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-layer ReLU networks with unbounded weights require a Lipschitz constant at least on the order of sqrt(n/m) (up to logarithmic factors) to fit n noisy labels, extending prior bounds to unbounded weights. For general depth K, use bar m = (K-1)m+1; a realized-kink bound uses k(f)+1. The proof replaces parameter-space covering with a function-space covering and relies on a rigidity lemma that ties kink coefficients to the realized Lipschitz constant on B2 and S^{d-1} (d≥3). Rigidity fails at d=2. An explicit interpolant at width 2n matches the bound, indicating tightness on the overparameterized end.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve conjectures on the necessity of large Lipschitz constants for overparameterized two-layer nets with unbounded weights, in the presence of label noise, and extend the known results (Bubeck–Li–Nagaraj; Bubeck–Sellke) to unbounded-weight regimes and to generic piecewise-linear activations like ReLU.

Method: Replace parameter-space coverings with function-space coverings. Prove a rigidity lemma: on the unit ball and on S^{d-1} (d≥3), each canonical kink’s coefficient is bounded by the Lipschitz constant of the realized function, since kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel generically. Use this, together with high-probability concentration for data on S^{d-1} or N(0,I_d/d), to derive a lower bound on Lip(f) in terms of n, m, ε, d. Extend to a realized-kink-count version with k(f)≤n, replacing m̄ by k(f)+1. Show the bound holds up to log factors; establish rigidity failure in d=2; construct an O(1)-Lipschitz interpolant at width 2n matching the bound at the overparameterized endpoint.

Result: Proved the conjectured law up to a log factor for continuous piecewise-linear activations (including ReLU) in the unbounded two-layer setting. The Lipschitz lower bound scales as Lip(f) ≥ c ε sqrt( n / (m̄ log(C m̄ n d / ε)) ), with m̄ = (K−1)m+1 and high probability over the data. A realized-kink-count variant yields Lip(f) ≥ c ε sqrt( n / ( (k(f)+1) log(C (k(f)+1) n d / ε) ) ) for k(f) ≤ n. Rigidity is essential to the argument; d≥3 is required; rigidity fails for d=2, where a simple width-2n interpolant matches the bound up to constants.

Conclusion: The conjectured Lipschitz law extends to unbounded-weight two-layer networks for continuous piecewise-linear activations, via a function-space covering and a rigidity lemma. The approach highlights the role of kinks and their coefficients in bounding Lipschitz complexity, clarifies the necessity of d≥3 for rigidity, and demonstrates tightness through an explicit interpolant at the overparameterized end.

Abstract: Bubeck, Li and Nagaraj conjectured that, for generic data, any two-layer neural network with $m$ neurons that fits $n$ noisy labels must have Lipschitz constant at least of order $\sqrt{n/m}$, with no restriction on the size of the weights. Bubeck and Sellke proved a universal version of this law for Lipschitz-parameterized classes, but under a polynomial bound on the parameters; at depth three that boundedness hypothesis is genuinely necessary. The two-layer unbounded-weight case requires a different argument. We prove the conjectured law, up to one logarithmic factor, for every continuous piecewise-linear activation, in particular for ReLU networks. For data drawn uniformly from $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$, $d\ge3$, or from $N(0,I_d/d)$, labels in $[-1,1]$ with noise level $σ^2>0$, and any width-$m$ two-layer network with arbitrary real weights, biases and affine skip connection, fitting the data $\varepsilon$ below the noise floor forces $\mathrm{Lip}(f)\ge c\,\varepsilon\sqrt{n/(\bar m\log(C\bar m nd/\varepsilon))}$, $\bar m=(K-1)m+1$, with high probability. A realized-kink-count version holds on the same event: every realized two-layer piecewise-linear function with $k(f)\le n$ distinct kink hyperplanes obeys the bound with $\bar m$ replaced by $k(f)+1$, irrespective of how many redundant hidden units parameterize it. The proof replaces parameter-space covering, impossible for unbounded weights, by a function-space covering. The central deterministic ingredient is a rigidity lemma: on $B_2$, and on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ for $d\ge3$, the coefficient of each canonical kink is controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the realized function, because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel at generic points. Rigidity genuinely fails at $d=2$, and an explicit two-layer ReLU interpolant with $O(1)$ Lipschitz constant at width $2n$ matches the law at the overparameterized endpoint.

</details>


### [93] [Predicting Pseudo-nitzschia harmful algal blooms along the Portuguese Coast using satellite-derived predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07834)
*Ayman Bnoussaad,El Khalil Cherif,Ligia Pinto,Ramiro Neves,Alexandra D. Silva,Alexandre Bernardino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Satellite-driven spatio-temporal ML predicts harmful algal bloom (HAB) events from Pseudo-nitzschia along the Portuguese Atlantic coast with moderate accuracy; ensemble tree methods (Random Forest/Extra Trees) show best discrimination under strict cross-validation; findings support satellite-based early warning in upwelling regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HABs threaten shellfish industries and coastal ecosystems; limited in-situ data in Europe motivate a satellite-only forecasting approach that can operate under realistic constraints.

Method: Develop a spatio-temporal ML framework using 9 shellfish zones (L1-L9) but focused on L1-L2 as a hotspot. Compile 2013–2023 data (1,440 observations; >1,000 satellite predictors including SST, upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, plankton functional types). Partition sampling locations into ecologically meaningful sub-regions via river-aware spatial clustering. Use a stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation that withholds entire years and spatial clusters to prevent leakage. Train ensemble tree-based models (Random Forest, Extra Trees) and evaluate with environmental and biological predictors, followed by feature-importance analysis.

Result: HAB occurrence shows moderate predictability across models. Random Forest with environmental predictors achieves ~0.74±0.05; Extra Trees with biological variables reaches ~0.77±0.06. Seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate model decisions; biological indicators help refine bloom probability during favorable periods.

Conclusion: The framework provides operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning along eastern boundary upwelling coasts, demonstrating the value of strict spatio-temporal validation and the utility of combined environmental/biological satellite predictors.

Abstract: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. Here we develop and evaluate a spatio-temporal machine-learning framework to predict harmful algal bloom (HAB) occurrence using exclusively satellite-derived predictors under realistic forecasting constraints. We characterised environmental and biological variability across shellfish production zones (L1-L9) using 5,882 observations, providing system-wide context. Predictive models were developed for zones L1-L2, a hotspot for Pseudo-nitzschia and domoic acid events, using a decade-long dataset (2013-2023; 1,440 observations; more than 1,000 satellite-based predictors including sea surface temperature, an upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, and plankton functional types). Sampling locations were partitioned into ecologically meaningful sub-regions using a river-aware spatial clustering scheme. A stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation strategy that simultaneously withholds entire years and spatial clusters prevents leakage and closely mimics real-world forecasting conditions. HAB occurrence proved moderately predictable across model classes and feature configurations. Ensemble tree-based methods achieved the strongest discrimination: Random Forest reached 0.74 +/- 0.05 with environmental predictors; Extra Trees reached 0.77 +/- 0.06 with biological variables added. Feature-importance analyses revealed that seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate model decisions, while biological indicators refine bloom likelihood within physically favourable periods. The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts.

</details>


### [94] [Explaining Near-Zero Hessian Eigenvalues Through Approximate Symmetries in Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07845)
*Marcel Kühn,Bernd Rosenow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Hessian bulk of a neural network’s training loss arises from weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of the network’s continuous parametrization symmetries; most eigenvalues are near zero, with high-curvature directions orthogonal to the symmetry subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain the origin of the large number of near-zero Hessian eigenvalues and relate them to underlying symmetries in network parameterization.

Method: Theoretically derive exact zero modes in deep linear networks due to exact symmetries; treat ReLU as a perturbation to break symmetries weakly; decompose spectrum by eigenvectors; analyze two-layer student–teacher model and CIFAR-10 training; extend to convolutional architectures.

Result: High-curvature directions lie outside the symmetry subspace, while the Hessian bulk lies within it; the near-zero modes are pseudo-Goldstone modes from weakly broken continuous symmetries; explicit construction of zero modes in linear case; empirical validation on a CIFAR-10 task and a CNN-like example.

Conclusion: Link the Hessian's bulk to weakly broken symmetries; provides a mechanistic origin for near-zero eigenvalues across architectures and justifies the pervasiveness of flat directions.

Abstract: The Hessian of the training loss governs the local geometry of the loss landscape, yet despite existing explanations for its largest eigenvalues, the origin of the vast multitude of vanishingly small eigenvalues remains elusive. We argue that the bulk consists of the weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of the continuous symmetries of the network parametrization. In deep linear networks these symmetries are exact: they generate flat directions and hence exact zero modes, whose eigenvectors we construct explicitly. Introducing a ReLU nonlinearity as a perturbation, we show that it breaks these symmetries weakly and explicitly. Resolving the spectrum at the level of eigenvectors, we find that the high-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace, while the bulk lies almost entirely within it. We demonstrate the mechanism in a two-layer ReLU student--teacher model and in a network trained on CIFAR-10. A convolutional example demonstrates that the same diagnostic extends beyond fully connected layers. Together, these results link the Hessian bulk to weakly broken symmetries and clarify the origin of near-zero modes.

</details>


### [95] [When Does Continual Learning Require Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07847)
*Anne Harrington,Nayan Saxena,Michael Murphy,Anastasia Borovykh,Zeyu Yun,Sridhar Kamath,Ara Eindra Kyi,Trevor Darrell,Jitendra Malik,Yutong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reframe continual learning for LLMs along space (new domains) and time (data drift) with a single mechanism-agnostic evaluation protocol across prompt-based, supervised, RL, and context-compression methods; results show no universal solution—different environmental changes demand distinct update strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current focus on mitigating forgetting is incomplete. Continual learning should increase model competence as the world changes. By disentangling space and time, we study realistic conditions (new domains, data drift, and accumulated state) and enable fair, cross-method comparisons.

Method: Introduce a mechanism-agnostic, sequential evaluation protocol and recast standard LLM benchmarks as sequential tasks. Compare diverse methods: prompt-based (GEPA, ACE), supervised (SFT, SDFT), reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT) under changing environments.

Result: Prompt-based methods adapt quickly but forget future tasks. Distillation-based methods accumulate knowledge but struggle to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency but does not substantially enhance learning of new tasks. Online RL adapts well to knowledge updates but is sensitive to noisy rewards.

Conclusion: Continual learning is not a single capability; different environmental changes require different update behaviors. Some adaptation should occur inside model weights, others through external scaffolding. Understanding where each method succeeds or fails can guide the design of stronger continual learning systems.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly capable, the next question is how can we enable models to continually learn? Today, the field largely frames this as a problem of context management and mitigating forgetting. We argue this framing is incomplete: continual learning is fundamentally about increasing model competence as the world changes. We disentangle this change along two axes -- space, where the model encounters new domains, and time, where the underlying data drifts under a fixed task. This framing lets us study continual learning under realistic conditions: new domains arrive over time, facts drift past their training cutoff, and agentic interactions accumulate state across episodes. To evaluate methods under this setting, we recast widely used LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and introduce a single mechanism-agnostic protocol that compares prompt-based methods (GEPA, ACE), supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT). Prompt-based methods fit each new stage quickly but degrade on future tasks. Distillation-based methods accumulate knowledge stably but struggle to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency without substantially improving the ability to learn new tasks. Online reinforcement learning adapts most effectively to knowledge updates but remains sensitive to noisy reward signals. Overall, our results suggest that continual learning is not a single capability: different patterns of environmental change require fundamentally different update behaviors, determining when adaptation must be learned inside model weights and when it can be achieved through external scaffolding. We hope that understanding where each method succeeds and fails will guide the design of stronger continual learning systems.

</details>


### [96] [NFTR: From Provable Mode-Averaging to Geodesic Subgoal Selection in Offline Goal-Conditioned RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07855)
*Erdemt Bao,Xing Lei,Jun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NFTR replaces HIQL's Gaussian subgoal policy with a conditional normalizing flow and introduces a triangle-slack reweighting to correct subgoal selection, yielding robust, non-Gaussian, and stable subgoal planning with theoretical guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two coupled failure modes of Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning (HIQL): optimistic bias from value-function advantages and mode collapse to a single Gaussian subgoal when multi-modality is present. The goal is to enable stable, diverse, and reachable subgoals in offline goal-conditioned RL.

Method: 1) Replace Gaussian subgoal policies with conditional Normalizing Flows (NFs) for AWR-based subgoal selection. 2) Derive a closed-form mode-averaging result showing NFs are the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection. 3) Introduce triangle slack score, built on a triangle inequality, to multiplicatively correct AWR weights and downweight subgoals with detour costs exceeding average reachability. 4) Prove the triangle slack vanishes on deterministic MDP geodesics and upper-bounds composability violation under stochastic dynamics. 5) Use RWDR objective to preserve AWR’s population-level monotonic improvement and provide a three-term suboptimality decomposition. 6) Combine to achieve subgoal selection that avoids Gaussian collapse and is stable under stochastic dynamics.

Result: The NFTR framework yields subgoal selection that avoids the Gaussian collapse described in HIQL and remains stable in stochastic environments. The conditional NF policy provides richer, non-Gaussian subgoal distributions, the triangle slack reweights AWR steps to respect reachability, and the RWDR objective preserves monotonic improvement with a clear suboptimality decomposition. The approach has a GitHub implementation at https://github.com/erdemtbao/NFTR.

Conclusion: NFTR offers a theoretically grounded and practically implementable solution for robust subgoal planning in offline goal-conditioned RL, addressing HIQL’s failure modes through explicit generative policy modeling (NFs) and a triangle-inequality-based reweighting (triangle slack) that preserves performance guarantees under stochastic dynamics.

Abstract: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning (HIQL), an offline goal-conditioned RL method, selects subgoals by value-function advantages alone. This rule has two coupled failure modes. Optimistic bias treats lucky stochastic outcomes as skillful choices, and mode collapse reduces a multi-modal subgoal distribution to a single Gaussian mean that often falls in unreachable regions. We propose NFTR (Normalizing Flows subgoal policies with Triangle-slack Reweighting). A conditional Normalizing Flow replaces the Gaussian policy, and a closed-form mode-averaging result identifies NFs as the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection. A triangle slack score, built on the architectural triangle inequality without relying on distance accuracy, multiplicatively corrects the AWR weight to downweight subgoals whose detour cost exceeds average reachability. Triangle-slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and remains a conservative upper bound on composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The RWDR objective preserves AWR's population-level monotonic improvement and admits a three-term suboptimality decomposition. Together, these two ingredients yield subgoal selection that provably avoids the Gaussian collapse described above and remains stable under stochastic dynamics. GitHub page: https://github.com/erdemtbao/NFTR

</details>


### [97] [Physics-Informed Machine Learning Under Small-Data Constraints: Lessons from Abrasive Waterjet Milling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07863)
*Sarah Grewe,Jörg Frochte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In small, expensive machining datasets, separating physics-driven data cleaning from statistical data curation and treating curation as competing hypotheses improves model evaluation; Gaussian Process (GP) models consistently rank highly and uncertainty estimates are reasonably calibrated, while the impact of Bayesian hyperparameter tuning and residual physics integration varies by model class. Methodological emphasis: explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation, and careful physics integration are crucial for reliable model comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physically dominated machining processes produce small, costly, material-specific datasets. In this regime, how data are cleaned/curated and how physics is integrated into learning can dominate performance and uncertainty, often more than the choice of learning algorithm.

Method: 1) Distinguish physics-based data cleaning from statistical data curation and treat curation as competing modelling hypotheses. 2) Demonstrate instability of model rankings across evaluation designs (15-point hold-out vs 10-fold CV). 3) Explore a spectrum of physics integration levels and compare Gaussian Process (GP) and tree-based models, including residual learning on a compact physics baseline. 4) Apply Bayesian hyperparameter tuning, examining its effect on parameter-sensitive baselines (GB, SVR) and multi-stage hybrids. 5) Assess GP predictive uncertainty calibration.

Result: Key findings: (i) separating cleaning from curation yields a more explicit and testable modeling framework; (ii) single-split rankings are unstable and GP variants tend to top rankings under cross-validation (hold-out rankings unstable); (iii) residual learning with a compact physics baseline is advantageous for GP (lower variance, interpretable decomposition) but harms tree-based models; (iv) Bayesian tuning improves parameter-sensitive baselines (GB, SVR) yet degrades multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size; (v) GP uncertainty intervals are approximately calibrated (86% empirical coverage at 90% nominal); (vi) overall guidance: for small, expensive datasets, reliable model comparison benefits from explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation, and careful physics integration choices.

Conclusion: For small, expensive process datasets, explicit separation of data-cleaning vs data-curation, robust evaluation designs, and thoughtful physics integration are essential to achieve reliable model comparison and uncertainty quantification.

Abstract: In physically dominated machining processes, experimental datasets are small, expensive, and material-specific; in this regime, data curation, evaluation design, and the form of physics integration can matter as much as the learning algorithm. Using an abrasive waterjet milling dataset ($n{=}155$, Inconel\,718), we make three methodological contributions. First, we separate physics-based data \emph{cleaning} from statistical \emph{curation} and treat the latter as competing modelling hypotheses rather than silent preprocessing. Second, we find that model rankings from a 15-point hold-out set can be unstable: the single-split winner drops from rank~1 to rank~7 under 10-fold cross-validation, while Gaussian Process (GP) variants occupy the top ranks. Third, we study a spectrum of physics integration levels and find that residual learning on a compact physics baseline is competitive for GP, yielding lower variance and an interpretable decomposition, but degrades tree-based models. Bayesian hyper parameter tuning improves parameter-sensitive baselines such as gradient boosting and SVR, yet harms multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size. GP uncertainty intervals are approximately calibrated ($86\%$ empirical coverage at nominal $90\%$). The resulting picture is methodological: for small, expensive process datasets, our results suggest that, in this setting, reliable model comparison benefits from explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation, and careful choices about how physics enters the model.

</details>


### [98] [Optimal Learning Rate Scaling Depends on Data in Deep Scalar Linear Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07884)
*Yedi Zhang,Peter E. Latham,Leena Chennuru Vankadara,Andrew Saxe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gradient-descent dynamics in deep scalar linear nets require depth-aware learning-rate scaling; data-dependent scaling yields depth-agnostic dynamics and constant linear convergence across depths (including infinity); this phenomenon also appears in networks with residual connections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how depth and data influence gradient-descent dynamics in deep linear architectures and whether data-agnostic learning-rate rules transfer across depths.

Method: Derive exact time-course solutions for f(x)= (∏_{l=1}^L w_l) x under gradient descent for integer depths L; determine optimal depth-wise learning-rate scaling; analyze data dependence and the impact of residual connections.

Result: With data-dependent optimal scaling, the dynamics become data-independent and weakly depend on depth, yielding a constant linear convergence rate across all depths, including infinite depth; data-agnostic scaling rules fail to transfer between depths; similar data-dependent effects are observed with residual connections.

Conclusion: Data-dependent depth-wise learning-rate scaling can equalize training dynamics across depth in deep linear networks, offering guidance for initialization/stepsize strategies and insights potentially extendable to nonlinear/deeper architectures; residual variants share the same qualitative behavior.

Abstract: In this short note we consider the gradient descent dynamics of deep scalar linear networks, $f(x) = \prod_{l=1}^L w_l x$, which enjoy exact time-course solutions for any integer depth. We show that even in this minimal model, the optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling depends on data, whereas data-agnostic scaling rules fail to transfer across depths. Under the data-dependent optimal scaling, the learning dynamics is independent of data and weakly dependent on depth, resulting in a constant linear convergence rate across all depths including infinity. We further show similar data-dependent effects in deep scalar linear networks with residual connections.

</details>


### [99] [Distributed Sketching on Data Partitions for OLS Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07888)
*Luyuan Yang,Brayden Garner,Shayan Shafaei,Chao Lan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Distributed OLS via partitioned subset sketching and averaging; exact excess loss analyzed; comparable to whole-dataset sketching when subset covariance divergence is small.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce computation by sketching on partitioned subsets instead of the entire dataset while maintaining statistical performance.

Method: Partition data into subsets, sketch each subset, fit OLS on each sketch, then average the estimators; analyze exact excess loss under fixed design; compare to sketching on whole data; derive dependence on divergence among subset covariances.

Result: Exact characterization of the excess loss of the averaged estimator; under small divergence among subset covariances, the excess loss is comparable to the whole-dataset sketching loss.

Conclusion: Partitioned data sketching reduces computational cost and yields similar statistical performance to whole-data sketching when subset covariances are similar; extends prior results on sketching for OLS under fixed design.

Abstract: This paper studies distributed sketching for ordinary least squares (OLS) regression, an approach that distributes small sketches of a large data set over multiple machines to separately construct OLS estimators and average them. Unlike prior studies that consider sketching on the whole data set, we consider sketching on partitioned subsets to further reduce computational cost. Under the fixed design setting, we characterize the exact excess loss of the averaged OLS estimator. Results show that this loss is comparable to the established loss for sketching on the whole data set when the divergence among subset covariances is small.

</details>


### [100] [Multimodal Unlearning Across Vision, Language, Video, and Audio: Survey of Methods, Datasets, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07907)
*Nobin Sarwar,Shubhashis Roy Dipta,Zheyuan Liu,Vaidehi Patil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey that defines a system-oriented taxonomy for multimodal unlearning, outlining how to selectively erase cross-modal associations while preserving utility across vision, language, audio, and video, and highlighting open problems and practical deployment considerations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal foundation models can memorize or encode sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe associations from training data. Retraining or full forgetting is impractical due to distributed knowledge across representations; there is a need for selective, cross-modal unlearning.

Method: Systematic survey that builds a unified taxonomy across architectures and modalities (vision, language, audio, video), analyzes trade-offs (deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, robustness), surveys emerging applications, and identifies open problems; provides a curated repository for resources.

Result: Develops a unified, system-oriented view and taxonomy for multimodal unlearning; enables systematic comparison across architectures and modalities; highlights key trade-offs and practical considerations; aggregates and cites recent advances; releases a curated repository to support future work.

Conclusion: Multimodal unlearning is essential for compliant and safe deployment of multimodal models. The survey offers a structured framework, identifies gaps, and guides future research and deployment by detailing trade-offs and open problems, plus a resource hub.

Abstract: With the growing adoption of VLMs, DMs, LLMs, and AFMs, these multimodal foundation models can inadvertently encode sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe cross-modal associations that originate from their training data. Retraining after deletion requests or policy updates is often impractical, and targeted forgetting remains difficult because knowledge is distributed across shared representations. Multimodal unlearning addresses this challenge by enabling selective removal across modalities while retaining overall utility. This survey offers a unified, system-oriented view of multimodal unlearning across vision, language, audio, and video, grounded in recent advances, emerging applications, and open problems. Our taxonomy enables systematic comparison across model architectures and modalities, clarifying trade-offs among deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness. This survey highlights open problems and practical considerations to support future research and deployment of multimodal unlearning. We release a curated repository: https://smsnobin77.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Unlearning/

</details>


### [101] [Efficient Safety Alignment of Language Models via Latent Personality Traits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07918)
*Mohamed Amine Merzouk,Nolan Smyth,Damiano Fornasiere,Linh Le,David Williams-King,Adam Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LPA exploits latent personality-aligned representations to defend against jailbreaking with minimal data, yielding near-zero attack success on HarmBench without harming standard performance and with fast, lightweight training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety methods for LLMs are vulnerable to adversarial jailbreaks and require large harmful-prompt datasets for robustness. LPA aims to achieve robust defense using a small set of harm-agnostic personality statements, reducing data and compute while preserving utility.

Method: Apply adversarial training to latent representations aligned with psychometric personality constructs, using only 66 harm-agnostic statements instead of harmful prompts; train to stabilize representations, implicitly constraining subspace used by jailbreaks; evaluate on HarmBench against direct requests and five jailbreak methods; compare training efficiency to LAT.

Result: Near-zero jailbreak attack success on HarmBench across methods; no deterioration on standard benchmarks; full training completed in minutes on a single GPU; uses 75x fewer examples than LAT; ablations show robustness, efficiency, generalization.

Conclusion: Latent Personality Alignment offers an efficient, data-light alternative to conventional adversarial training for LLM safety by leveraging personality-anchored latent spaces to confine adversarial subspaces; further work needed on broader threat models and real-world deployment.

Abstract: Current safety methods for large language models are known to be vulnerable to adversarial attacks, motivating research into robust alternatives. Latent Adversarial Training (LAT) is among the most effective defenses, but can degrade utility and requires training on large datasets of harmful prompts. We introduce Latent Personality Alignment (LPA), which replaces explicit harm refusal with adversarial training on just 66 harm-agnostic statements drawn from psychometric personality literature. We hypothesize that personality-anchored representations share latent structure with harm avoidance, so adversarially stabilizing them implicitly constrains the subspace exploited by jailbreak attacks. LPA achieves near-zero attack success rates on HarmBench across direct requests and five jailbreak methods, despite never seeing harmful content during training and no loss of performance on standard benchmarks. Moreover, the training process is lightweight; the entire procedure completes in minutes on a single GPU and uses 75x fewer examples than standard LAT. Extensive ablations demonstrate the robustness, efficiency, and generalization of our method.

</details>


### [102] [path_boost: A Python Package for Interpretable Graph-Level Prediction using Path-Based Gradient Boosting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07935)
*Claudio Meggio,Johan Pensar,Riccardo De Bin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PathBoost is an interpretable graph-learning method that uses gradient boosting over predictive labeled paths; it discovers path features on the fly, producing an additive, interpretable model and achieving competitive performance with GNNs and graph kernels on molecular datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for interpretable, scalable graph-learning methods that identify informative substructures without the opacity of graph neural networks or the combinatorial cost of exhaustively enumerating paths.

Method: Iteratively discover and extend predictive labeled paths during boosting; construct an additive model over path-based features; supports regression and binary classification; compatible with scikit-learn; allows custom base learners and selectors; automatic starting node selection; parallel training across anchor nodes; built-in feature importance; parallelization.

Result: Demonstrated on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds; atoms as nodes, bonds as edges; benchmarked PathBoost against a standard graph neural network and a graph kernel on six molecular datasets; open-source implementation available on PyPI and GitHub.

Conclusion: PathBoost provides an interpretable, scalable alternative to black-box graph models by delivering an additive, path-based feature representation with competitive performance, easy integration into existing workflows, and accessible open-source tooling.

Abstract: We present path_boost, a Python package for interpretable supervised learning on graph-structured input data. The package implements PathBoost, a gradient boosting algorithm that automatically discovers predictive labeled paths within graphs during the learning process. Unlike graph neural networks, which are generally difficult to interpret, PathBoost produces an additive prediction model over path-based features that explicitly reveals which substructures drive predictions. To avoid an exhaustive enumeration of all possible paths, the algorithm iteratively selects and extends paths during learning based on their predictive power, using boosting to combine weak learners into a strong ensemble. The package supports both regression and binary classification. Key features include compatibility with scikit-learn workflows, support for custom base learners and selectors, automatic starting node selection, parallel training across anchor nodes, and built-in variable importance computation. We demonstrate PathBoost on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds, where atoms serve as nodes and bonds as edges, and further benchmark PathBoost against an established graph neural network and a graph kernel method across six molecular datasets. The package is available on PyPI and GitHub under an open-source license.

</details>


### [103] [Evaluating the Generalizability of Foundation Models for Extreme Environmental Events: Case Study of California Wildfire PM2.5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07951)
*Yongcan Huang,Li Jiang,Ze Yu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmark comparing six time-series foundation model (TSFM) configurations against trained baselines on 12-year hourly PM2.5 data around US wildfires. Foundation models underperform compared to LSTM/BiLSTM/Transformer baselines; zero-shot TSFMs yield modest gains over persistence, with Chronos-2 showing severe RMSE tail instability; LoRA fine-tuning improves stability and performance but cannot surpass trained recurrent baselines. Implications: larger pretrained models do not guarantee better environmental forecasting under extreme event scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess how state-of-the-art time-series foundation models perform under extreme out-of-distribution spikes in air-quality data and whether they surpass traditional trained baselines for wildfire-related PM2.5 forecasting.

Method: 12-year hourly PM2.5 dataset (2013–2025), 1,375 wildfire incidents, 79 California sites. Leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) to test generalization to unseen fires.
