Compare six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, Time-MoE; LoRA-finetuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully-trained LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer and persistence. Metrics: MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds for 6-, 12-, 24-hour horizons.

Result: A consistent hierarchy emerges: BiLSTM yields the best MAE (5.16 μg/m^3) and highest exceedance F1 across thresholds, including Hazardous (>225.5 μg/m^3) with F1 of 0.63 (vs. ≤0.54 for TSFMs). Zero-shot TSFMs surpass persistence only modestly. Chronos-2 in zero-shot mode shows strong RMSE tail instability (23.4 μg/m^3, negative R^2) due to sporadic large errors. LoRA fine-tuning markedly improves both TSFM families and largely fixes instability, yet no foundation model beats the trained recurrent baselines on any metric.

Conclusion: Larger pretrained TSFMs do not universally dominate environmental forecasting in extreme conditions. Training recurrent baselines remains superior for wildfire-related PM2.5 forecasting, though LoRA adaptation can help TSFMs stabilize and gain some performance. Provides practical guidance for deployment and cautions against assuming pretrained scale guarantees performance in out-of-distribution, high-impact scenarios.

Abstract: Wildfire smoke events produce extreme PM$_{2.5}$ concentrations that pose severe public health risks, yet forecasting rare, hazardous-level spikes remains a fundamental challenge. Time series foundation models (TSFMs), pretrained models offering zero-shot inference and efficient adaptation, perform strongly on general benchmarks, but their behavior under extreme out-of-distribution conditions is poorly understood. We present the first systematic benchmark comparing six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, and Time-MoE, plus LoRA fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully-trained baselines (LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer) and naive persistence on a 12-year (2013--2025) hourly PM$_{2.5}$ dataset covering 1,375 wildfire incidents across 79 California monitoring sites. A leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol evaluates generalization to unseen fires, using MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds across 6-, 12-, and 24-hour horizons. Results reveal a consistent hierarchy. The BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE ($5.16\,μg/m^3$) and the highest exceedance F1 at every threshold, including the Hazardous band ($>225.5\,μg/m^3$), reaching 0.63 versus at most 0.54 for any foundation model. Zero-shot TSFMs improve on persistence only modestly, and zero-shot Chronos-2 exhibits severe RMSE tail instability ($23.4\,μg/m^3$, negative $R^2$) from sporadic large errors. LoRA fine-tuning substantially improves both adapted families and largely repairs this instability, yet no foundation model surpasses the trained recurrent baselines on any metric. These findings challenge the assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate environmental forecasting and provide actionable deployment guidance for wildfire air quality prediction.

</details>


### [104] [Linear Attention Architectures: Mechanisms, Trade-offs, and Cross-Layer Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07953)
*Tommaso Cerruti,Tim Rieder,George Rowlands,Lingfeng Jin,Imanol Schlag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comparative study of softmax attention and four recurrent linear-attention architectures (DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, Gated DeltaNet-2) using a common recurrent-memory formalism, evaluated on 350M-parameter models trained to 15B tokens, with optimizer/hyperparameter analyses, stack variants, and cross-layer routing (CLVR); key findings: Kimi Delta Attention with Muon yields best final validation loss; pure Gated DeltaNet with AdamW offers highest training throughput; CLVR provides modest losses improvements; inference-speed benchmarks are not reported.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-attention has quadratic complexity; linear-attention / recurrent-memory approaches offer scalability. The goal is to compare expressive power, memory dynamics, training efficiency, and implementation complexity of several linear-attention variants within a unified framework and to explore lightweight cross-layer routing to enhance DeltaNet-style memories.

Method: Express DeltaNet-family mechanisms in a common recurrent-memory notation; run extensive experiments at 350M parameters, 15B tokens, comparing optimizers (e.g., AdamW, Muon), stacking strategies (hybrid vs pure), sequence-length runtimes, and later scaling to 1.3B and 3B parameters; perform downstream evaluations and introduce Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) to test cross-layer memory routing.

Result: Kimi Delta Attention with Muon achieves the lowest final validation loss in the 350M/15B sweep; a pure Gated DeltaNet stack trained with AdamW attains the highest normalized training throughput; hybrid stacks improve loss but reduce throughput; Muon generally lowers final validation loss versus AdamW in matched architectures; CLVR lowers final validation loss for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet; the natural DeltaNet formulation of forwarding a lower-layer delta-rule write error to the next layer’s value target does not improve outcomes, whereas routing into the aligned hidden stream with forwarded write values yields modest gains in matched runs.

Conclusion: DeltaNet-style memories can be tuned for performance via optimizer choice and cross-layer routing. The study highlights trade-offs between training throughput and final loss, and shows CLVR as a modest but beneficial technique. A remaining gap is the lack of empirical inference-speed benchmarks; results are focused on training dynamics and final validation loss at specific scales and settings.

Abstract: Self-attention lets each token retrieve information from the full context, but its quadratic cost in sequence length limits training and inference at long context. This paper presents a comparative study of softmax attention and four recent recurrent linear-attention architectures: DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, and Gated DeltaNet-2. We express these mechanisms in a common recurrent-memory notation, making explicit how they differ in expressivity, memory decay, erase and write control, training throughput, and implementation complexity. Our experiments center on 350M-parameter models trained for 15B tokens, and include optimizer and learning-rate comparisons, hybrid-versus-pure stack comparisons, sequence-length runtime measurements, larger DeltaNet runs at 1.3B and 3B parameters, and a small set of downstream evaluations. The reported speed results measure training throughput and iteration time; we do not provide an empirical inference-speed benchmark. Within the reported 350M-parameter, 15B-token sweep, Kimi Delta Attention with Muon reaches the lowest final validation loss, a pure Gated DeltaNet stack trained with AdamW has the highest normalized training throughput, hybrid stacks generally improve loss at a throughput cost, and Muon consistently lowers final validation loss relative to AdamW in the matched architecture settings we evaluate. We introduce and evaluate lightweight cross-layer routing mechanisms for DeltaNet-style memories. The most natural DeltaNet-inspired formulation, forwarding a lower layer's delta-rule write error into the next layer's value target, does not improve over matched baselines. Routing into the aligned hidden stream and forwarding the write value instead yields a modest improvement in the matched runs we report: Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) lowers final validation loss for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.

</details>


### [105] [KronQ: LLM Quantization via Kronecker-Factored Hessian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07964)
*Donghyun Lee,Yuhang Li,Ruokai Yin,Priyadarshini Panda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KronQ uses gradient covariance via a Kronecker-factored Hessian to enhance post-training quantization (PTQ), introducing bidirectional incoherence processing and a gradient/activation-based sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision. It significantly outperforms GPTQ/GPTAQ on 2-bit weight-only quantization for LLaMA-3-70B (WikiText-2 perplexity 7.93 vs degenerative results).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current second-order PTQ methods rely solely on input activation statistics and assume equal contribution of all output channels to the layer-wise objective, potentially misallocating precision and harming accuracy.

Method: Apply Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation to quantization loss, making it depend on both activation and gradient covariances. Introduce bidirectional incoherence processing by extending input-side random rotation to the output dimension using gradient covariance to reduce weight magnitude variance across input and output. Derive a new inter-layer mixed-precision sensitivity metric from traces of gradient and activation Hessians to guide precision allocation.

Result: Demonstrates strong performance in a 2-bit weight-only setting on LLaMA-3-70B, achieving 7.93 perplexity on WikiText-2, whereas GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or yield degenerate quantizations (>2000 perplexity).

Conclusion: Incorporating gradient information and a Kronecker-factored Hessian framework into PTQ yields better low-bit quantization performance and a new path for reliable inter-layer precision allocation; further validation across more models and tasks is needed.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a widely adopted technique for compressing large language models (LLMs) without retraining. Existing second-order PTQ methods, including GPTQ, construct quantization objectives exclusively from input activation statistics, effectively assuming that all output channels contribute equally to the layer-wise reconstruction objective. We propose KronQ, a PTQ framework that challenges this assumption by introducing the gradient covariance into the quantization pipeline. Under the Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, the quantization loss depends jointly on both the activation and gradient covariances, and KronQ exploits this at two complementary levels. (1) KronQ introduces bidirectional incoherence processing, extending the existing input-side random rotation to the output dimension using the gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output dimensions. (2) KronQ derives a new sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision allocation, driven by the gradient and activation Hessian traces. Notably, in the case of 2-bit weight-only quantization on LLaMA-3-70B, while GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate quantizations (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity.

</details>


### [106] [Latent Memory Palace: Reasoning for Control as Autoregressive Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08724)
*Chuning Zhu,Eva Xu,Jose Barreiros,Krishnan Srinivasan,Paarth Shah,Abhishek Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Latent Memory Palace (LMP) for control policies, enabling reasoning in an autoregressive latent space via variational inference; introduces LMP-π (policy) and LMP-tok (tokenizer) with improved performance and interpretable, adaptive test-time compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between language-model-like reasoning and fine-grained control, enabling adaptive, interpretable long-horizon planning in continuous control.

Method: Formulate reasoning as variational inference over an autoregressive latent distribution, deriving a tractable latent-space RL objective to optimize the variational lower bound. Implement LMP-π as the policy and LMP-tok as a variable-length action tokenizer, enabling iterative, memory-palace–like retrieval during decision making.

Result: Empirical results show strong performance in both simulation and real-world domains; the approach yields interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. The framework also yields a variable-length action tokenizer (LMP-tok) that improves downstream autoregressive policies.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning for control via variational inference is a viable paradigm. LMP provides a memory-augmented latent reasoning framework that improves performance and interpretability for continuous-control policies and enhances autoregressive policies via a tokenizer.

Abstract: Human decision-making is highly flexible -- some actions are taken immediately; others require longer deliberation. Language models have exhibited a similar capacity for adaptive "reasoning." However, transferring this capability to continuous control policies has been challenging, as directly reasoning in language space may lack the granularity for spatial understanding and precise motions. In this work, we show that reasoning for control policies can emerge by organizing information in an autoregressive latent space reminiscent of a memory palace, where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. Our method, Latent Memory Palace (LMP), formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution. We derive a latent-space reinforcement learning technique to tractably optimize its variational lower bound. The resulting policy, LMP-$π$, achieves strong empirical performance in simulation and real-world domains while exhibiting interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. We further show that the same framework yields a variable-length action tokenizer, LMP-$\texttt{tok}$, which significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies. Together, these results present a new perspective on latent reasoning for control through the lens of variational inference.

</details>


### [107] [Provably Optimal Learning Algorithms for Assistance Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08012)
*Nivasini Ananthakrishnan,Mark Bedaywi,Michael I. Jordan,Stuart Russell,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces an online variant of assistance games with a human (informed) and assistant (uninformed) interacting over time. Defines assistance regret and provides provably efficient, decentralized algorithms achieving a (1-1/e) approx of the optimal joint policy with regret ~T^{3/4}, poly-time in action/state spaces; shows computational hardness for beating (1-1/e); a pseudo-decentralized variant with shared randomness attains ~T^{1/2} regret, near-optimal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable provably efficient learning in repeated assistance settings where the assistant only observes human actions while the human observes latent state, addressing a gap in algorithmic guarantees for cooperative human–machine interaction.

Method: Formulates assistance regret and develops decentralized learning algorithms for both players. Proves a (1-1/e)-approximate regret bound of ~O(T^{3/4}) with polynomial runtime, valid for any no-regret algorithm used by the assistant. Establishes computational hardness for achieving better than (1-1/e). Introduces a pseudo-decentralized variant using a shared random string to obtain ~O(T^{1/2}) regret (up to log factors).

Result: Decentralized algorithms yield a (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret with runtime polynomial in action/state space, achieving ~T^{3/4} regret. A hardness result rules out sub-(1-1/e) guarantees in general. The shared-randomness variant improves to ~T^{1/2} regret, matching lower bounds up to logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: The work delivers the first provably efficient learning procedures for repeated assistance games, with near-optimal regret rates and a clear separation between fully decentralized and pseudo-decentralized (shared randomness) settings. It also clarifies the computational limits for improving the approximation factor.

Abstract: This paper studies an online variant of the assistance games framework, where an informed agent and an uninformed agent repeatedly interact over $T$ timesteps to optimize a common reward function. While the informed agent (the human) observes a latent state of the world, the uninformed agent (the assistant) observes only the human's actions. We provide the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games. We introduce the notion of assistance regret: the gap between the cumulative utility of interactions and that of the optimal joint policies in hindsight, which map latent states to action pairs. We present decentralized algorithms for both the human and the assistant that achieve a $(1-1/e)$-approximate assistance regret rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{3/4})$, with runtime polynomial in the size of the action and state spaces. These algorithms are general; in particular, they accommodate any no-regret algorithm for the assistant. We prove that achieving a regret approximation factor better than $(1-1/e)$ is computationally intractable. Furthermore, we demonstrate how these generic no-regret algorithms can be tailored to a pseudo-decentralized setting -- using a shared random string -- to achieve a rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/2})$, optimal up to logarithmic factors.

</details>


### [108] [Collate: Collaborative Neural Network Learning for Latency-Critical Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08013)
*Shuo Huai,Di Liu,Hao Kong,Xiangzhong Luo,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Collate enables federated learning with heterogeneous edge models under latency constraints by dynamically zeroizing/recovering architectures and a proto-corrected aggregation; achieves accuracy gains with little overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge FL suffers from device heterogeneity causing inference latency variance; existing FL work focuses on training efficiency and often neglects inference latency under real-time constraints.

Method: Dynamic zeroizing-recovering of local model architectures to fit each device's latency constraint; proto-corrected federated aggregation to integrate heterogeneous local models into a unified global model while honoring multiple latency targets; uses a single training process to satisfy diverse systems.

Result: Under latency constraints, extended models achieve ~1.96% average accuracy improvement; shrunk models achieve ~3.09% average accuracy improvement; minimal additional training overhead; outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in reported experiments.

Conclusion: Collate provides a practical framework for latency-aware heterogeneous FL, enabling higher-accuracy inference across diverse edge devices with one training run; code and data are to be released.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) empowers multiple clients to collaboratively learn a model, enlarging the training data of each client for high accuracy while protecting data privacy. However, when deploying FL in real-time edge systems, the heterogeneity of devices among systems has a severe impact on the performance of the inferred model. Existing optimizations on FL focus on improving the training efficiency but fail to speed up inference, especially when there is a latency constraint. In this work, we propose Collate, a novel training framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet the latency constraints of multiple edge systems simultaneously. We design a dynamic zeroizing-recovering method to adjust each local model architecture for high accuracy under its latency constraint. A proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme is also introduced to aggregate all heterogeneous local models, satisfying the latency constraint of different systems with only one training process and maintaining high accuracy. Extensive experiments indicate that, compared to state-of-the-art methods and under a latency constraint, our extended models can improve the accuracy by 1.96% on average, and our shrunk models can also obtain a 3.09% accuracy improvement on average, with almost no extra training overhead. The related codes and data will be available at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/Collate

</details>


### [109] [PGD-NO: A Neural Operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition for 3D Million-scale Physics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08025)
*Weiheng Zhong,Jing Bi,Victor Oancea,Hadi Meidani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PGD-NO introduces a Precomputed Geometry Decomposition neural operator that decouples geometry encoding from solution querying via precomputed geometry tokens, enabling linear memory scaling and high-fidelity PDE solving on meshes over 10 million nodes with competitive accuracy and interpretable attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome memory bottlenecks and single-node limitations of neural PDE solvers, enabling large-scale, high-resolution simulations on standard hardware through decoupled geometry encoding.

Method: Iterative geometry decomposition extracts geometry tokens as a deterministic precomputation step, producing geometry encodings that feed a neural operator; the model separates feature extraction from solution querying and leverages attention for interpretability, achieving linear memory scaling.

Result: Demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy on diverse industrial benchmarks and scalable memory usage up to meshes >10 million nodes, with intrinsic interpretability from attention mechanisms.

Conclusion: PGD-NO mitigates traditional mesh-size constraints, offering an efficient, scalable, and interpretable neural PDE solver suitable for large-scale industrial design tasks.

Abstract: While neural PDE solvers have demonstrated significant potential for accelerating engineering simulations, existing architectures remain constrained by high memory consumption and the single node bottleneck, where the maximum processable mesh resolution is strictly limited by the VRAM of a single compute unit. To address these challenges, we propose PGD-NO, a neural operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition, that relocates the computational overhead of geometric encoding to a deterministic pre-computation phase. By utilizing an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, our model decouples feature extraction from solution querying. This architecture enables linear memory scalability, allowing high fidelity learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes, a scale where existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion. PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks and provides intrinsic interpretability through attention mechanisms. By effectively overcoming traditional mesh-size constraints, PGD-NO offers a robust and efficient solution for the next generation of large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

</details>


### [110] [Rethinking Small VLM Quantization: From Component-Wise Analysis to Hardware-Aware Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08029)
*Hyeju Shin,Chorwon Kim,Ryangsoo Kim,Hark Yoo,Jaein Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation of quantization for on-device vision-language models (five hypotheses, six configurations) shows architecture-driven quantization sensitivity (MoE vs dense), deployment-specific INT8 latency for SigLIP, INT4 trade-offs (VRAM reduction but slower generation), largely additive quantization errors with a modality-alignment caveat, and platform-dependent energy efficiency due to memory bandwidth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable efficient deployment of compact vision-language models on edge devices by understanding component-wise quantization effects.

Method: Framework evaluating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations; decouples vision encoder, projector, and LLM backbone; experiments on Jetson Orin NX and AGX.

Result: Key findings: (1) Structural paradigm governs INT4 sensitivity; MoE backbones mitigate INT4 noise while dense backbones degrade; (2) SigLIP encoders incur disproportionate INT8 latency due to deployment-specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction; (3) INT4 quantization of LLMs reduces VRAM but slows token generation due to dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive, except along the modality-alignment path, which is architecture-dependent; (5) Intelligence-per-joule varies across platforms due to memory bandwidth constraints.

Conclusion: Quantization behavior is architecture- and platform-dependent; MoE backbones can mitigate INT4 noise; design decisions must balance compute, memory, and energy for target hardware; encoder-hardware interactions and modality-alignment pathways are critical design considerations for reliable on-device VLM deployment.

Abstract: The emergence of vision language models with fewer than 3 billion parameters has accelerated the implementation of on-device multimodal intelligence. However, a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization remains a bottleneck for optimal deployment. This paper presents a systematic evaluation framework for empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on the Jetson Orin NX and AGX. By separating the vision encoder, projector, and large language model backbone yields the following results: (1) Quantization sensitivity is governed by the structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) rather than scale alone, with MoE backbones mitigating INT4 noise where dense backbones degrade; (2) SigLIP encoders incur disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere--a deployment-specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction, not a SigLIP flaw; (3) Although INT4 quantization of LLMs greatly reduces VRAM consumption, it also causes slower token generation due to dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive, except along the modality-alignment path, which is architecture-dependent; (5) The intelligence-per-joule profile varies significantly across platforms owing to memory bandwidth constraints.

</details>


### [111] [What to Keep, What to Forget: A Rate--Distortion View of Memory Compaction in LLMs and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08032)
*Ashwin Gerard Colaco,Nada Lahjouji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unifies memory/compaction across LLM systems under a rate–distortion framework, proposes a seven-axis taxonomy, a cross-layer benchmark, and design principles to enable transfer of storage-management ideas across layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory and context in LLM agents consume increasing compute/memory; existing approaches are fragmented across layers and lack a unified objective or evaluation, hindering cross-pollination and principled budgeting.

Method: Introduce a rate–distortion objective with a layer-agnostic lower bound; develop a seven-axis taxonomy to classify cross-layer methods; demonstrate cross-layer transfers of memory-management techniques; propose a benchmark and a reference experiment; derive compaction-aware design principles.

Result: Shows that at every layer the retention signal is driven by attention magnitude or recency; common failure mode is discarding information before the query is known with no undo; notes under-measurement of repeated compaction and lack of a single budget axis across layers; provides a benchmark proposal and design principles, and maps open problems across the stack.

Conclusion: Provides a unified cross-layer theory and toolkit for memory compaction in LLM systems, enabling cross-layer transfer of techniques, highlighting measurement gaps, and outlining open problems and benchmarks to standardize evaluation.

Abstract: Large language models, and the agents built on them, spend an ever-growing share of their compute and memory on remembering: caching attention keys and values, carrying long prompts, maintaining recurrent state, and storing what happened in previous turns and sessions. Because none of this memory is free, four largely separate research communities have each learned to compact it. They evict or quantize the KV cache, prune or distill prompts, bound architectural state, and consolidate agent memory. We argue that these are instances of one problem: a rate--distortion decision about what context-derived information to retain versus discard, at what fidelity, under a resource budget, so as to preserve downstream task utility. We make this lens precise with a single compaction objective and a layer-agnostic lower bound, use it to build a seven-axis taxonomy that classifies methods from across the stack uniformly, and use it to transfer mechanisms between layers that have never been connected, from serving-stack KV management to agent long-term memory. Two patterns hold across the survey. At every layer the signal that decides what to keep is attention magnitude or recency, and it fails in the same way everywhere, by discarding, before the query is known and with no way to undo it, information the query later needs. And while compression is measured carefully on single-turn long context, the repeated compaction that agents actually perform is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds one budget axis across all the layers at once. We turn both observations into a benchmark proposal, a small reference experiment, and a set of compaction-aware design principles, and we map the open problems.

</details>


### [112] [An exact information theory of generalization phase transitions in Bayesian diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08041)
*Henry Hunt,Mason Kamb,Surya Ganguli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models that reverse diffusion by Bayesianly inferring the past training sample from restricted, noisy observations; reveals an information-theoretic transition between memorization and generalization governed by mutual information vs log N; provides empirical validation and links to early-training diffusion behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain how diffusion models can learn complex, high-dimensional distributions with finite data and why information restriction enables generalization over memorization. Provide an analytically tractable framework to study the role of information restriction in generative modeling.

Method: Define BIRD models where each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. Time-reverse diffusion by inferring the producing training sample via Bayesian posterior. Connect to existing spatially local restriction models. Derive phase boundary under minimal assumptions in the joint space of training data amount, reverse-time step, and information restriction. Compare to trained diffusion models across architectures (UNets, DiTs).

Result: Spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models early in training. Identified an information-theoretic phase boundary: memorization when mutual information between restricted observations and training data exceeds log number of training points; generalization otherwise. Experiments across datasets validate the predicted transition location. Generation operates near the memorization boundary, with both BIRD and early-training models adjusting information restriction over time to track the phase boundary.

Conclusion: Information restriction is fundamental to overcoming the curse of dimensionality in generative AI. The BIRD framework clarifies why diffusion models can learn from finite data and how progress during training is tied to staying near the memorization-generalization boundary.

Abstract: How diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality to learn complex distributions over high dimensional spaces from a finite training set, instead of memorizing it, remains a fundamental mystery. To address this, we introduce analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, in which each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. A BIRD model time-reverses diffusion by inferring which past training sample produced its current restricted observation using the Bayesian posterior. This model class generalizes existing analytical diffusion models that use spatially local information restriction. We show that spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models \textit{early in training}, across different architectures such as UNets and DiTs. Under minimal assumptions on the data distribution, we identify an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization in the joint space of amount of training data, time in the reverse generative process, and amount of information restriction: a BIRD model memorizes when the mutual information between its restricted noisy observations and the training data exceeds the log number of training points, and it generalizes otherwise. Experiments across a range of datasets confirm our theoretically predicted location for the transition. We find that generation proceeds near the edge of memorization: both spatially local BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track the memorization-generalization phase boundary by increasingly restricting information over time. Overall, our results reveal a fundamental role for information restriction in generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

</details>


### [113] [Who Analyses the Analyser? Self-Validating LLM Hazard Analysis with Constitutional Meta-STPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08054)
*Samuel Tetteh,Udip Shrestha,Joshua R. Waite,Cody Fleming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Constitutional Meta-STPA to govern LLM-assisted safety analysis by deriving a governance constitution from the tool’s own safety-analytic chain; demonstrates recoverability of governance principles using ensemble LLMs, indicating meta-governance is robust to model choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address a key blind spot: the analyser (the LLM-based tool) is safety-relevant and can hallucinate, lack audit trails, and emit unverifiable constraints. The work seeks to close the loop by analysing the analyser with STPA and formalizing governance.

Method: Builds a closed-loop meta-STPA around LLM-assisted safety tools; derives a governance constitution from the loss→hazard→UCA→constraint cascade; formalizes the object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over 29 principles; outputs 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles, each tied to enforcement points. Empirically evaluates via frontier ensembles (e.g., claude-opus-4.8 + claude-sonnet-4) and a second independently authored tool to test recoverability of the governance principles.

Result: Published constitution comprising 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles; 29-principle coverage framework with a soundness lemma. Empirical findings: the frontier ensemble recovers 18/21 canonical and all 8/8 governance principles from the tool’s design; a weaker pair recovers 12/21 and 3/8; the same 8/8 governance principles also emerge from a second independently authored tool.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that meta-governance can be extracted from the design of AI-assisted safety tools and recovered across different models/tools, indicating the constitution is not model-limited. Provides a formal, auditable governance framework for LLM-assisted safety analysis and a replicable methodology for evaluating analyser governance.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted to draft the artifacts of safety analysis such as, losses, hazards, Unsafe Control Actions (UCAs), and safety constraints, inside rigorous processes such as Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA). Yet a blind spot runs through this fast-growing literature: every system gets analysed except the LLM-assisted tool doing the analysing, which is itself a safety-relevant system that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and leave no audit trail from prompt to artifact. We take seriously the question the field has skipped -- {who analyses the analyser?} and answer it by turning STPA on the tool itself. We present \{Constitutional Meta-STPA}, an LLM-assisted STPA tool built around a closed loop: the tool runs a {meta-STPA} of the class of AI-assisted safety tools and {derives} rather than asserts, its governance constitution from the resulting loss$\to$hazard$\to$UCA$\to$constraint chain, yielding a published constitution of $21$ Tool Principles and $8$ Meta-Safety Principles, each bound to a code enforcement point. We formalise the measured object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set $P$ ($|P|{=}29$) with a soundness lemma that isolates coverage from model and scanner, and report four findings. {(i)~Self-derivation:} a frontier ensemble ({claude-opus-4.8}${+}${claude-sonnet-4}) recovers $18/21$ canonical and all $8/8$ governance principles from the tool's own design, while a weaker pair recovers $12/21$ and $3/8$, so the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited, and the same $8/8$ re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

</details>


### [114] [Reinforcing the Generation Order of Multimodal Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08056)
*Yidong Ouyang,Zhe Wang,Sourav Bhabesh,Dmitriy Bespalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learning-based generation-order control for Diffusion Language Models via Group Relative Policy Optimization improves text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding; exceeds logits-based ordering in complex tasks with gains on GenEval and VLMEvalKit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Logs-only ordering is insufficient for complex multimodal generation; a learnable policy is needed to optimize generation sequence to capture spatial relationships and cross-modal reasoning.

Method: Introduce a learnable control module trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to select generation order in diffusion language models for text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding; evaluate on GenEval and VLMEvalKit.

Result: The approach yields improvements in both domains: enhanced text-to-image alignment with better capture of fine-grained spatial relations in generated images and improved multimodal reasoning/comprehension; reported relative gains of 4.08% on GenEval and 4.85% on VLMEvalKit.

Conclusion: A GRPO-trained control block for generation order is effective across text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding, indicating policy-guided token generation order as a valuable direction for improving DLM performance.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) have recently achieved substantial progress in natural language generation tasks. Recent research demonstrates that adaptive token generation ordering can significantly improve performance in mathematical reasoning and code synthesis applications. In this work, we investigate the optimization of generation order for both text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. We first establish that, unlike structured problems in language generation such as Sudoku puzzles, model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding. To address this challenge, we introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine the generation order. Our results demonstrate that learning this control block substantially improves both text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding in DLMs. In particular, it enhances the model's ability to capture fine-grained spatial relationships in generated images while also strengthening performance on multimodal reasoning and comprehension tasks. We evaluate our framework on GenEval, an object-focused benchmark for text-to-image alignment, where it achieves 4.08% relative improvements. In addition, experiments on VLMEvalKit confirm 4.85% relative improvements in multimodal understanding, highlighting the broad effectiveness of our approach.

</details>


### [115] [Towards Efficient Large Language Model Serving: A Survey on System-Aware KV Cache Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08057)
*Jiantong Jiang,Peiyu Yang,Rui Zhang,Feng Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey of system-aware KV cache infrastructure for LLM serving (sKis), organizing KV cache research along temporal, spatial, and structural dimensions, and analyzing cross-behavior design affinities to enable low-latency, high-throughput LLM inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV caches are memory-intensive and costly but critical for fast autoregressive decoding in LLM serving; a structured, system-centric view is needed to guide design across platforms.

Method: Literature synthesis that categorizes existing KV-cache efforts into three orthogonal dimensions—execution/scheduling (temporal), placement/migration (spatial), and representation/retention (structural)—and analyzes interactions between behaviors and objectives.

Result: A cohesive framework and terminology for KV-cache design in serving systems, highlighting cross-domain affinities and outlining opportunities for future work to advance KV-cache architectures within modern LLM serving infrastructures.

Conclusion: The paper/systematizes a rapidly evolving area and provides a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs (sKis) in contemporary LLM serving infrastructure.

Abstract: Despite the rapid advancements of large language models (LLMs), LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly. The key-value (KV) cache, which stores KV tensors during autoregressive decoding, is crucial for enabling low-latency, high-throughput LLM inference serving. In this survey, we focus on system-aware KV infrastructure for serving LLMs (abbreviated as sKis). We revisit recent work from a system behavior perspective, organizing existing efforts into three dimensions: execution and scheduling (temporal), placement and migration (spatial), and representation and retention (structural). Furthermore, we analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links, highlighting future opportunities. Our work systematizes a rapidly evolving area, providing a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure.

</details>


### [116] [When Thinking Hurts: Epistemic Signals in the Reasoning Chains of Visual Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08059)
*Mayank Singal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Three-family empirical characterization of answer entropy vs chain entropy in thinking-mode visual-language models (VLMs) under POPE adversarial samples. Findings show three qualitative patterns across models (complete collapse, no collapse, selective thinking). Chain entropy often outperforms answer entropy on samples with chains, with variable margins across models. A VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy as a stronger predictor, especially for free-form answers; HallusionBench shows moderate signal. Structured abstention affects 12-22% of queries and a practical abstention gate substantially improves accuracy (71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage) without extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty quantification for thinking-mode VLMs; compare the predictive power of chain-of-thought (chain) entropy versus final answer entropy; evaluate abstention mechanisms to improve decision quality while controlling cost, across multiple models and tasks.

Method: Empirical study across three thinking-mode VLMs (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking, GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking, InternVL3-8B) trained for thinking-mode behavior. Use identical POPE adversarial samples to compute answer entropy and chain entropy. Characterize patterns of entropy behavior (collapse, no collapse, selective thinking). Compare chain vs answer entropy on chain-generated subsets. Extend evaluation with a 300-sample VQAv2 pilot and HallusionBench, and analyze structured abstention and an abstention gate with 62.7% coverage.

Result: Three qualitative patterns: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking shows complete collapse (answer entropy AUROC 0.492); GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking shows no collapse (0.716); InternVL3-8B shows selective thinking (chains on ~50% of samples; answer entropy 0.675 in full chains, 0.602 for thinking-only). Across models, chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on chain-generated subsets (values: 0.647 vs 0.492 for Qwen; 0.759 vs 0.716 for GLM; 0.608 vs 0.602 for InternVL, respectively). VQAv2 pilot (n=300) yields chain entropy 0.680 vs answer entropy 0.595 (largest gap for free-form answers: 0.733 vs 0.467). HallusionBench tasks show moderate signals (~0.64) for Qwen models. Structured abstention affects 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries; an abstention gate raises accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no extra inference cost.

Conclusion: Chain entropy is a more reliable uncertainty signal than final-answer entropy for thinking-mode VLMs, particularly when chain-of-thought is generated. The predictive advantage of chain entropy is robust across tasks and models, though effectiveness varies by model. Abstention mechanisms offer substantial accuracy gains with modest coverage and little overhead, suggesting practical deployments should consider chain-focused uncertainty and selective abstention.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification for visual language models (VLMs) conventionally targets the answer token distribution. We provide the first three-family empirical characterisation of answer entropy behaviour in thinking-mode VLMs. Running four models on identical POPE adversarial samples, we find three qualitatively distinct patterns: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking shows complete collapse (ans H AUROC = 0.492); GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking shows no collapse (0.716); and InternVL3-8B shows selective thinking (chains on only 50% of samples, ans H = 0.675 full / 0.602 thinking-only). Across all three thinking-mode models, thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on the subset where chains are generated (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs. 0.492, 0.716, 0.602 respectively), suggesting chain signals are the more reliable predictor whenever chains are present. This holds strongly for Qwen and GLM, but with only marginal and statistically unreliable advantage for InternVL3 (n_FP = 17). A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy (0.680) outperforms answer entropy (0.595) on VQAv2 questions, with the gap largest for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). On harder reasoning tasks (HallusionBench) both Qwen models show moderate signal (approx. 0.64), consistent with incomplete pre-commitment on difficult questions. We additionally document structured abstention affecting 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries, and a practical abstention gate raising accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no additional inference cost.

</details>


### [117] [Cross-Modal Generative Framework for Signal Translation from Fetal-Maternal Electrocardiograms to Fetal Doppler Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08073)
*Tongli Su,Alireza Rafiei,Marly van Assen,Reza Sameni,Gari D. Clifford,Faezeh Marzbanrad,Nasim Katebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cross-modal generative model synthesizes fetal Doppler envelopes from dual-lead ECG (fetal and maternal) to quantify recoverable vs. unrecoverable Doppler components and assess maternal-fetal coupling; cross-modal attention improves physiological signal reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the relative electrical (fECG) and mechanical (Doppler) contributions to fetal circulation and quantify recoverable versus residual Doppler components; leverage maternal-fetal cardiovascular coupling to inform clinical decisions.

Method: A cross-modal generative framework using dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention (for maternal ECG input) and self-attention (for long-range temporal dependencies). Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies. Objective: synthesize Doppler envelopes; losses include PSD mean squared error. Evaluated against baselines including a two-channel baseline and naive dual-channel concatenation.

Result: PSD MSE: 49.9 ± 15.8 dB^2, ~51% lower than two-channel baseline. Heart-rate error: 4.71 ± 0.77 bpm, ~1.5% better than baseline. Cross-modal attention yields ~39% PSD MSE reduction over naive concatenation, indicating a significant role of maternal-fetal coupling.

Conclusion: The framework advances modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling Doppler envelope synthesis from dual-lead ECG and by enabling analysis of recoverable and residual Doppler components to isolate purely mechanical contributions, thereby supporting comprehensive fetal assessment.

Abstract: Fetal electrocardiogram (fECG) and Doppler ultrasound provide complementary views of fetal cardiovascular function: fECG captures electrical activity while Doppler reflects mechanical hemodynamics shaped by factors such as placental resistance and vascular compliance. Understanding the recoverable and unrecoverable Doppler components through reconstruction from fECG offers insight into the relative contributions of electrical versus mechanical factors in fetal circulation, thereby informing clinical decisions. In addition, clinical evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling suggests that maternal cardiovascular dynamics may also inform fetal hemodynamics. To computationally model these relationships, we propose a cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG and self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies, our model synthesizes Doppler envelopes with power spectral density mean squared error (PSD MSE) of 49.9 +/- 15.8 dB^2 (51% lower than two-channel baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 +/- 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline; negligible relative to the 110-160 bpm physiological range). Cross-modal attention yields a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation, quantifying the contribution of maternal-fetal coupling. Our proposed framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling the synthesis of Doppler envelopes from dual-lead ECG. By analysis of both recoverable and residual Doppler components, this approach enables quantification of the purely mechanical contributions to Doppler waveforms -- those not recoverable from electrical recordings -- ultimately facilitating a more comprehensive fetal assessment.

</details>


### [118] [Modular Pretraining Enables Access Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08077)
*Ethan Roland,Murat Cubuktepe,Erick Martinez,Stijn Servaes,Keenan Pepper,Mike Vaiana,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena,Judd Rosenblatt,Addie Foote,Cem Anil,Alex Cloud*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRAM enables selective disabling of model capabilities via gradient-routed auxiliary modules, offering a scalable, cost-effective alternative to data filtering for dual-use access control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address dual-use risk in AI by enabling multi-profile models without training many separate models; maintain performance on retained capabilities while removing targeted ones.

Method: Attach trainable auxiliary modules to a base network; selectively update modules during pre-training to induce specialization; ablate modules at inference to remove capabilities; evaluate on synthetic and real dual-use domains; compare against data filtering; scaling analysis across model sizes; cost comparison.

Result: GRAM can disable targeted capabilities while preserving others; more robust to finetuning than post-hoc unlearning; with scale, the data-filtered gap grows for removed capabilities but remains small for retained; closely tracks data filtering; training cost is independent of number of profiles, yielding ~5x reduction in 5-profile setting.

Conclusion: GRAM offers a scalable, cost-efficient approach to access control, potentially matching or surpassing data-filtered models in effectiveness while reducing training costs; demonstrates practicality across diverse dual-use domains.

Abstract: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma. An AI capability that helps one user cure a disease can help another synthesize one. This dilemma could be resolved with access control, limiting dual-use AI capabilities to trusted deployments with a legitimate need. A gold standard for access control would be to serve separate models with different capabilities to different users. However, training and deploying multiple models is prohibitively expensive. To address this challenge, we propose gradient-routed auxiliary modules (GRAM), a pre-training method that adds modules to a neural network and selectively updates them to induce specialization. Ablating a module at inference time removes its capability from the network, approximating a model trained on filtered data. We evaluate GRAM on synthetic stories and realistic dual-use data spanning virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, and specialized code. These experiments show that GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving the rest, and resists their recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Most importantly, a Chinchilla-optimal scaling analysis from 50M to 5B parameters shows that the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones, and that GRAM closely tracks data filtering. GRAM's training cost is independent of the number of supported capability profiles, yielding a 5x reduction over data filtering in our 5-profile setting.

</details>


### [119] [Deep Learning Method for Stationary Distribution of Reflected Brownian Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08091)
*Jim Dai,Zhanhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning framework to learn the Laplace transform of high-dimensional reflected Brownian motions using the Basic Adjoint Relationship (BAR), enabling efficient and accurate tail-probability estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed-form solutions for the stationary distribution and tail probabilities of high-dimensional RBMs are scarce; there is a need for scalable, data-driven methods that leverage structural identities like BAR.

Method: Design a neural network to approximate the Laplace transform of the RBM, with a loss function and training data sampling strategy grounded in the Basic Adjoint Relationship; evaluate on RBM instances with known tail probabilities and compare predictions to ground truth; provide open-source code.

Result: The method achieves near-perfect prediction of tail probabilities in high-dimensional RBMs and demonstrates strong generalization beyond analytically tractable regimes.

Conclusion: A BAR-guided deep learning approach is a promising general tool for analyzing high-dimensional stochastic systems, enabling accurate computation of Laplace transforms and tail metrics where analytic solutions are unavailable.

Abstract: The stationary distribution of reflected Brownian motion (RBM) plays an important role in the analysis of high-dimensional stochastic systems, yet closed-form solutions are known only for a few special cases. Computing important performance metrics, such as tail probabilities, is even more intractable, despite their practical relevance. In this paper, we develop a deep learning approach that accurately and efficiently learns the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR). Our framework combines a careful design of the loss function, training data sampling procedure, and neural network architecture. We evaluate the proposed method on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrate near-perfect prediction in high-dimensional settings, highlighting its potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes. Our code can be found at https://github.com/zhangz73/NN4MGF.

</details>


### [120] [Stochastic Order Learning: An Approach to Rank Estimation Using Noisy Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08103)
*Chaewon Lee,Seon-Ho Lee,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel framework, SOL, treats rank estimation with noisy ordinal labels as a stochastic ordering problem where each instance has multiple plausible ranks. It optimizes a dual-objective embedding with a discriminative loss and a stochastic order loss to model uncertainty and enforce probabilistic ordering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty; single deterministic labels are insufficient for robust rank estimation under noise. Modeling multiple plausible ranks per instance can improve robustness.

Method: Stochastic Order Learning (SOL) constructs an embedding space and employs two complementary losses: a discriminative loss that shapes interactions between instances and a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations among instances, reflecting multiple plausible ranks per sample.

Result: Extensive experiments on diverse datasets show SOL achieves reliable rank estimation across various noise types and levels; the method demonstrates robustness to label noise. The authors provide open-source code at https://github.com/cwlee00/SOL.

Conclusion: SOL successfully captures ordinal label uncertainty in rank estimation, improving robustness to noisy ordinal labels and offering a generalizable framework for ordinal tasks; the public implementation facilitates adoption and further exploration.

Abstract: Rank estimation under label noise poses a fundamental challenge, as ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption. In this paper, we reformulate rank estimation with noisy ordinal labels as a stochastic ordering problem, in which each instance is inherently associated with multiple plausible ranks instead of a single deterministic label. Based on this view, we propose stochastic order learning (SOL), a learning framework that captures ordinal label uncertainty and learns an embedding space through two complementary objectives: a discriminative loss that structures instance--centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations between instances. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate that SOL enables reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/SOL.

</details>


### [121] [Vanilla SGD with Momentum Survives Heavy-Tailed Noise: Convergence Analysis without Gradient Clipping or Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08104)
*Ryusei Yamada,Naoki Sato,Hideaki Iiduka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vanilla SGD with momentum, when faced with heavy-tailed gradient noise, converges across strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives but at suboptimal rates compared to robust variants (clipped/normalized SGD). Momentum does not fully mitigate heavy-tailed noise, and the analysis provides a unified, first-of-its-kind convergence treatment without gradient control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the fundamental performance of vanilla SGD with momentum under heavy-tailed gradient noise, and to establish theoretical convergence guarantees across common objective classes without relying on gradient clipping or normalization.

Method: Theoretical convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum across strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives in the presence of heavy-tailed noise, without using gradient control mechanisms; refinement of existing vanilla SGD results and empirical validation on synthetic functions.

Result: The convergence rates obtained are inferior to the optimal rates attainable by clipped or normalized variants of SGD, demonstrating inherent limitations of vanilla methods under heavy-tailed noise; momentum does not fully overcome the difficulty posed by heavy tails; results are corroborated by experiments on synthetic functions.

Conclusion: Vanilla SGD (even with momentum) has fundamental limitations under heavy-tailed noise; to achieve optimal convergence rates one should employ gradient clipping or normalization; the work provides a baseline and highlights the gap between vanilla and robust SGD methods.

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a cornerstone of modern optimization. While its performance under heavy-tailed noise is often addressed through specialized modifications such as gradient clipping or normalization, we investigate a more fundamental question: how does vanilla SGD, particularly with momentum, perform in the presence of heavy-tailed noise? In this paper, we refine existing convergence results for vanilla SGD and, more importantly, provide the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives, without employing any gradient control mechanisms. Our results demonstrate that the obtained convergence rates are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized variants of SGD, thereby revealing inherent limitations of vanilla methods under heavy-tailed noise. The theoretical findings are supported by experiments on synthetic functions.

</details>


### [122] [Contrastive Order Learning: A General Framework for Ordinal Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08109)
*Chaewon Lee,BeomJun Shim,Kwang Pyo Choi,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression called ConOrd integrates contrastive learning with order learning using a contrastive order loss that employs soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling global modeling of ordinal relationships across all batch pairs; achieves state-of-the-art results on tasks like facial age estimation and blind image/video quality assessment; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contrastive learning leverages all samples in a batch but ignores label ordinality, while order-learning models ordinality but relies on local, margin-based comparisons. There is a need to capture global ordinal structure within a batch for robust ordinal regression.

Method: Proposes a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights derived from rank differences, enabling fine-grained, global modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs in a batch; leverages contrastive learning objectives augmented with ordinal-aware weighting.

Result: Extensive experiments on ordinal regression tasks—facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment—demonstrate that ConOrd achieves state-of-the-art performance and generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios.

Conclusion: ConOrd effectively unifies contrastive learning and order learning to model global ordinal structure, offering improved performance in ordinal regression; source code available at the provided GitHub URL.

Abstract: We propose contrastive order learning (ConOrd), a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that integrates the strengths of contrastive learning and order learning. While contrastive learning effectively leverages all samples in a batch, it typically ignores the inherent ordering among rank labels. Conversely, order learning explicitly models label ordinality but often relies on local, margin-based comparisons, limiting its ability to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses these limitations by introducing a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling fine-grained modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs within a batch. Extensive experiments on a range of ordinal regression tasks, including facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment, demonstrate that ConOrd consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance and generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/ConOrd.

</details>


### [123] [Workload-Preserving Differentially Private Synthetic Data for Causal Inference via Maximum-Entropy Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08122)
*Amir Asiaee,Kaveh Aryan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes causal-workload DP synthetic data for preserving causal moments to enable valid causal inference (ATE/ATT) with a one-shot DP workload, plus adaptive workload selection (Causal-AIM) and noise-aware multiple-imputation for calibrated uncertainty; analyzes tradeoffs vs generic workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generic DP-synthetic-data workloads preserve distributional fidelity but often fail to preserve causal estimands, which rely on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments. The work aims to embed the relevant orthogonal moments from doubly robust estimators into the DP workload to enable valid causal inference under privacy constraints.

Method: Design DP query sets around orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators; enable direct use by stable moment-map estimators or reconstitute via maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data; provide a theoretical ATE error decomposition into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms; introduce Causal-AIM adaptive workload selector; propose noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data; ensure the single released workload supports ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without extra privacy spending.

Result: The framework yields a decomposition of ATE error into identifiable components; empirically, causal workloads offer advantages under strict DP budgets and when calibrated uncertainty is required, while generic workloads may outperform in point RMSE as privacy constraints loosen; a single causal workload can support multiple causal estimands without additional privacy spending.

Conclusion: Preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise are essential for valid causal inference with DP synthetic data. Distributional fidelity helps point accuracy, but the validity of causal conclusions requires design that targets causal moments and accounts for DP noise propagation. The broader takeaway is that workloads should be built with downstream causal estimators in mind, illustrating a tradeoff between distributional fidelity and causal validity.

Abstract: Workload-based differentially private (DP) synthetic data methods privately measure aggregate queries and post-process the noisy answers into synthetic records. Generic workloads can achieve strong distributional fidelity, but causal estimands such as the average treatment effect (ATE) depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments that generic marginals need not preserve. We propose causal workloads: DP query sets designed around the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators. The released workload can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or reconstructed by maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data; our theory decomposes ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms. We also introduce Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selector, and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data. Because the workload is released once, the same DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending. Empirically, causal workloads are most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty, while generic workloads often retain an advantage for point RMSE as privacy relaxes. The broader lesson is a tradeoff: distributional fidelity can help point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real.

</details>


### [124] [DeepPySR -- A Symbolic Regression Framework with Dynamic Pruning, Pareto Selection, and Hierarchical Composition for Real-World Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08150)
*Fuling Chen,Kevin Vinsen,Phillip Melton,Rae-Chi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepPySR extends symbolic regression with dynamic variable pruning, an exponential Pareto-based selection criterion, and a multi-layer hierarchical architecture, achieving improved accuracy and interpretability on physics and biomedical/social datasets compared to PySR and baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic regression promises glass-box models but faces high-dimensional inputs, fragile Pareto-front selection between accuracy and simplicity, and irregular data (multicollinearity, class imbalance). A method that prunes irrelevant features during search, robustly balances trade-offs, and enables hierarchical composition could yield interpretable, accurate models in sensitive domains like medicine and social science.

Method: Introduce a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to discard irrelevant features during search; adopt an exponential Pareto selection criterion to enforce a strong bias toward simpler models without sacrificing accuracy; implement a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic expression composition; evaluate on four Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical/social-science datasets, comparing against PySR and baselines.

Result: On body fat (R^2: 0.794 vs. 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs. 0.787), student performance (R^2: 0.964 vs. 0.948), and Raine BMI (R^2: 0.525 vs. 0.370), DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines, producing interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors.

Conclusion: DeepPySR provides a scalable, transparent SR framework addressing high dimensionality and data irregularities, delivering improved predictive performance and interpretable models across physics, biomedical, and social-science domains.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) discovers analytical equations from data, yielding glass-box models with directly interpretable formulas, unlike black-box methods that rely on unstable post-hoc tools such as SHAP or LIME. This transparency is crucial in clinical medicine and social science, but SR faces three challenges: high-dimensional inputs, principled selection of Pareto-front formulae, and data irregularities such as multicollinearity and class imbalance. We introduce DeepPySR, which addresses these issues with a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates trade-offs between accuracy and complexity, and a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition. On four Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical and social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines on body fat (R$^2$: 0.794 vs.\ 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs.\ 0.787), student performance (R$^2$: 0.964 vs.\ 0.948), and Raine BMI (R$^2$: 0.525 vs.\ 0.370), producing interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors.

</details>


### [125] [Understanding Layer Patching in Model Size Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08170)
*Sara Kangaslahti,Jonathan Geuter,Nihal V. Nayak,Marco Fumero,Francesco Locatello,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematically studies zero-shot model size interpolation via patching teacher layers into a student, reframes layer-subset selection as a shortest-path optimization, and introduces a KL-divergence-based greedy patching algorithm (KLPatch). Sequential patching (first-to-last or last-to-first) often performs surprisingly well; results vary by model family; provides practical guidance for near-optimal interpolated models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable intermediate-sized models without retraining by leveraging boomerang distillation and layer patching; understand how the choice of patched layers affects interpolation performance.

Method: Formulates the problem of selecting which layers to patch as an optimization; proves equivalence to finding a shortest path in an acyclic graph; analyzes the impact of patching order; proposes KLPatch, a greedy patching method based on KL divergence; compares sequential strategies (start-to-end, end-to-start) with KLPatch across model families.

Result: Layer patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior; simple sequential patching strategies often yield strong performance; KLPatch frequently improves over naive last-to-first patching and approximates the optimization objective; interpolation behavior and best strategies vary across model families, highlighting the need for principled guidance.

Conclusion: The study provides a principled understanding of how layer patching governs model size interpolation and offers practical algorithms and recommendations for constructing near-optimal interpolated models without additional training.

Abstract: Zero-shot model size interpolation aims to create new models of intermediate target sizes by combining existing models without additional training. Recent work on boomerang distillation [Kangaslahti et al., 2026] shows that a student language model distilled from a larger teacher can be expanded by iteratively patching its layers, replacing student layers with contiguous blocks of teacher layers to obtain models whose size and performance interpolate between the student and the teacher. In this work, we provide the first systematic study of student-layer selection for model size interpolation. We cast finding the optimal layer subset for each model size as an optimization problem and prove it can be viewed as a shortest-path problem in a certain acyclic graph. In experiments, we show that patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects that vary substantially across model families. We find that simple sequential strategies--patching either from the first layer to the last or from the last to the first--often achieve surprisingly strong performance in practice. We further introduce KLPatch, a greedy patching algorithm based on KL divergence, which often improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Together, our results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offer practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models.

</details>


### [126] [Open-ended Multi-agent Autocurricula via Visual Inspection of Policies with Multi-modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08193)
*Lorenzo Pantè,Andrea Fanti,Roberto Capobianco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Video Language Model-based framework (VIP) analyzes policy videos to generate curricula, yielding more effective training than text-only or scalar-score baselines in SMAC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing open-ended RL curricula requires measuring task difficulty relative to an agent's learning progress. Policy videos capture rich behavioral cues that static scores or textual summaries may miss, motivating a visual-based assessment.

Method: VIP uses a lightweight Video Language Model (VideoLLaMa2-7B) to process recorded policy videos and produce curriculum recommendations. The evaluation is conducted on StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC), comparing VIP against a text-only ablation and scalar task-score baselines.

Result: VIP-generated curricula lead to more effective learning progress than the baselines, demonstrating that video-based policy inspection can outperform scalar metrics and text-only signals in guiding RL curricula.

Conclusion: Visual inspection of policies via VLMs is a viable, lightweight approach for automatic curriculum design in RL, with demonstrated effectiveness on SMAC and potential for broader applicability and scalability.

Abstract: Open-ended curricula in Reinforcement Learning (RL) aim to train generally-capable agents by identifying tasks that facilitate learning increasingly complex skills. A major challenge when designing such curricula is assessing task difficulty relative to the agent's current learning progress. While previous work has explored using scalar task scores or textual summaries of the agent's behavior, here we study a different approach: directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos. We introduce a simple yet effective instantiation of this approach which leverages a Video Language Model (VLM) to both process these videos and provide curriculum recommendations, which we call Visual Inspection of Policies (VIP). Since videos can naturally contain any number of controllable agents, we empirically study VIP on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC). We show that even with a lightweight and openly accessible VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B), VIP can use policy videos to generate more effective curricula than both its text-only ablation and methods that rely on scalar task scores.

</details>


### [127] [PIT-SUN: A Deployable Empirical Marginal Transform Framework with Expectation-Consistent Recovery for Regression in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08202)
*Mingyu Zhao,Zhaohan Li,Zhenxiong Miao,Xu Zhang,Dewei Leng,Yanan Niu,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PIT-SUN is a deployable marginal-recovery framework that uses probability-integral transforms and multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate original-space expectations, improving point accuracy, calibration, and ranking with lightweight deployment overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MSE struggles with heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets in value-driven recommender systems; inverse transforms break expectation consistency, and existing linear recovery lacks guidance for sparse, complex marginals.

Method: Construct a single empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate; perform inverse-quantile lookup; select a variance-controlled recovery base and drift monitoring; apply multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate the original-space expectation instead of inverting transformed predictions.

Result: Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

Conclusion: PIT-SUN offers a practical, deployable framework for marginal recovery that preserves expectation-consistency and improves performance for value-driven recommendation under complex marginal distributions.

Abstract: Estimating original-space conditional expectations is central to value-driven recommender systems, including dwell time, GMV, and LTV forecasting. Standard MSE is expectation-consistent in principle, but its gradients become unstable on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets, causing mean collapse and tail shrinkage. Target transformation alleviates this scale conflict, yet any useful nonlinear marginal transform loses expectation consistency under direct inversion. This is not an implementation oversight: a direct inverse-transform estimator is universally expectation-consistent only when the inverse transform is affine, which cannot simultaneously provide bounded tail compression.
