Method: Introduce a partial dependence-based steering objective that constrains the model’s marginal response over chosen features to match a user-specified target function, implemented within neural network training and evaluated on various regression tasks including dynamical systems forecasting.

Result: Models trained with the proposed method outperform unconstrained models, are more data-efficient, and yield explanations that align with the user-provided knowledge, unlike explanations from unconstrained models.

Conclusion: Partial dependence–based explanation-guided learning is effective for aligning model behavior with domain knowledge while improving predictive performance and interpretability, particularly in regression and dynamical systems forecasting; potential for broader applicability in other settings.

Abstract: Over the last few years, there has been an increased interest in making machine learning models more interpretable. Although a great deal of effort goes into developing techniques for interpreting the interactions learned by a given model, fewer studies focus on assessing the quality of such explanations. Even fewer focus on how to adjust the model to produce explanations faithful to prior knowledge, a process known as explanation-guided learning. Furthermore, most approaches in this area focus on classification problems and usually assume prior knowledge about which input features or regions are most important. In this work, we introduce a new approach to steering neural networks based on partial dependence, such that their average response to certain features aligns with specific functional domain knowledge about the problem. We empirically demonstrate on a range of regression problems, including dynamical systems forecasting, that models whose training has been controlled using our method perform better than unconstrained models and are more data-efficient. Moreover, we highlight that interpretations obtained from the former actually align with the user-provided knowledge, whereas those obtained from the latter do not.

</details>


### [155] [BiSCo-LLM: Lookup-Free Binary Spherical Coding for Extreme Low-Bit Large Language Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08643)
*Yuantian Shao,Peisong Wang,Zhilei Liu,Chuangyi Li,Yuanteng Chen,Pengcheng Xie,Yiwu Yao,Zhihui Wei,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Codebook-free binary spherical coding (BiSCo-LLM) for extreme low-bit LLM weight compression using hyperspherical binarization, residual BSQ, and category-wise recovery distillation. Includes a small 8-bit auxiliary channel; no explicit codebooks or index overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome memory, bandwidth, and storage bottlenecks in deploying large LLMs, where scalar/group quantization loses capacity near 2 bits/weight and vector quantization incurs codebooks and extra storage.

Method: 1) Map local weight chunks to a unit hypersphere and binarize to spherical codes, yielding a bit-packed sign stream as payload. 2) Apply residual Binary Spherical Quantization (BSQ) to encode reconstruction error without stored codebooks, providing a rate-distortion path. 3) Perform category-wise recovery distillation after replacing Transformer module categories to reduce mismatch between reconstructed weights and model behavior. 4) Use an 8-bit protected-channel path as auxiliary stabilization for sensitive channels, counted separately from BSQ payload. Storage budget includes binary codes, neural decoders, protected-channel payloads, LoRA adapters, and metadata.

Result: Abstract does not report quantitative results. It outlines a storage-budget-focused framework with codebook-free spherical coding, residual distortion encoding, category-wise distillation, and a small auxiliary channel.

Conclusion: BiSCo-LLM presents a novel codebook-free approach to extreme low-bit LLM compression via spherical coding and residual quantization, aiming to minimize storage while maintaining model behavior through distillation and stabilization mechanisms.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly constrained by memory capacity, weight bandwidth, and checkpoint storage during deployment. Existing low-bit compression methods mainly follow two directions. Scalar or group-wise quantization is simple and compatible with efficient low-precision kernels, but its representation capacity becomes limited when the target budget approaches 2 bits per weight. Vector-quantized weight compression provides a richer block-level representation, but usually introduces explicit codebooks, index lookup, and additional storage accounting. This paper presents BiSCo-LLM, a codebook-free binary spherical coding framework for extreme low-bit LLM weight compression. The core pipeline is built on three components. First, local weight chunks are mapped onto a unit hypersphere and binarized into compact spherical codes, so that the main payload is a bit-packed sign stream rather than explicit VQ centroids. Second, a residual BSQ stage encodes the reconstruction error left by the base spherical codec, providing an explicit rate-distortion path without stored codebooks. Third, category-wise recovery distillation is performed after replacing each Transformer module category, reducing the mismatch between local weight reconstruction and assembled model behavior. A small 8-bit protected-channel path is used as an auxiliary stabilization mechanism for sensitive channels and is counted separately from the BSQ payload. The reported storage budget includes binary codes, neural decoders, protected-channel payloads, LoRA adapters, and metadata.

</details>


### [156] [Multi-Modal, Multi-Environment Machine Teaching for Robust Reward Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08647)
*Ali Larian,Qian Lin,Chang Zong Wu,Daniel S. Brown*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-environment, multi-modal teaching for inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) to learn rewards robust to distribution shifts; shows comparisons as the strongest global constraints in unlimited data; introduces a hierarchical teaching algorithm across multiple MDPs and demonstrates improved generalization and lower regret vs uniform baselines under fixed feedback budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IRL rewards inferred from demonstrations or feedback often overfit to a single environment. To deploy agents in new contexts, reward functions must generalize across environment dynamics and accommodate heterogeneous feedback modalities.

Method: Proposes a hierarchical machine-teaching algorithm that operates across multiple MDPs: first greedily selects informative environments to expose complementary reward constraints, then queries low-cost feedback within those environments. The work also analyzes how different modalities constrain rewards, showing comparisons impose stronger global constraints in the unlimited-data regime.

Result: Empirically, the proposed approach achieves substantially lower regret and stronger generalization to held-out environments than uniform teaching baselines under the same feedback budget.

Conclusion: Multi-environment, multi-modal teaching is essential for learning reward functions that generalize across dynamic contexts; naive single-environment teaching is insufficient for robust IRL.

Abstract: As autonomous agents are increasingly deployed across diverse operational contexts, aligning their behavior with human intent demands reward functions that remain robust to such changes rather than overfitting to any single environment. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) provides a principled way to infer such objectives from human feedback. However, existing analyses of optimal teaching approaches for IRL focus on single-environment, demonstration-only settings, leaving underexplored how heterogeneous feedback modalities and environment dynamics jointly constrain reward functions that generalize across multiple environments. Because demonstrations in one MDP entangle reward information with that environments specific structure, the resulting rewards frequently fail to generalize when the agent is deployed in a new setting. We first analyze how different feedback modalities constrain rewards, showing that, in the unlimited-data regime, comparisons impose strictly stronger global constraints than other modalities. Beyond this theoretical analysis, we introduce a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm for reward learning that operates across multiple MDPs. The algorithm first greedily selects informative environments that expose complementary reward constraints, then strategically queries low-cost feedback within those environments. Empirically, our method achieves substantially lower regret and stronger generalization to held-out environments than uniform teaching baselines under identical feedback budgets, demonstrating the importance of multi-environment, multi-modal teaching for learning dynamics-robust reward functions.

</details>


### [157] [Secure Decentralized Federated Learning via Gossip and Virtual Voting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08651)
*Amirhossein Taherpour,Xiaodong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A secure, gossip-derived DFL framework (gspDAG-FL) that achieves provenance finality using a Hashgraph-style consensus over a compact topology DAG, enabling peer-to-peer model exchange with verification, while tolerating Byzantine and lazy participants and reducing coordination costs; experiments show learning quality near ledger-FL with improved throughput up to N=100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide auditability, provenance finality, and resilience in decentralized federated learning without reintroducing global coordination costs; address Byzantine and lazy participants while preserving DFL locality.

Method: Nodes exchange model payloads with neighbors; full nodes collect event certificates and accepted gossip proofs, reconstruct a topology DAG, and perform Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates; finality is defined over unique model-origin tuples. The framework combines payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation; formal adversarial model; safety and conditional liveness proven; convergence guarantee for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying mixing.

Result: Safety and conditional liveness of the control plane are proven; a convergence guarantee is provided for certified perturbed gossip with time-varying effective mixing. Empirical results on MNIST and Penn Treebank (N up to 100) show learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL, with reduced coordination bottlenecks, higher throughput, and strong invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

Conclusion: gspDAG-FL offers secure, scalable decentralized FL with ledger-like provenance and finality, achieving near-ledger learning quality while mitigating coordination costs and remaining robust to adversaries.

Abstract: Decentralized federated learning (DFL) removes the central server by letting nodes exchange model updates through peer-to-peer gossip, but existing gossip-based methods often lack provenance finality and resilience to Byzantine or lazy participants. Ledger-assisted federated learning (FL) improves auditability, yet blockchains, shards, or settlement committees can reintroduce global coordination costs that conflict with DFL locality. This paper proposes \emph{gspDAG-FL}, a secure DFL framework that derives consensus from the same gossip history used to disseminate models. Nodes exchange model payloads only with neighbors, while full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs, reconstruct a compact Topology directed acyclic graph (DAG), and run Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates. Finality is over unique model-origin tuples, not identical local parameter states. To improve resilience, gspDAG-FL combines payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation. We formalize the adversarial setting, prove safety and conditional liveness of the control plane, and give a convergence guarantee for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying effective mixing. Experiments on MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling, using fair held-out validation/audit data and networks up to \(N=100\), show that gspDAG-FL achieves learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

</details>


### [158] [EdgeRefine: Privacy-Utility Balance for Graphs via Jaccard Sampling under Edge Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08659)
*Wenxiu Ding,Muzhi Liu,Zheng Yan,Mingjun Wang,Yifan Zhao,Qiao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EdgeRefine is a local-DP framework for graph learning that adaptively refines edges to improve the privacy-utility trade-off. It uses Jaccard-based edge existence estimates to rank and selectively remove edges, with privacy budget epsilon guiding the true/false edge ratio and a sampling rate k controlling total edge count. Empirically, it achieves near noise-free accuracy and outperforms baselines across datasets and architectures, with strong resilience to graph reconstruction attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph-structured data can leak sensitive link information. Edge-level differential privacy that naively perturbs all adjacency entries degrades utility as privacy strength increases. A targeted, budget-aware approach to edge refinement could preserve utility while maintaining privacy.

Method: Estimate edge existence with Jaccard similarity to obtain probability rankings for edges. Use privacy budget ε to determine the ratio of true to false edges, and sample edges separately according to this ranking. Control the total number of edges with a sampling rate k. Evaluate on multiple GNNs and datasets under edge-DP and test against reconstruction attacks.

Result: EdgeRefine achieves accuracy comparable to the noise-free baseline and substantially outperforms privacy-preserving baselines. For ε=2.5: +17.8% node accuracy on ACM with GAT, +19.7% on Cora with GCN. Graph classification incurs ~5% average accuracy degradation versus the noise-free baseline. Against graph reconstruction attacks, relative absolute errors exceed 1 across budgets (e.g., 1.962 on Cora, 1.472 on AMAP).

Conclusion: Adaptive, budget-aware edge refinement under local DP can substantially improve the privacy-utility trade-off in graph learning, delivering strong accuracy and robustness to privacy leakage with competitive performance across tasks and architectures.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have shown considerable success in learning from graph-structured data, but their use in privacy-sensitive areas remains difficult because graph structure can leak sensitive link information. To satisfy edge-level differential privacy, a common approach is to inject noise into all elements of the graph's adjacency matrix, thereby obfuscating the existence of any single edge. However, stronger privacy requires more noise, and excessive noise reduces utility, making the privacy-utility balance a major barrier to practical privacy-preserving graph learning.
  To address this issue, we propose EdgeRefine, a local differential privacy framework that improves this trade-off through adaptive edge refinement. EdgeRefine first estimates edge-existence probabilities using Jaccard similarity and ranks edges for noisy edge removal. To ensure the sparsity and reliability of the final graph, it uses the privacy budget $ε$ to determine the ratio of true to false edges, samples them separately based on this probability ranking, and controls the total number of edges with a separate sampling rate $k$. Extensive experiments show that EdgeRefine achieves accuracy comparable to the noise-free baseline and substantially outperforms other privacy-preserving methods across datasets and GNN architectures. Under privacy budget $ε= 2.5$, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines by 17.8\% on ACM under GAT and 19.7\% on Cora under GCN. In graph classification, it achieves an average accuracy degradation of around 5\% compared to the noise-free baseline. Under graph reconstruction attacks, EdgeRefine maintains relative absolute error levels above 1 across all privacy budgets, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP, indicating strong resilience against privacy leakage.

</details>


### [159] [Resample or Reroute? Budget-Aware Test-Time Model Selection for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08665)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Budget-aware test-time model selection (RoR) for LLM routing; allocates per-query budget between resampling and rerouting to maximize expected correctness; online policy; empirical results show favorable Pareto frontier vs baselines, verifier-gated gains, robustness analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackles the cost-quality trade-off in routing LLMs by treating resampling and rerouting as competing uses of a single budget. Deployed systems face imperfect verifiers and finite budgets, unlike idealized oracles.

Method: Introduce an online resample-or-reroute (RoR) policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost; exploit recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling; perform replay experiments on an 11-model open-weight pool across four benchmarks; compare to baselines (single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, random allocation); conduct ablations and verifier-gated analyses; assess robustness under provider price vectors and label-free verifiers.

Result: RoR attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto frontier; gains are largest on the most heterogeneous benchmark; ablations show gains are verifier-gated and shrink as verifier quality degrades; robustness experiments clarify where conclusions generalize.

Conclusion: Budget-aware RoR improves per-query correctness under constrained budgets with imperfect verifiers; effectiveness depends on verifier quality; results inform practical deployment and highlight the need for reliable verification and pricing considerations.

Abstract: Routing among large language models (LLMs) trades response quality against serving cost, motivated by the reported gap between deployed routers and a per-instance oracle. Recent analysis shows that test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures; however, that guarantee holds only under an idealized oracle equipped with correctness labels and an unconstrained budget, neither of which a deployed system has. To the best of our knowledge, no previous work treats resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. Therefore, this work formulates budget-aware test-time model selection: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate each unit of budget between resampling and rerouting so that expected correctness is maximized. An online resample-or-reroute (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost is proposed, and its behavior is grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Replay experiments on newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool over four benchmarks of differing difficulty show that the proposed RoR policy attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines for the tested pools, with the largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark; an ablation further shows the gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades, and robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where the conclusions carry over.

</details>


### [160] [A Practical Investigation of Training-free Relaxed Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08690)
*Guoxuan Xia,Luka Ribar,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Relaxed speculative decoding aims to speed up autoregressive sampling by using a faster drafter and verifying in parallel, trading exact lossless preservation for speed and flexibility; gains depend on drafter quality and require capability evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically analyze training-free relaxed speculative decoding approaches, unify existing methods within a common framework, benchmark them, and extract practical guidance for practitioners.

Method: Empirical benchmarking across contemporary settings, synthesis of existing relaxed speculative decoding approaches into a unified framework, and characterization of trade-offs (speed, verification, and capability).

Result: Relaxation can yield speedups but imposes substantial capability evaluation needs; many relaxed methods rely on a high-quality drafter (a strong language model), making them unsuitable for lightweight multi-token drafters.

Conclusion: Relaxed speculative decoding offers speed and flexibility gains at the cost of additional evaluation of model capabilities; practitioners should consider drafter strength and whether lossless guarantees are essential.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates sampling from an autoregressive LLM by using a faster auxiliary model to draft tokens which are then verified in parallel by the LLM. Standard speculative decoding is lossless: its rejection and resampling steps exactly preserve the LLM's sampling distribution. Recent work argues that relaxing this strict guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains. We practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unify existing approaches within a shared framework, benchmark them on contemporary settings, and distil takeaways and empirical findings for practitioners. Important takeaways include: relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding, and many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

</details>


### [161] [MPFlow: Learning Budgeted Max-Flow Optimization on the Lightning Network with Deep Graph Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08703)
*Harrison Rush,Vincent Davis,Simone Antonelli,Vikash Singh,Jesse Shrader,Emanuele Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL-based liquidity placement in Bitcoin Lightning Network to maximize routing capacity under a fixed budget by adding edges to maximize s-t max-flow; uses graph neural network policy with PPO and action masking, trained with a hub-exclusion curriculum; outperforms heuristics and deployed in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve routing capacity and liquidity distribution in the Lightning Network under budget constraints; leverage a data-driven, topology-aware method to outperform simple heuristics and generalize to unseen network topologies.

Method: Formulates the problem as budget-constrained edge additions to maximize s-t max-flow. Proposes a lightweight graph neural network policy trained with PPO and action masking, using a hub-exclusion curriculum that removes top hubs from training subgraphs to encourage capacity-aware placement. Evaluates on real LN snapshots with multiple seeds and unseen graphs; deployed for peer recommendations.

Result: The approach consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across seeds and unseen graphs. It has been deployed in production, executing 4640 channel-open decisions that allocated 267.3 BTC over $16 million across 30 managed nodes.

Conclusion: RL-guided channel placement is effective for LN liquidity optimization, demonstrating strong generalization to unseen graphs and practical viability through production deployment; curriculum and capacity-aware learning mitigate hub bias and improve max-flow routing capacity.

Abstract: We address liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network (LN): given a fixed budget, which channels should a node open to maximize its routing capacity? We cast this as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem on graphs, selecting $k$ edge additions that maximize $s$--$t$ max-flow, a theory-grounded measure of routing capacity, and solve it with graph reinforcement learning. Our lightweight agent combines a message-passing policy network with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking, and is trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum: the network's top hubs are removed from training subgraphs, forcing the policy to learn capacity-aware placement rather than hub attachment. In extensive experiments on real Lightning Network snapshots, our method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs. The agent has been deployed in production for peer recommendations, executing 4640 channel-open decisions that cumulatively allocate 267.3 BTC over $16 million across 30 managed nodes.

</details>


### [162] [Deep Learning for Joint Narrowband Interference Cancellation and Soft Demodulation in OFDM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08717)
*Emmanouil Kavvousanos,Francky Catthoor,Vassilis Paliouras*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified deep learning framework (NBI-CNet + LLR-CNet) jointly cancels multi-tone NBI and performs robust soft demodulation for OFDM, achieving lower latency, reduced complexity, and mitigation of error floors compared to CS-based methods and baselines, with strong performance gains and FFT-size generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Narrowband interference severely degrades OFDM by corrupting subcarriers and producing non-Gaussian residuals that undermine soft-demodulation. Conventional CS-based mitigation incurs high latency and leaves residuals that cause LLR unreliability and error floors.

Method: Two-stage DL architecture: (1) NBI-CNet—a physics-informed convolutional network that estimates NBI parameters and cancels multi-tone interference in one forward pass without needing the interferer count; (2) LLR-CNet—a residual-whitening network that maps post-Mitigation non-Gaussian residuals to well-calibrated soft metrics, effectively correcting LLRs.

Result: NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% vs EOMP-IDS for large sizes (N=2048, Q=64). The joint framework eliminates error floors, achieves 0.2–0.5 dB SNR margin to an optimal iterative baseline at BLER=1e-4 under SIR=-10 dB, and >3 dB coding gain under milder interference with heavy spectral overlap (Q=12). It avoids signal-peak confusion and prevents a 2e-4 error floor from interferer-estimation errors, with scale-invariant generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes.

Conclusion: A unified DL-based solution efficiently and robustly mitigates NBI in OFDM, addressing both interference cancellation and reliable soft demodulation, yielding high performance, reduced latency, and strong generalization without retraining for different FFT sizes.

Abstract: Narrowband interference (NBI) severely degrades orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM) systems by corrupting subcarriers and rendering classical soft demodulation ineffective. Conventional compressed-sensing (CS) mitigation exhibits high sequential latency and leaves structured, non-Gaussian residuals that cause log-likelihood ratio (LLR) unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when employing classical Gaussian demappers. We resolve this pipeline mismatch using a unified deep learning framework for joint NBI cancellation and robust soft demodulation. First, NBI-CNet employs a physics-informed convolutional architecture to estimate NBI parameters and remove multi-tone interference in a single forward pass. Without requiring prior knowledge of the active interferer count, NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% ($N{=}2048, Q{=}64$) compared to the state-of-the-art EOMP-IDS algorithm. Second, LLR-CNet acts as a structural whitener by mapping non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft metrics. Simulations demonstrate that this joint framework eliminates the error floors inherent to traditional baselines across dense grids. Under severe interference ($\text{SIR}{=}{-}10$ dB), the pipeline operates within a $0.2$ to $0.5$ dB SNR margin of the optimal iterative baseline at a target block error rate (BLER) of $10^{-4}$. Under mild interference ($\text{SIR}{=}10$ dB) with heavy spectral overlap ($Q{=}12$), where classical greedy algorithms erroneously subtract valid data components and corrupt the payload, NBI-CNet avoids signal-peak confusion to deliver a coding gain exceeding $3$ dB. Finally, the architecture circumvents the $2{\times}10^{-4}$ error floor triggered by interferer-estimation errors, while its scale-invariant design enables robust generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

</details>


### [163] [Super Weights in LLMs and the Failure of Selective Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08733)
*Shreyas Subramanian,Adewale Akinfaderin,Akarsha Sehwag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training and pruning 'Super Weights' (individually important parameters) do not universally predict trainability or improve fine-tuning. Isolating or targeting these weights fails; structured, full-layer decompositions (e.g., LoRA) are effective even with tiny parameter budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the claim that a small set of high-importance parameters (Super Weights) govern model performance under pruning, and to compare their trainability to structured low-rank fine-tuning methods across LLMs.

Method: 1) Prune Super Weights and assess degradation across OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B. 2) Train Super Weights in isolation (100–8,192 params) and expand to neighborhoods (up to ~36K) in down_proj layers; compare to random positions. 3) Apply Vanilla LoRA: update all positions in attention matrices via low-rank updates (0.16% of params), and extend similar low-rank updates to down_proj. 4) 10-seed ablation: constrain LoRA updates to Super Weight coordinates vs unconstrained. 

Result: Pruning Super Weights degrades performance but not universally across LLMs. Isolated training of Super Weights collapses to random-guess accuracy for OLMo-1B/7B; expanding to neighborhoods yields no improvement. Targeted training of Super Weights causes failure, while randomly chosen equal-sized parameter sets in down_proj can improve performance. Vanilla LoRA with full-layer attention updates achieves strong fine-tuning with only 0.16% of parameters, and similar LoRA updates to down_proj also work. Constraining LoRA updates to Super Weight coordinates yields results indistinguishable from unconstrained LoRA, showing no special trainability of Super Weights. 

Conclusion: Parameter importance does not imply trainability in isolation. Effective fine-tuning hinges on structured, layer-wide decompositions (like LoRA) rather than targeting individual 'Super Weights', and sparsity alone (via pruning) is not inherently beneficial in isolation.

Abstract: Recent work identified Super Weights, individual parameters whose removal degrades model performance by orders of magnitude. We show that this degradation due to pruning Super Weights does not universally apply to all LLMs. Furthermore, if these parameters are so important, Super Weight-aware training should be effective. We show the opposite. Training Super Weights in isolation (100 to 8,192 parameters) drops accuracy to random-guessing levels on both OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B, and expanding to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters provides no improvement. The failure is specific to Super Weight coordinates: training an equal number of randomly chosen positions in the same down_proj layers instead improves over the baseline, so the collapse comes from targeting Super Weights, not from sparsity itself. Vanilla LoRA, updating every position in attention weight matrices through low-rank structure, succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters, and applying the same low-rank update to down_proj succeeds as well. A 10-seed ablation confirms that constraining LoRA updates at positions corresponding to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results. These findings establish that parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability in isolation, and that effective fine-tuning relies on structured decompositions over entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights.

</details>


### [164] [Dimensionality Reduction Meets Network Science: Sensemaking on UMAP's kNN Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08746)
*Duen Horng Chau,Donghao Ren,Fred Hohman,Dominik Moritz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Leverage UMAP's internal kNN graph for graph-based data sensemaking, not just the 2D embedding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UMAP constructs a high-dimensional kNN graph that encodes the data manifold before projection; exploiting this graph can yield interpretable insights using standard graph algorithms.

Method: Apply PageRank to identify representative points, use k-core decomposition to reveal dense cores versus sparse peripheries, and compute clustering coefficients to detect tight-knit neighborhoods on UMAP's internal kNN graph; evaluate on MNIST and Fashion MNIST and compare with exemplar methods like k-medoids and density-based clustering like HDBSCAN.

Result: Graph-based analyses yield interpretable, complementary insights: PageRank highlights representative data points, k-core reveals core-periphery structure, and clustering coefficient exposes tight-knit neighborhoods; performance is competitive with or complementary to specialized methods.

Conclusion: Interpreting UMAP's internal kNN graph with standard graph analyses provides practical, complementary data sensemaking that extends beyond the 2D embedding.

Abstract: While UMAP is widely used for exploring high-dimensional data, typical workflows focus on its lower-dimensional embedding, largely overlooking the rich k-nearest-neighbor (kNN) graph that UMAP constructs internally. This graph encodes the data manifold in its original high-dimensional space, before the distortion that UMAP's 2D projection introduces. We demonstrate the untapped potential of this internal representation, showing how standard graph algorithms applied to this graph enhance data sensemaking: (1) PageRank identifies representative data points, (2) k-core decomposition reveals dense core regions versus sparse periphery, and (3) clustering coefficient detects tight-knit neighborhoods with highly-similar data points. Through quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST, we show that these graph-based analyses are not only practical but also competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids for exemplar selection, HDBSCAN for density-based clustering).

</details>


### [165] [SLORR: Simple and Efficient In-Training Low-Rank Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08754)
*David González-Martínez,Shiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLORR is a stateless, architecture-preserving training-time low-rank regularization framework using Hoyer and nuclear-norm variants, with GPU-friendly approximations to regularizers, enabling compressible models at low overhead across vision and language pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-time low-rank regularizers rely on costly SVDs, modify architectures with extra parameters, or depend on stateful caches, hindering compressibility and practicality.

Method: Proposes SLORR, two variants based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and the nuclear norm, using GPU-friendly forward/backward approximations for the regularizers, stateless and architecture-preserving, with approximation guarantees.

Result: In ImageNet-1K short-horizon training for ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, ViT-L/16; pretraining ResNet-18; SLORR yields compressibility with <8% training overhead; SLORR-Hoyer in LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales preserves performance substantially better than unregularized models, with <1% average training overhead.

Conclusion: SLORR enables effective, low-cost, in-training low-rank regularization that preserves model performance while achieving compressibility; offers two viable variants and maintains architecture integrity.

Abstract: Low-rank factorization is widely used to compress neural networks, but modern models are often not naturally amenable to aggressive factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers can improve compressibility, but they often require SVDs of large weight matrices, modify the model architecture (introducing additional trainable parameters), or rely on stateful cached quantities. To address these limitations, we introduce SLORR, a simple, stateless, and architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization, instantiated with two main variants based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and the nuclear norm. SLORR directly regularizes the original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for the forward and backward passes of the regularizers, for which we provide approximation guarantees. We first evaluate SLORR on ImageNet-1K across short-horizon continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16, and pretraining of ResNet-18, where SLORR induces compressibility while introducing less than 8% training overhead. We further evaluate SLORR-Hoyer in LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales: SLORR-trained compressed models preserve performance substantially better than unregularized models while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [Covering Points with Rectangular Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08183)
*Madhumita Kundu,Daniel Lokshtanov,Soumi Nandi,Saket Saurabh,Kushal Singanporia*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Discrete boundary covering by axis-parallel rectangles is W[1]-hard in k; continuous version is fixed-parameter tractable with 2^{O(k log k)} poly(n) time; NP-completeness results for L-shapes; NP-completeness of the continuous problem prbc.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend the boundary-covering framework (Langerman–Morin) from hyperspheres to axis-parallel rectangles and compare discrete vs continuous variants.

Method: Define BCDAPR (discrete) and PBC (continuous). Prove W[1]-hardness for BCDAPR parameterized by k. Develop an FPT algorithm for PBC via structural analysis of k rectangles and a reduction to at most 2^{O(k log k)} instances of DDMT-CSP, each solvable in polynomial time. Prove NP-completeness for boundary covering by axis-aligned L-shapes and use this to establish NP-completeness of PBC.

Result: Key boundaries between complexity classes: discrete version is W[1]-hard; continuous version is FPT (2^{O(k log k)}n^{O(1)}). NP-completeness for L-shapes and for the continuous boundary covering problem via reductions.

Conclusion: Continuous boundary covering by axis-parallel rectangles is tractable in the FPT sense; the discrete version remains computationally intractable under standard assumptions. The work clarifies the complexity landscape and suggests further study on special cases or tighter FPT bounds.

Abstract: Geometric covering problems ask for a small family of geometric objects whose union covers a given point set. We study the more restrictive \emph{boundary covering} variant, where every point must lie on the boundary of a chosen object. Motivated by the framework of Langerman and Morin\,[Discret.\ Comput.\ Geom., 2005] for hyperspheres, we initiate the study of boundary covering by axis-parallel rectangles.
  We first consider the \emph{discrete} setting, where rectangles must be selected from a given family. We define \bcdaprfull\ (\bcdaprshort): given a point set \(P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^2\), a family \(\mathcal{R}\) of axis-parallel rectangles, and an integer \(k\), decide whether \(P\) can be covered by the boundaries of at most \(k\) rectangles from \(\mathcal{R}\). We prove that \bcdaprshort\ is \(\mathrm{W}[1]\)-hard parameterized by \(k\).
  We then study the \emph{continuous} variant, \prbcfull\ (\prbcshort), where rectangles may be placed freely. Given \(P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^2\) and \(k\), the goal is to decide whether \(P\) can be covered by the boundaries of at most \(k\) axis-parallel rectangles. In contrast to the discrete case, we show that \prbcshort\ is fixed-parameter tractable, with running time \(2^{\cO(k\log k)}\cdot n^{\cO(1)}\), where \(n=|P|\). Our algorithm relies on a structural analysis of how \(k\) rectangles interact with the point set, reducing \prbcshort\ to at most \(2^{\cO(k\log k)}\) instances of \ddmtcsp, each solvable in polynomial time. On the hardness side, we prove NP-completeness for boundary covering by axis-aligned \(L\)-shapes and use this reduction to establish NP-completeness of \prbcshort.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [167] [SASGeo: Stability-Aware Semantic Map Localization for GNSS-Denied UAVs -- A Framework and Synthetic Proof of Concept](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07737)
*Natalia Trukhina,Vadim Vashkelis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Semantic map-localization (SAS) uses persistent semantic structures and structured evidence to localize GNSS-denied UAVs via cross-view retrieval, achieving strong discriminability in synthetic tests but lacking real-flight validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNSS-denied UAVs require occasional absolute fixes to bound drift in visual-inertial odometry. Appearance-based cross-view retrieval is fragile to season, illumination, and map changes. SAS proposes semantic geometry as a robust alternative.

Method: SAS represents environment with persistent structures (roads, buildings, waterways, railways, intersections, field boundaries) and integrates semantic raster alignment, relational graph evidence, feature stability, and geographic distinctiveness. It uses explicit positive/contradictory/unknown observations and integrity-aware rejection; provides concrete weighting and decision models and a reproducible synthetic proof of concept.

Result: In 220 randomized trials with rotation, scale changes, crops, occlusion, map changes, and decoys, global semantic descriptor achieved 58.6% Recall@1; spatial semantic matching variants achieved 94.5–95.5%. Wilson 95% intervals separate global from spatial variants but overlap among spatial variants, indicating semantic geometry benefits but not decisive contribution of individual modules.

Conclusion: Structured semantic geometry can discriminate locations under controlled cross-view perturbations; highlights aliasing, map aging, and rejection tests needed for future real-world validation and flight navigation experiments.

Abstract: GNSS-denied unmanned aerial vehicles require occasional absolute position fixes to bound the drift of visual-inertial odometry. Cross-view image retrieval can provide such fixes, but raw appearance is sensitive to season, illumination, viewpoint, map age, and sensor modality. We propose \sas, a semantic map-localization framework that represents the environment through persistent structures such as roads, buildings, waterways, railways, intersections, and field boundaries. The method combines semantic raster alignment, relational graph evidence, feature stability and geographic distinctiveness, explicit positive/contradictory/unknown observations, and integrity-aware rejection of ambiguous fixes. Unlike a broad architecture-only proposal, this paper specifies concrete weighting and decision models and reports a reproducible synthetic proof of concept. In 220 randomized retrieval trials with rotation, scale changes, partial crops, occlusion, simulated map changes, and hard semantic decoys, a global semantic descriptor achieved 58.6\% Recall@1, while spatial semantic matching variants achieved 94.5-95.5%. Wilson 95\% intervals separate the global descriptor from the spatial variants but overlap among the spatial variants, so the experiment supports semantic geometry rather than a definitive benefit from each proposed module. The preliminary experiment does not validate real-flight navigation; rather, it demonstrates that structured semantic geometry can discriminate locations under controlled cross-view perturbations and identifies the harder aliasing, map-aging, and rejection tests required next.

</details>


### [168] [Physics-Guided Biomechanical Gait Adaptation for Humanoid Locomotion on Extreme Sloped Terrains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07830)
*Xuanyu Chen,Mohan Liu,Dengchen Mei,Zhihao Gu,Haitian Zhang,Kaimin Mao,Haiyue Zhu,Shijun Yan,Lin Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A two-stage physics-guided framework, HumoSlope, enables robust humanoid locomotion on diverse slopes via a slope-aware ZMP prior and a Biomechanical Slope Gait Adapter (BSGA) that uses privileged terrain descriptors during training, achieving blind sim-to-real traversal on outdoor grass slopes up to 32.1° (62.7% gradient).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model-free reinforcement learning often yields conservative, crouched gaits on slopes due to persistent gravitational bias; existing reward formulations struggle to enforce posture stability and efficient uphill propulsion; there is a need to decouple balance and gait optimization for slope-rich environments.

Method: Stage I introduces a slope-adaptive Zero Moment Point (ZMP) regularizer computed on the local inclined support plane to enforce terrain-consistent balance; Stage II employs Biomechanical Slope Gait Adapter (BSGA) with training-time privileged signals (macroscopic terrain descriptors) to gate soft reward priors, modulating CoM height and lower-limb coordination to promote hip-dominant uphill propulsion and knee-oriented downhill braking; the actor remains proprioceptive and relies on no online exteroceptive sensing.

Result: Extensive sim-to-real experiments show reduced posture degeneration and enable blind, continuous traversal of outdoor grass slopes up to 32.1° (62.7% gradient), validating a physics-guided approach to challenging slope adaptation.

Conclusion: The two-stage, physics-guided HumoSlope framework demonstrates improved slope robustness by separating balance and gait optimization and leveraging privileged, training-time signals, with successful sim-to-real transfer and no need for exteroceptive sensing during execution.

Abstract: Model-free reinforcement learning has enabled impressive humanoid locomotion; however, control on steep slopes remains largely unexplored. Unlike flat or discrete terrains, sloped terrains impose a persistent gravitational bias that demands simultaneous stability and posture control. Consequently, under generic reward formulations, policies can converge to slow, conservative low-center-of-mass (CoM) crouched gaits.
  In this work, we propose a novel two-stage physics-guided framework, dubbed HumoSlope, dedicated to robust humanoid locomotion on diverse sloped terrains. Specifically, Stage I establishes a terrain-consistent balance prior by introducing a slope-adaptive Zero Moment Point (ZMP) regularizer evaluated directly on the local inclined support plane rather than a world-horizontal reference. To prevent the resulting policy from defaulting to a crouched posture, Stage II introduces the Biomechanical Slope Gait Adapter (BSGA). Utilizing extracted macroscopic terrain descriptors as privileged, training-only signals, BSGA dynamically gates soft reward priors to modulate CoM height and lower-limb coordination based on the estimated slope geometry -- encouraging hip-dominant uphill propulsion and knee-oriented downhill braking. Crucially, the deployed actor remains entirely proprioceptive, requiring no online exteroceptive sensing.
  Extensive Sim-to-Real experiments demonstrate that our framework effectively mitigates posture degeneration and enables blind, continuous traversal of outdoor grass slopes up to 62.7% ($32.1^\circ$), validating a physics-guided approach to challenging slope terrain adaptation.

</details>


### [169] [Shift & Drift: A Zero-Shot Benchmark for Generalizable and Robust Autonomous Driving Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07844)
*Alessandro Canevaro,Hang Yu,Julian Schmidt,Peizheng Li,Silvan Lindner,Wilhelm Stork,Georg Martius,Julian Jordan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Shift & Drift introduces a dual-track benchmark to stress-test motion planners under semantic shift and state-distribution drift. It shows imitation-learning planners excel in ID but falter under semantic shifts and actuation noise, while RL-based planners degrade more gracefully, highlighting a trade-off between imitation fidelity and closed-loop resilience and providing a robust benchmark for deployment-ready evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limited generalization of closed-loop motion planners trained on large, object-level datasets to novel urban topologies and perturbations experienced in real deployments.

Method: Proposes two tracks: (1) Semantic Shift Track uses a novel conversion pipeline turning the DeepScenario Open 3D aerial dataset into the nuPlan simulation framework to enable zero-shot evaluation across 1,182 scenarios in four German cities and San Francisco, emphasizing dense pedestrian–cyclist interactions; (2) State-Distribution Drift Track injects stochastic perturbations into the ego vehicle dynamics to quantify robustness to compounding execution errors. Evaluates multiple planning paradigms (including imitation learning and reinforcement learning) on these tracks.

Result: Imitation learning methods achieve high performance on ID benchmarks but show significant failures under semantic shift, especially in pedestrian-dense environments, and exhibit persistent drift under temporally correlated actuation noise. The RL-based planner demonstrates more graceful degradation, maintaining higher safety and progress metrics across both tracks.

Conclusion: There is an empirical trade-off between imitation fidelity and closed-loop resilience. The proposed benchmark provides a rigorous, standardized way to evaluate progress toward reliable deployment of motion planners under both semantic and state-distribution shifts.

Abstract: While closed-loop motion planners trained on large-scale, object-level datasets, e.g., nuPlan, demonstrate strong in-distribution (ID) performance, their generalization to novel urban topologies and recovery mechanisms following execution perturbations remain under-explored. To address this, we present Shift & Drift, a novel dual-track benchmark designed to rigorously stress-test motion planners across two critical axes of distribution shift: (1) The Semantic Shift Track leverages a novel conversion pipeline that transforms the aerial, DeepScenario Open 3D dataset into the nuPlan simulation framework. This enables zero-shot evaluation of planners trained on North American and Singaporean data against 1,182 scenarios spanning four German cities and the US city of San Francisco featuring dense pedestrian-cyclist interactions. (2) The State-Distribution Drift Track injects stochastic perturbations into the ego vehicle's dynamics to quantify robustness against compounding execution errors. Based on this, we systematically evaluate the failure modes of diverse planning paradigms under semantic and state-distribution shifts. While imitation learning methods achieve high scores in ID benchmarks, they exhibit significant failures under semantic shift, particularly in pedestrian-dense environments, and suffer from persistent drift when subjected to temporally correlated actuation noise. In contrast, the evaluated reinforcement-learning-based planner demonstrates more graceful degradation, maintaining higher safety and progress metrics across both tracks. Our findings reveal an empirical trade-off between imitation fidelity and closed-loop resilience, providing the community with a rigorous benchmark to evaluate progress toward reliable deployment.

</details>


### [170] [STEMbot: A Compliant Robot for Under-Canopy Plant Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07873)
*Zachary Charlick,Nilay Roy Choudhury,Haoyu Ma,Xiaonan Huang,Dmitry Berenson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A miniature climbing robot, STEMbot, combines PIN-SLAM with a semantic OTree and a manifold-constrained A* planner with ray-tracing to enable autonomous, branch-aware navigation under plant canopies for early pest detection; hardware validation shows high geometric fidelity and robust odometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pest monitoring is labor-intensive; many pests inhabit leaf undersides or stems, making early detection difficult with drones/rovers. A small, perception-capable climber that can traverse branches could enable earlier intervention.

Method: Integrates a fully geometric PIN-SLAM pipeline with a semantic Octree for mapping while climbing; uses a manifold-constrained A* planner with ray-tracing for branch-aware traversal and occlusion-aware goal specification. Hardware experiments validate traversal on stems 7–33 mm and navigation across four plant specimens.

Result: Geometric reconstructions achieve an average Chamfer distance < 1 cm versus offline photogrammetry; maintains globally consistent odometry enabling autonomous navigation; successful traversal and mapping demonstrated on four plant specimens.

Conclusion: STEMbot demonstrates robust, perception-enabled climbing under canopies with accurate mapping and branch-aware navigation, enabling potential for early pest detection. The results support feasibility of autonomous under-canopy monitoring in small-to-medium stem architectures.

Abstract: The scalability of organic agriculture is partially limited by the labor costs associated with monitoring for pests. While drones and rovers are well-suited for agricultural monitoring from above or next to plants, many pests live on the underside of leaves or on plant stems, making them detectable only after they have caused significant damage. To enable early pest detection we present STEMbot, a miniature climbing robot system designed for autonomous navigation under plant canopies. Unlike existing climbing platforms that lack on-board perception or are restricted to unbranched vertical trunks, STEMbot integrates a fully geometric PIN-SLAM pipeline with a semantic OcTree to achieve robust localization and mapping while climbing the plant. To plan STEMbot's motion we propose a manifold-constrained A* planner along with ray-tracing goal specification to enable branch-aware traversal and the inspection of occluded targets. We validate our system through hardware experiments, demonstrating reliable traversal of stems ranging from 7-33mm and autonomous navigation across four distinct plant specimens. Quantitative evaluations show that our system achieves high-fidelity geometric reconstructions with an average Chamfer distance of less than 1cm relative to an offline photogrammetry baseline, confirming that STEMbot maintains the globally consistent odometry needed for autonomous navigation.

</details>


### [171] [Time-to-Collision Based Dynamic Obstacle Avoidance Using Pretrained Vision Models for Robots in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07885)
*Erik Jagnandan,Mulugeta Haile,Gregory Barber,Pratik Chaudhari*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Data-efficient, interpretable vision-based dynamic obstacle avoidance for outdoor robots using monocular depth and multi-frame keypoint tracking without training; achieves meaningful TTC-based responses and evasive actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the need for large robot-specific training data and sim-to-real gaps in dynamic obstacle avoidance for unstructured outdoor environments, while maintaining interpretability.

Method: Use UniDepth to generate dense depth from RGB; extend SuperPoint/SuperGlue to track keypoints over long sequences; triangulate 3D keypoints with camera intrinsics and depth; run bundle adjustment initialized from 3D keypoints; compute per-keypoint time-to-collision; select a 2D ground-plane motion primitive to evade the closest high-TTC-threat keypoint.

Result: On M3ED real-world data: precision 0.49, recall 0.38 for TTC<1s; correct evasive motion direction in 84% of true positives; detects TTC<1s for 20 of 22 unique obstacles; requires only 74 seconds of data for hyperparameters; no model training needed.

Conclusion: Demonstrates data efficiency, interpretability, and generalizability across obstacle types; avoids sim-to-real transfer and end-to-end learning while delivering practical dynamic obstacle avoidance.

Abstract: Dynamic obstacle avoidance in unstructured outdoor environments remains a critical challenge for autonomous mobile robots, particularly when large-scale robot-specific training data and simulation-based policies are impractical. We present a data-efficient, interpretable method for vision-based dynamic obstacle avoidance that operates entirely on real-world data, avoiding the sim-to-real transfer problem inherent in simulation-trained policies. Our approach leverages UniDepth, a large pretrained monocular depth estimation model, to produce dense depth maps from RGB video without requiring stereo cameras or LiDAR at inference time. Dynamic obstacle avoidance is achieved by extending the SuperPoint and SuperGlue feature correspondence pipeline to track keypoints across long frame sequences, projecting their 2D pixel-space positions into 3D using camera intrinsics and predicted depth, running bundle adjustment initialized from these 3D keypoints, and computing per-keypoint time-to-collision (TTC). A 2D motion primitive in the ground plane is then selected to move the robot away from the closest point of approach of the minimum-TTC keypoint. Evaluated on real-world data from the M3ED dataset, our pipeline achieves a precision of 0.49 and a recall of 0.38 in identifying frames with a ground truth TTC below 1 second, and correctly generates the evasive motion direction in 84\% of true positive detections. Crucially, it detects at least one frame with TTC less than 1 second for 20 out of 22 unique physical obstacles present in our test sequences. Unlike end-to-end learned methods that demand thousands of hours of robot-specific training data, our approach eliminates model training entirely, requiring only 74 seconds of data for hyperparameter tuning. This demonstrates exceptional data efficiency while preserving interpretable and generalizable behavior across diverse obstacle types.

</details>


### [172] [Monocular Vision Based Control Framework for Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07897)
*Shail Jadav,Dongheui Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A single RGB-based grasping framework unifies soft and rigid object grasping via open-vocabulary detection, segmentation, tracking, depth estimation, and a language-driven stiffness prior; deformation is captured with a Procrustes-based metric, while rigid objects use scaled track-point distances; validated on a Franka Emika arm across soft/rigid items with visual feedback alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve robust grasping of objects with widely varying mechanical properties in unstructured environments using a sensor-efficient, single pipeline without tactile sensing or object-specific models.

Method: A monocular, RGB-only pipeline integrating open-vocabulary object detection, segmentation, boundary-aware point assignment, real-time tracking, and monocular depth estimation. A language-based stiffness estimator predicts object compliance from semantic description to guide grasp strategy. For deformables, Procrustes-based dissimilarity from tracked keypoints acts as a deformation proxy; for rigid objects, gripper width is adjusted by scaling distances between tracked points. The controller uses a position-controlled gripper with visual feedback.

Result: Real-world pick-and-place experiments on a Franka Emika Research 3 using lettuce, fresh mozzarella, croissants, paper towels, and hard plastic bottles show stable grasping across soft and rigid objects using only visual input, demonstrating a practical, generalizable approach for food handling and household manipulation.

Conclusion: The study presents a sensor-efficient, generalizable, RGB-only grasping framework capable of handling objects with diverse mechanical properties within a unified control pipeline, reducing reliance on tactile sensing and object-specific models.

Abstract: Grasping in unstructured environments requires handling objects with widely different mechanical properties, from soft and deformable items to rigid everyday objects. Most existing approaches address these categories separately and often rely on tactile sensing, object-specific models, or specialized grippers. In this paper, we present a unified monocular vision-based grasping framework that targets both soft and rigid objects within a single control pipeline, using only RGB input and a position-controlled gripper. The proposed system combines open-vocabulary object detection, image segmentation, boundary-aware point assignment, real-time point tracking, and monocular depth estimation to recover object motion and geometry from visual observations. A key component of the framework is a language-based stiffness estimation model that infers an object's expected compliance from its semantic description and provides an object-level prior for selecting the grasping strategy before contact. For deformable objects, grasp adaptation is governed by a Procrustes-based dissimilarity measure computed from tracked keypoints, which acts as a visual proxy for deformation. For rigid objects, the gripper width is regulated through the scaling of tracked point distances. We validate the proposed method in real-world pick-and-place experiments on a Franka Emika Research 3 arm using objects with substantially different mechanical properties, including lettuce, fresh mozzarella cheese, croissants, paper towels, and hard plastic bottles. Results demonstrate that the framework achieves stable grasping across both soft and rigid objects using visual feedback alone, highlighting a practical, sensor-efficient, and generalizable approach for food handling and household manipulation.

</details>


### [173] [Towards Soft Robotic Exogloves for Musculoskeletal Manipulation to Reduce Pain and Spasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07958)
*Antonia Salluce,Maeryn Erdheim,Gailen Davis,Lauren H. Sullivan,Max-William Kanz,Jacqueline Libby*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Soft pneumatic exogloves that both enhance hand mobility and provide massage-like compression to relax spastic muscles, using personalized, conformal soft actuators (dorsal, ventral, palmar) tested via finite element modeling and pilot wearability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the dual clinical need of improving mobility and reducing pain/spasticity in the hand, a gap in current solutions that typically target either mobility or therapy but not both.

Method: Design and fabrication of novel soft pneumatic actuators (dorsal, ventral, palmar) tailored to an individual’s hand topology; 3D modeling and finite element analysis under 100 and 200 kPa; optimization to minimize stress; stereolithography printing; assembly into a wearable exogloves; pilot wearability test.

Result: Actuators customized for full conformal contact and optimized force distribution; dorsal actuator achieved full contact and maximal force; ventral actuator compacted for hyperflexed fingers; palmar actuator printed with complex geometry; glove worn by a pilot user validating comfort and initial effectiveness.

Conclusion: Preliminary study demonstrates feasibility of a soft exoglove that simultaneously aids mobility and provides compression therapy; promising for personalized rehabilitation, with future work needed on quantitative performance metrics, safety, and broader clinical testing.

Abstract: Hand spasticity and resulting pain affect 12 million people worldwide, including stroke survivors, arthritis patients, and those with other muscle and nerve deficiencies. Soft robotic exogloves are being introduced to help patients enhance mobility or manage pain; however, there are no current solutions that address both pain and mobility. We present preliminary development of a soft robotic exoglove that both aids in mobility and administers massage-like compression to relax spastic muscles. The glove consists of soft pneumatic actuators that are personalized to an individual's hand topology and kinematics, allowing for optimal conformability and targeted mobility. Novel soft actuators were designed, analyzed, fabricated, assembled into an exoglove, and experimentally tested. Actuators were 3D modeled and analyzed with finite element modeling under pressures of 100 and 200 kPa. Geometries were optimized to minimize stress before fabrication and testing. A dorsal finger actuator was successfully customized to a participant's hand topology, providing full conformal contact and maximal force distribution. A ventral finger actuator was successfully fabricated that can be drastically compressed in size to fit into the tight space of a hyperflexed spastic finger. A palmar actuator was successfully printed with stereolithography, showing potential for 3D-printed soft actuators with more complex geometries. The glove was assembled and successfully worn by a pilot user to validate initial findings in comfort and effectiveness.

</details>


### [174] [Soft Robotic Exogloves for Dexterous Mobility -- Towards Personalized Rehabilitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07968)
*Paul Dela Cruz,Mostafa Mo. Massoud,Jacqueline Libby*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Personalized pneumatically-actuated soft exogloves fitted via hand-specific topology scans enable precise anatomical conformity, improved pHRI modeling, and targeted MCP/PIP motion for rehabilitation and dexterous manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standardized hand measurements for soft exogloves fail to accommodate individual anatomy, limiting fit, comfort, and dexterous control.

Method: Use topology-based hand scans to create a silicone-molded, personalized exoglove; perform finite element analysis of pHRI with a simplified personalized finger model; conduct pneumatic pressure control tests for static and dynamic finger flexion; compare multiple designs emphasizing a strain-limiting layer.

Result: Topological scans enable precise hand tailoring; personalized pHRI analysis indicates controllable contact forces; pneumatic actuation achieves accurate MCP and PIP motion with some non-ideal proximal compression; relaxing the strain-limiting layer improves actuator-to-finger alignment.

Conclusion: Personalization in structural conformability, joint topology, pHRI modeling, and time-dependent actuation profiles provides a foundation for optimized exoglove design to support dexterous manipulation and neuromuscular rehabilitation.

Abstract: Soft robotic exogloves can provide hand rehabilitation and assistance. Fitting these gloves often relies on standardized measurements not tailored to the individual, limiting their effectiveness, especially for fine articulation necessary for dexterous manipulation. We present the design, fabrication, modeling, and testing of a personalized pneumatically-actuated soft robotic exoglove. The glove was fit to a user's hand with topological scans and fabricated with silicone mold casting. Finite element analysis (FEA) was performed to evaluate actuator bending and forces from physical human-robot interaction (pHRI) between an actuator and a simplified personalized biomechanical finger model. Pneumatic pressure control experiments were conducted to flex the user's finger with static and dynamic references. Fabrication results show that topological scans enable precise tailoring to hand anatomy. Simulations showed that anatomical personalization enables analysis of pHRI contact forces, and results indicate sufficient joint mobilization with non-ideal compression on the proximal phalanx. Pneumatic testing indicates that pressure control allows accurate and targeted mobility of the metacarpophalangeal (MCP) and proximal interphalangeal (PIP) joints with intrinsic stiffness. Testing of multiple designs showed that relaxing the strain-limiting layer improves actuator-to-finger joint alignment during actuation. This work presents personalization to the human hand in structural conformability, joint topology, modeling of pHRI contact, and time-dependent actuation-deformation profiles. This lays a groundwork for informing exoglove design optimization to enable assistance in dexterous manipulation and neuromuscular rehabilitation of fine motor skills.

</details>


### [175] [In vivo feasibility study of humanoid robots in surgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07972)
*Zekai Liang,Nikita Thareja,Peihan Zhang,Calvin Joyce,Soofiyan Atar,Florian Richter,Garth Jacobsen,Shanglei Liu,Ryan Broderick,Michael Yip*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A concise summary: The paper evaluates humanoid robots for laparoscopic surgery by building a humanoid-based teleoperation framework with general instruments and testing it through benchtop, dry-lab, and in vivo porcine studies to assess feasibility, performance, and readiness relative to established platforms, highlighting promise and technical challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address healthcare staffing pressures by exploring embodied humanoid robots for surgical tasks, assessing whether they can meet precision, control, and safety requirements of minimally invasive surgery.

Method: Develop a humanoid-based laparoscopic teleoperation framework using general-purpose instruments; perform benchtop characterization; conduct dry-laboratory user studies across diverse surgical experience levels; and perform in vivo porcine studies to evaluate capability and safety.

Result: Provide quantitative and qualitative assessment of technical feasibility, task performance, and clinical readiness; compare against established surgical platforms; identify key limitations and technical gaps that must be addressed for clinical deployment.

Conclusion: Humanoids show potential to assist in surgical tasks but face substantial technical challenges; with further development they may augment or complement traditional MIS platforms, yet clinical deployment requires overcoming safety, precision, and control hurdles.

Abstract: Recent advances in actuation, control and learning have rapidly pushed humanoid robots from a distant vision towards near-term real-world deployment. Healthcare is a particularly pressing domain, in which staffing shortages and increasing care demand are widening the gap between clinical workload and available skilled labour. Although current automation has largely focused on digital and logistical tasks, much hospital work remains embodied, requiring mobility, manipulation and safe interaction in human-designed environments. Humanoid form factors offer unique potential, particularly for assisting with surgical tasks. Traditionally, robotic systems for surgery are purpose-built platforms such as Intuitive Surgical's da Vinci Surgical System, and it remains unclear how close current humanoid systems are to meeting the precision, control and safety requirements of minimally invasive surgery. Here we present a systematic evaluation of contemporary humanoid technology for laparoscopic surgical tasks. We develop a humanoid-based laparoscopic teleoperation framework using general-purpose instruments and assess its abilities through benchtop characterization, dry-laboratory user studies spanning diverse surgical experience levels and in vivo porcine studies. Across these evaluations, we quantify technical feasibility, task performance and clinical readiness relative to established surgical platforms. Together, our study provides an evidence-based assessment of current humanoid abilities and limitations for surgical applications, highlighting both their promise and key technical challenges that must be addressed before clinical deployment.

</details>


### [176] [D-CLIPSE: Distributed Consensus-based Localization with Passive Listening on Shared State Exchange](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07995)
*Kyle Biron-Gricken,James Richard Forbes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A consensus-based distributed filter for multi-robot localization that exchanges preintegrated odometry and select shared states achieves near-centralized accuracy and improved consistency with lower communication burden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Centralized fusion yields optimal localization but is impractical for multi-robot systems due to hardware, communication, and compute constraints; existing distributed filters often sacrifice consistency and efficiency.

Method: A consistent, communication-efficient, consensus-based distributed filtering framework where each robot runs a local filter and exchanges preintegrated odometry and relevant shared states with neighboring robots; consensus mechanisms fuse information to maintain global consistency while reducing bandwidth.

Result: Validation in both simulations and real experiments shows near-centralized localization accuracy and improved consistency compared with state-of-the-art decentralized approaches, indicating practical viability.

Conclusion: The proposed framework delivers scalable, consistent, near-centralized multi-robot localization in a distributed setting by sharing compact preintegrated measurements and states, enabling reliable downstream planning and control.

Abstract: Multi-robot localization that is accurate and consistent is imperative for downstream tasks such as planning and control. Centralized filtering approaches optimally fuse all available sensor measurements of the team. However, a centralized solution is rarely implementable due to hardware, communication, and computational constraints. Distributed approaches deploy a filter on each robot to estimate their own state and neighbours' states using inter-robot communication. This paper proposes a consistent, communication-efficient, and consensus-based distributed filtering framework that shares both preintegrated odometry and relevant shared states among communicating robots. The proposed method is validated in simulated and experimental scenarios, showing near centralized performance in accuracy, and especially in consistency, compared to the current state-of-the-art decentralized approach.

</details>


### [177] [Factors Influencing Conversational Engagement in Robot-Delivered Individual Cognitive Stimulation Therapy (iCST) for Dementia in Home Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07998)
*Emmanuel Akinrintoyo,Nicole Salomons*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Robot-delivered iCST in home settings shows personalized prompts increase engagement; engagement declines within sessions due to fatigue; first-session metrics predict long-term participation; living situation modulates engagement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand conversational dynamics in robot-assisted cognitive stimulation therapy for dementia, addressing gaps in home-based, long-term interaction patterns and design implications for adaptive robots.

Method: In-home deployment of Co-STAR with eight people with dementia for one week; 30-minute CST sessions; analysis of conversational metrics (words per turn, speech production rate, response duration, response latency, self-referential language); compare personalized vs generic prompts; examine interaction phases and participant characteristics (e.g., living situation).

Result: Three interactional properties identified: (1) personalized prompts increase response duration, self-referential language, and overall engagement; (2) within-session decline in verbal output and autobiographical engagement during later phases, suggesting cognitive fatigue; (3) first-session metrics predict long-term participation; living situation influences engagement patterns.

Conclusion: Findings provide empirical insights into factors shaping conversational engagement in robot-delivered iCST and inform the design of adaptive conversational robots for dementia therapy in home settings.

Abstract: Social robots offer a promising means of supporting cognitive therapies for dementia care by guiding structured conversation and therapeutic activities. However, little is known about the conversational dynamics that emerge during robot-delivered cognitive stimulation therapy (CST) sessions. This study analysed the interaction patterns from robot-delivered individual CST (iCST) sessions conducted with people living with dementia in home settings. Our Co-STAR (Cognitive Stimulation Therapy by an Autonomous Robot) system was deployed in the homes of eight PwDs for one week, who completed 30-minute sessions. Conversational metrics, including words per turn, speech production rate, response duration, response latency, and self-referential language, were analysed to examine how conversational engagement is shaped by prompt personalisation, interaction phase, and participant characteristics. The findings highlight three key interactional properties of robot-delivered iCST. First, personalised prompts significantly increase response duration, self-referential language, and overall engagement compared to generic prompts. Second, conversational behaviour changes within sessions, with a reduction in the verbal output and autobiographical engagement observed during later interaction phases, which suggests cognitive fatigue. Third, first-session conversational metrics were associated with long-term participation, while living situation influenced conversational engagement patterns. These findings provide empirical insights into the factors that shape conversational engagement in robot-delivered iCST. They inform the design of adaptive conversational robots for dementia therapy.

</details>


### [178] [RadLoc: Radar-based 3-DoF Global Localization via Fast, Robust, and Lightweight Spatial Descriptor Across Diverse Environmental Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08115)
*Hogyun Kim,Jiwon Choi,Jungwoo Lee,Younggun Cho*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RadLoc is a fast, robust end-to-end radar-based global localization pipeline (place recognition to 3-DoF pose) that uses 1D CA-CFAR preprocessing, a compact near-range-dominant descriptor, and hierarchical coarse-to-fine retrieval, paired with phase-correlation pose estimation; claims state-of-the-art speed and smallest descriptor size across 5 datasets (15 sequences).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in radar sensor-based global localization by delivering a holistic pipeline that couples accurate place recognition with efficient 3-DoF pose estimation, enabling real-time performance in SLAM and multi-session SLAM.
