Method: Preprocessing acceleration via 1D CA-CFAR filtering; design of a compact, near-range-dominant radar descriptor for spinning radar images; hierarchical coarse-to-fine retrieval; phase-correlation-based 3-DoF pose estimation; integration into a global localization module for SLAM systems.

Result: Extensive experiments on 15 sequences across 5 datasets show robust performance, achieving the smallest descriptor size and fastest retrieval time among state-of-the-art approaches.

Conclusion: RadLoc provides a fast, robust, and lightweight end-to-end radar-based global localization solution suitable for SLAM and multi-session SLAM, validated by extensive experiments and supported by supplementary materials.

Abstract: While global localization using spinning radar has gained attention for its robustness to adverse weather and challenging environments, many studies have focused on individual components such as place recognition or pose estimation. In this paper, we take a holistic view of radar sensor-based global localization and present RadLoc, a fast, robust, and lightweight end-to-end pipeline from place recognition to 3-DoF pose estimation. RadLoc accelerates pre-processing using 1D CA-CFAR filtering and leverages the near-range dominance in spinning radar images to design a compact descriptor and an efficient hierarchical coarse-to-fine retrieval strategy. Moreover, coupled with phase correlation-based 3-DoF pose estimation, it forms a versatile global localization module applicable to SLAM and multi-session SLAM systems. Extensive experiments on 15 sequences across 5 datasets demonstrate that RadLoc achieves robust performance while maintaining the smallest descriptor size and fastest retrieval time among state-of-the-art approaches. The supplementary materials are available at https://sparolab.github.io/research/radloc/.

</details>


### [179] [X-ACTA: eXtended Analytic Center Tension distribution Algorithm for fixed and mobile cable-driven-parallel-robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08265)
*Domenico Dona',Vincenzo Di Paola,Alberto Trevisani,Matteo Zoppi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Extended Analytic Center method for CDPR tensioning outside the Wrench-Feasible Workspace (WFW) that yields differentiable, smooth tension profiles with fast real-time convergence, minimal wrench error, and Pareto-dominance over state-of-the-art methods, validated numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CDPRs operate beyond their WFW in aggressive maneuvers or in cable failure scenarios, requiring tension distributions that remain differentiable and physically feasible. Existing slack-based approaches poorly handle wrench errors outside the WFW or lose smoothness; a differentiable method capable of handling non-linear constraints outside WFW is sought.

Method: Proposes an extended Analytic Center method as a criterion for selecting cable tensions outside the WFW, preserving differentiability and incorporating non-linear constraints. The approach aims for continuous and differentiable tension profiles, fast real-time convergence to a unique solution, and minimal wrench error (confined to a negligible region of the WFW) compared to slack-based methods.

Result: Demonstrates that the proposed method achieves smoother tension profiles and reduced wrench error. Pareto dominance is shown relative to a leading state-of-the-art method. Numerical experiments validate the method’s effectiveness and real-time performance.

Conclusion: The extended Analytic Center method provides a differentiable, non-linear constrained tension selection framework for CDPRs outside the WFW, delivering fast convergence, smooth tensions, and improved wrench performance, with numerical evidence supporting its superiority over competing approaches.

Abstract: Steering Cable-Driven Parallel Robots (CDPRs) beyond their Wrench-Feasible Workspace (WFW) augments their capabilities in challenging scenarios such as during aggressive maneuvers or following a cable failure. In this context, although the determination of cable tensions is a well-studied topic, only a few approaches address these scenarios. Therefore, this paper introduces an extended version of the Analytic Center method as a criterion for selecting cable tensions outside the WFW while maintaining differentiability and including non-linear constraints. Notably, the proposed method maintains continuous and differentiable tension profiles, ensures fast real-time convergence to a unique solution, and, in contrast to other slack-based formulations, relegates wrench errors to a negligible area of the WFW. Its superiority in terms of smoothness and wrench error is confirmed via Pareto dominance with respect to the leading state-of-the-art method. Lastly, the effectiveness of the method is demonstrated through numerical experiments.

</details>


### [180] [TFP: Temporally Conditioned Memory-Fusion Policies for Visuomotor Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08283)
*Yushen Liang,Yue Peng,Baosheng Jin,Tianluo Zhang,Xinyu Zhang,Shuyi Zhou,Zhuoran Chen,Xinqi Liu,Shenji Wan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TFP introduces Temporally Conditioned Memory-Fusion Policies that integrate an episode-local belief with Liquid Time-Constant dynamics into the VLA backbone, improving success rates on LIBERO, LIBERO-plus, and MIKASA diagnostics under occlusion and stage-dependent tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reactive VLA policies falter on stage-dependent manipulation where visually similar states require different actions depending on task progress; need memory and dynamics-aware belief updates.

Method: Propose TFP framework: maintain an episode-local task-progress belief with Liquid Time-Constant dynamics; inject updated belief into the flow-matching action decoder via adaptive modulation; yields action chunks conditioned on temporally accumulated context; lightweight memory-action integration into backbones.

Result: Improves average success rate from 96.9% to 98.75% on LIBERO; from 91.4% to 93.77% on LIBERO-plus; on MIKASA Touch diagnostic, up to 75.0% success; mechanistic analyses show write-gain near manipulation events ~6x larger than non-event phases; hidden-state interventions indicate belief modulates generated action chunks; uses a 3.3B-parameter model.

Conclusion: Compact, event-sensitive memory dynamics can improve VLA policies under occlusion, visual perturbation, and stage-dependent task structure.

Abstract: Vision--Language--Action (VLA) policies such as $π_{0.5}$ and OpenVLA perform well on many manipulation tasks, but they are often reactive: the next action is predicted from the current observation, instruction, and proprioceptive state. This assumption breaks down in stage-dependent manipulation, where visually similar states may require different actions depending on latent task progress and previous interaction outcomes. We argue that such tasks require not only memory, but dynamics-aware belief updates: the policy should preserve task progress during stable or occluded phases and revise its belief near contact, release, or subgoal transitions. We introduce Temporally Conditioned Memory-Fusion Policies (TFP), a lightweight memory-action framework for VLA backbones. TFP maintains an episode-local task-progress belief with Liquid Time-Constant dynamics and injects the updated belief directly into the flow-matching action decoder through adaptive modulation. This lets temporally accumulated context shape the generated action chunk, rather than serving only as passive history context. With a 3.3B-parameter model, TFP improves the average success rate from \(96.9\%\) to \(98.75\%\) on LIBERO and from \(91.4\%\) to \(93.77\%\) on LIBERO-plus. On the memory-focused MIKASA ShellGameTouch diagnostic, TFP achieves success up to \(75.0\%\). Mechanistic analyses show that write-gain changes near manipulation events are about \(6\times\) larger than far non-event phases, and hidden-state interventions show that the belief causally modulates generated action chunks. These results suggest that compact, event-sensitive memory dynamics can improve VLA policies under occlusion, visual perturbation, and stage-dependent task structure.

</details>


### [181] [AnyDexRT: Calibration-Free Dexterous Hand Retargeting with Few-Shot Human Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08341)
*Chenxi Wang,Ying Feng,Hongjie Fang,Shangning Xia,Lixin Yang,Chuan Wen,Cewu Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Calibration-free retargeting for teleoperation across human-like dexterous hands. AnyDexRT uses self-supervised fingertip correspondence learning, few-shot human guidance to anchor task-relevant mappings, and a contact classifier to refine pinch poses, achieving more intuitive control with less manual tuning across diverse hands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective teleoperation of dexterous robotic hands hinges on kinematic retargeting. Existing approaches rely on hand-crafted objectives, precise calibration, or global shape alignment between human and robot hand spaces, making them brittle and sensitive to hand-specific tuning across different hand designs.

Method: (1) Self-supervised fingertip correspondence learning to establish hand-to-robot fingertip mappings without full supervision. (2) Few-shot human guidance to anchor the mapping in task-relevant regions, reducing reliance on global alignment. (3) A contact classifier to refine pinch-related poses, improving manipulation realism.

Result: Experiments across diverse dexterous hands and real-world teleoperation tasks show improved retargeting quality, reduced manual tuning, and more intuitive and efficient control compared with prior retargeting methods. Project website provided.

Conclusion: Calibration-free, more robust and intuitive retargeting for dexterous teleoperation across multiple hands. AnyDexRT reduces hand-tuning requirements and enhances cross-hand generalization, demonstrating practical applicability.

Abstract: Teleoperation is a key interface for controlling dexterous robotic hands and collecting demonstrations for imitation learning. Its effectiveness largely depends on kinematic retargeting, which maps operator hand motions to feasible and intuitive robot hand motions. Existing methods often require hand-crafted objectives, precise calibration, or global shape matching between human and robot hand spaces, making them sensitive to hand-specific tuning and less reliable across different dexterous hands. We propose AnyDexRT, a calibration-free retargeting method for intuitive dexterous teleoperation across human-like dexterous hands. AnyDexRT combines self-supervised fingertip correspondence learning with few-shot human guidance to anchor the mapping in task-relevant regions, and further refines pinch-related poses using a contact classifier. Experiments on diverse dexterous hands and real-world teleoperation tasks show that AnyDexRT improves retargeting quality, reduces manual tuning, and provides more intuitive and efficient control than prior retargeting methods. Project website: https://chenxi-wang.github.io/projects/anydexrt

</details>


### [182] [SkillPlug: Unsupervised Skill Mining for Few-Shot Adaptation in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08354)
*Zi-han Ding,Ziwei Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SkillPlug introduces a plug-in skill-learning module that mines a transferable library of behavior primitives from multi-task demonstrations and uses a lightweight router/head to adapt to new tasks without retraining the whole policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data-inefficiency and lack of modular reuse in end-to-end visuomotor policies; enable rapid adaptation across diverse manipulation tasks via reusable skills.

Method: Add a skill-conditioning module to existing visuomotor policies; mine a shared, compact set of skill primitives through self-supervised objectives from multi-task demonstrations; after mining, freeze skills and train lightweight router and action head for new tasks.

Result: Empirical evaluation on two simulation benchmarks and a real robot shows that transferred skills improve multi-task performance and few-shot adaptation.

Conclusion: SkillPlug provides scalable, data-efficient generalization by mining reusable skills that can be composed and specialized with minimal retraining.

Abstract: Learning transferable visuomotor imitation policies that generalize across diverse manipulation tasks and adapt rapidly to new tasks from only a handful of demonstrations remains challenging. Most modern policies are trained end-to-end to map observations directly to low-level actions, offering little explicit structure for reusing and recombining behaviors across tasks and making transfer data-inefficient under limited supervision. We propose SkillPlug, a plug-in framework that augments an existing visuomotor policy with a skill-conditioning module and mines a shared, transferable skill library from raw multi-task demonstrations. SkillPlug learns skills via self-supervised objectives that promote compact, reusable, and non-redundant behavior-level primitives, forming a task-shared prior for compositional control. After skill mining, we keep the learned skills fixed and specialize to unseen tasks by fine-tuning only lightweight router and action head, enabling efficient adaptation without full end-to-end retraining. We evaluate SkillPlug on two simulation benchmarks and on a real robot, and observe that the mined transferable skills consistently improve both multi-task performance and few-shot adaptation. Overall, SkillPlug offers a scalable way to mine reusable skills that improve data-efficient generalization in robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [183] [FSD-VLN: Fast-Slow Dual-System Modeling for Aerial Long-Horizon Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08359)
*Xueke Zhu,Qingyan Meng,Liutao Yu,Wei Zhang,Zhengyu Ma,Huihui Zhou,Yonghong Tian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FSD-VLN introduces a fast-slow dual-system for UAV vision-language navigation: a slow semantic-prior stream from pretrained multimodal models and a fast diffusion-transformer stream for cross-temporal action distributions, coupled with a time-aware optimizer. It achieves ~2x better unseen-scene success and >50% reductions in action latency and total runtime, illustrating the value of decoupled semantic reasoning for long-horizon aerial VLN.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate misalignment between high-level semantic understanding and low-level action generation in VLN for UAVs, which leads to jittery trajectories and high decision latency in long-horizon flights.

Method: Two asynchronous branches: (1) slow stream extracting stable semantic priors from pretrained vision-language models; (2) fast stream using a Diffusion Transformer (DiT) to model cross-temporal action distributions and produce stable flight commands; plus a time-aware adaptive optimizer to stabilize long-sequence training. Evaluated in large-scale low-altitude simulations on unseen scenes.

Result: Improved unseen-scene navigation success by up to 2x over SOTA; single-action inference delay and total task runtime reduced by over 50%.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the efficacy of decoupled semantic-control modeling for long-horizon aerial VLN and offers a practical architectural paradigm for robust, low-latency UAV navigation in unknown environments.

Abstract: Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) enables UAV autonomous navigation in unknown environments by mapping language instructions to real-time visual inputs. Compared with GPS-dependent or pre-programmed navigation, VLN supports intuitive human-machine interaction and stronger environmental adaptability, requiring tight integration of high-level semantic reasoning and low-latency flight control.Existing methods suffer from structural misalignment between global multimodal understanding and sequential action generation, causing jittery trajectories and severe decision latency for long-horizon aerial navigation. To solve this issue, we propose FSD-VLN, a fast-slow dual-system architecture disentangling semantic reasoning and low-latency flight command generation.The framework has two asynchronous branches: a slow stream extracting stable semantic priors from pre-trained vision-language models, and a Diffusion Transformer (DiT) fast stream modeling cross-temporal action distributions to produce consistent flight outputs. We further introduce a time-aware adaptive optimizer to stabilize long-sequence training and reduce gradient oscillation.Large-scale low-altitude simulation experiments show FSD-VLN achieves up to 2X higher navigation success rates on unseen scenes than SOTA methods, while cutting single-action inference delay and total task runtime by over 50%. Our work validates the benefit of decoupled semantic-control modeling and provides a practical paradigm for long-horizon aerial VLN.

</details>


### [184] [On Exploring Input Resolution Scaling For Anytime LiDAR Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08391)
*Ahmet Soyyigit,Shuochao Yao,Heechul Yun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Single-model multi-resolution inference with a deadline-aware scheduler enables anytime LiDAR-based 3D object detection, achieving memory efficiency and outperforming prior approaches on nuScenes, with collision-free simulation results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic cyber-physical systems require adjusting latency and accuracy on the fly. LiDAR-based 3D detection faces irregular data and resource constraints; prior solutions often use multiple models or fixed budgets.

Method: A single DNN supports multi-resolution inference for pillar/voxel representations; input resolution is dynamically scaled at runtime; a deadline-aware scheduler predicts per-resolution runtimes to pick the highest feasible resolution under the time budget.

Result: On nuScenes, the approach significantly outperforms existing anytime methods for LiDAR object detection; in simulated autonomous driving, it enables collision-free navigation and reduces stalls due to environmental complexity.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effective, memory-efficient anytime computing for LiDAR detection with a single model; the deadline-aware scheduler enables timely high-resolution inference; applicable to real-time CPS; future work could refine runtime prediction and extend to other modalities or datasets.

Abstract: Making tradeoffs between execution latency and result utility (i.e., anytime computing) for adapting to dynamic operational requirements has been shown to enhance the performance of cyber-physical systems. In this work, we focus on enabling anytime computing for deep neural networks (DNNs) that process LiDAR point clouds for 3D object detection. We propose a novel method that enables multi-resolution inference for models that process point clouds as pillars or voxels, allowing the input to be dynamically scaled and processed at the resolution needed to meet timing requirements. Importantly, our memory-efficient approach requires the deployment of only a single DNN model, avoiding the need to deploy multiple models, each trained for a different input resolution. We also introduce a deadline-aware scheduler that selects the highest possible resolution for any given input by accurately predicting the execution time for all possible resolutions at runtime, which is challenging due to the irregularity of LiDAR point clouds. Experimental results on the nuScenes autonomous driving dataset demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing anytime computing approaches for LiDAR object detection. Finally, we deploy our approach in a simulated autonomous driving system, where it consistently enables collision-free navigation while avoiding unnecessary stalls caused by environmental complexity.

</details>


### [185] [EgoWAM: World Action Models Beyond Pixels with In-the-Wild Egocentric Human Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08436)
*Baoyu Li,Xinchen Yin,Mengying Lin,Yixin Zhang,Danfei Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WAMs with targeted world-prediction signals improve human-to-robot transfer from egocentric data; EgoWAM shows Pixel is weak, while DINO and 3D flow provide strong gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address transferability gaps in imitation from egocentric human data by learning world representations that abstract appearance, capture agent-invariant physics, and separate camera motion from environment changes.

Method: EgoWAM fixes the policy backbone, action head, and data mix, while varying only the world prediction target (Pixel, DINO, 3D flow) in a controlled co-training setup; evaluated on three real-world bimanual tasks.

Result: DINO-based world targets improve out-of-distribution generalization by up to 4x; 3D flow improves in-domain performance by 20-30%; Pixel-based prediction transfers weakly; overall WAM co-training scales better with wild egocentric data than behavior cloning.

Conclusion: Using an abstract, physically grounded world target that decouples camera motion from environment changes enables more effective human-to-robot transfer; EgoWAM demonstrates that co-training with such world models outperforms standard behavior cloning on egocentric data.

Abstract: Egocentric human data offers scalable supervision for robot manipulation. However, behavior cloning entangles transferable content like objects, scenes, and task semantics, with non-transferable factors like human morphology, head motion, and behavioral style. We study whether World Action Models (WAMs) provide a better training signal by requiring policies to predict not only actions, but also how the scene evolves. The central question is what world representation best enables human-to-robot transfer. We hypothesize that an effective world target should abstract appearance, capture agent-invariant physical effects, and separate camera motion from environment change. We introduce EgoWAM, a controlled human-robot co-training framework that fixes the policy backbone, action head, and data mixture while varying only the world prediction target, comparing Pixel, DINO, and 3D motion flow. Across three real-world bimanual tasks, WAM co-training scales more effectively with in-the-wild egocentric human data than behavior cloning. Pixel-based prediction transfers weakly, while DINO and 3D flow yield substantial gains: DINO improves out-of-distribution object and scene generalization by up to 4x, and 3D flow improves in-domain performance by 20-30%. More details: https://gatech-rl2.github.io/egowam.github.io

</details>


### [186] [Harness VLA: Steering Frozen VLAs into Reliable Manipulation Primitives via Memory-Guided Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08448)
*Yixian Zhang,Huanming Zhang,Feng Gao,Xiao Li,Zhihao Liu,Chunyang Zhu,Jiaxing Qiu,Yuchen Yan,Jiyuan Liu,Wenhao Tang,Zhengru Fang,Yi Nie,Changxu Wei,Yu Wang,Wenbo Ding,Chao Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A memory-augmented harness combines a frozen vision-language-action primitive with a fixed library of analytic primitives via planning to extend VLA robustness to perturbations without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end VLA models struggle under deployment perturbations; semantic reasoning from LLMs helps but cannot handle irregular grasps and articulated-object interaction alone. A hybrid approach can leverage stable primitives while learning task-specific ranges.

Method: Freeze a VLA as a contact-rich primitive and compose it with a small fixed library of analytic primitives (grounding, staging, transport, navigation, release). The system learns the operating range of these fixed primitives from task traces, global success rules, and failure models. The planner handles semantic re-grounding, non-contact execution, and VLA re-staging, while the VLA handles local contact-rich phases. No VLA fine-tuning.

Result: Significant improvements over strong baselines across perturbed domains: +38.6 percentage points on LIBERO-Pro and +25.4 on RoboCasa365; 58.4% on RoboTwin C2R. Demonstrated on perturbed tabletop, household kitchen, and bimanual manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Harness VLA extends pretrained VLAs beyond their original trajectory distribution without finetuning by grounding them with a memory-augmented planner and a fixed analytic primitive library, achieving robust, cross-domain manipulation.

Abstract: Language-conditioned manipulation requires both precise contact-rich control and robust reasoning over language, scenes, and long horizons. End-to-end Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models provide strong local visuomotor skills, but they are trained on in-distribution task trajectories and often fail under deployment perturbations such as semantic retargeting, goal re-binding, spatial-layout shifts, and unstable local contacts. LLM coding agents provide complementary semantic and compositional reasoning, but purely analytic primitives struggle with irregular grasping, constrained placement, and articulated-object interaction. We present Harness VLA, a memory-augmented agentic framework that exposes a frozen VLA as a retryable contact-rich primitive and composes it with a small fixed library of analytic primitives for grounding, staging, transport, navigation, and release. Rather than expanding the skill library, the harness learns the operating range of these fixed primitives from task-specific execution traces, global success rules, and failure models. By lifting semantic re-grounding, non-contact execution, and VLA re-staging to the planner while reserving the frozen VLA for local contact-rich phases, Harness VLA extends pretrained VLAs beyond their original trajectory distribution without finetuning. Across perturbed tabletop, household kitchen, and clean-to-randomized bimanual manipulation, Harness VLA improves over the strongest relevant baselines by 38.6 and 25.4 percentage points on LIBERO-Pro and RoboCasa365, respectively, and reaches 58.4% on RoboTwin C2R.

</details>


### [187] [FabriVLA: A Lightweight Vision-Language-Action Model for Precise Multi-Task Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08575)
*Shiyuan Yang,Borong Zhang,Jizheng Zhang,Zhijia Tao,Junfei Guo,Donglai Ran,Xu Bian,Qingbiao Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FabriVLA is a lightweight Vision-Language-Action model that uses a 1B-scale VLM backbone (InternVL3.5) with a flow-matching action head and shallow feature fusion, achieving strong multi-task manipulation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable strong, multi-task manipulation with compact models without relying on giant VLA backbones, addressing efficiency and deployment constraints.

Method: Integrates InternVL3.5 vision-language backbone with a flow-matching action head that employs gated self-attention over action tokens, plus shallow VLM layer fusion to enrich spatial context; trained via single-stage joint optimization from a pretrained VLM and a randomly initialized action head.

Result: On Meta-World MT50 across 50 tasks, FabriVLA achieves a tier-average success rate of 90.0%, demonstrating that a compact VLA built on a ~1B VLM can achieve strong performance without billions-parameter backbones.

Conclusion: Demonstrates viability of compact VLA architectures leveraging mid-size VLMs for robust manipulation, offering efficient deployment and competitive performance relative to larger backbones.

Abstract: We present FabriVLA, a lightweight Vision-Language-Action model for Precise Multi-Task Manipulation. FabriVLA combines an InternVL3.5 vision-language backbone with a flow-matching action head featuring gated self-attention across action tokens and shallow VLM layer fusion for enriched spatial context. The model is trained via single stage joint optimization from a pretrained VLM and randomly initialized action head. On the Meta-World MT50 benchmark spanning 50 diverse manipulation tasks, FabriVLA achieves a tier-average success rate of 90.0%, demonstrating that a compact VLA built on a 1B scale VLM can achieve strong performance without relying on multi billion parameter VLA backbones.

</details>


### [188] [A New Human-Likeness and Comfort Index for Robot Movements Along Prescribed Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08620)
*Rosanna Coccaro,Enrico Ferrentino,Antonio Parziale,Angelo Marcelli,Pasquale Chiacchio*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lognormal-based human-likeness index (HLI) is proposed to quantify how human-like robot end-effector trajectories are, aiming to predict perceived comfort in human-robot interaction. Validated across three experiments with 68 subjects, showing a consistent positive relationship between HLI and comfort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acceptance in physical human-robot interaction hinges on comfort and ergonomics, not merely visual similarity; a priori trajectory evaluation is needed to ensure comfortable motion before execution.

Method: Model human movement using lognormal time laws; define a human-likeness index (HLI) for prescribed end-effector trajectories; use HLI to evaluate trajectory generation algorithms before execution; validate with three experimental campaigns involving physical robot interaction and comfort judgments from 68 participants.

Result: A global consistent trend: higher HLI correlates with greater perceived comfort; the index reliably reflects comfort across experiments, enabling pre-execution assessment of trajectories.

Conclusion: The proposed HLI provides a practical pre-execution metric to guide trajectory generation for more comfortable HRI, with potential refinements in movement modeling and broader validation.

Abstract: As human-robot interaction rapidly spreads in numerous fields, the subject of robot acceptance gains increasing importance. Visual similarity to the human body, as occurs for humanoids, is generally not enough to ensure acceptance in physical interaction, as acceptance directly links to comfort and ergonomics, which are measured in terms of the quality of the robot movement perceived by the human. This paper discusses the connection between comfort and similarity of the robot movement to the human one. By considering the kinematic characterization of human movement, this paper focuses on the time laws of such movements, wherein the end-effector path is prescribed. Based on the lognormality principle for modeling human movements, a human-likeness index is defined and used to provide an a priori characterization of trajectories. Such an index can be used to evaluate the performance of trajectory generation algorithms in producing human-like movements before they are actually executed. For validation purposes, 68 subjects are required to judge their comfort. The results of three experimental campaigns involving a physical interaction with a robot demonstrate a globally consistent trend between the preference in terms of perceived comfort and the distribution of the suggested human-likeness index.

</details>


### [189] [Native Video-Action Pretraining for Generalizable Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08639)
*Qihang Zhang,Lin Li,Luyao Zhang,Shuai Yang,Yiming Luo,Shuaiting Li,Ruilin Wang,Junke Wang,Jiahao Shao,Gangwei Xu,Jiaming Zhou,Yishu Shen,Yudong Jin,Fangyi Xu,Shuailei Ma,Jiaqi Liao,Guanxing Lu,Zifan Shi,Yongkun Wen,Yujie Zhao,Weixuan Tang,Xinyang Wang,Chaojian Li,Jiapeng Zhu,Ka Leong Cheng,Nan Xue,Xing Zhu,Yujun Shen,Yinghao Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LingBot-VA 2.0 is an embodied video-action foundation model that uses a semantic visual-action tokenizer, causal pretraining from scratch, a sparse MoE backbone, and asynchronous inference with learned forward dynamics, achieving real-time control and few-shot generalization in manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video generative models designed for digital content are ill-suited for physical environments; there is a need for a foundation model aligned with semantics and actions to enable robust embodiment and control.

Method: Four design principles: (1) semantic visual-action tokenizer aligning visual representations with semantics and actions; (2) causal pretraining from scratch to address temporal causality and avoid catastrophic forgetting; (3) sparse MoE backbone to scale capacity while maintaining efficiency; (4) enhanced asynchronous inference predicting future latents in parallel with action execution and re-grounding via learned forward dynamics for real-time closed-loop control.

Result: Real-world deployment demonstrates robustness and few-shot generalization across complex manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: LingBot-VA 2.0 advances embodied video-action foundation models with improved instruction following, action precision, efficiency, and real-time control, showing promise for generalization in physical manipulation tasks.

Abstract: The advent of video-action models offers a promising path for robot control. Nevertheless, we argue that repurposing video generative models designed for digital content creation is inherently inadequate for physical environments. To bridge this gap, we present LingBot-VA 2.0, a video-action foundation model built from the ground up for embodiment. Four core design principles showcase its evolution from LingBot-VA. (1) Departing from traditional reconstruction-focused VAEs, we introduce a semantic visual-action tokenizer, which aligns visual representations with both semantics and actions, improving instruction following and action precision in subsequent policy learning. (2) Given the strictly causal nature of temporal dynamics, we adopt a causal pretraining paradigm, training from scratch to circumvent the catastrophic forgetting that frequently occurs when adapting bidirectional architectures. (3) To meet the demands of high-frequency inference, our model employs a sparse MoE backbone, expanding model capacity without compromising efficiency. (4) Real-time closed-loop control is realized through an enhanced asynchronous inference scheme, which predicts future latents in parallel with action execution while re-grounding each rollout on the latest observation via learned forward dynamics. Real-world deployment validates LingBot-VA 2.0 as a robust foundation model, as evidenced by its few-shot generalization across complex manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [190] [Learning Adaptive Solvers for Distributed Factor Graph Optimization on Matrix Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08735)
*Jaeho Shin,Maani Ghaffari,Yulun Tian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A learning-augmented distributed solver for factor graphs on matrix Lie groups (DeepCORD) that unrolls a Riemannian optimizer into differentiable steps and learns a self-supervised policy to adapt solver parameters across synchronous/asynchronous communication, yielding improved objective on SE(3) pose graphs and SL(4) projective submap alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing distributed solvers are brittle, rely on hand-tuned settings, and largely address rigid-body pose graphs. There is a need for generalizable optimization on matrix Lie groups with adaptive behavior to varying communication regimes.

Method: Unfold parallel and accelerated Riemannian optimization into differentiable iterations; train a self-supervised feedback policy that modulates solver parameters based on optimization phase and communication status; supports distributed optimization over matrix Lie groups under synchronous and asynchronous regimes; demonstrated on SE(3) pose graphs and SL(4) submap alignment.

Result: Outperforms existing distributed baselines on most benchmarks across realistic operating scenarios, achieving lower objective values on real-world SE(3) pose graphs and SL(4) projective submap alignment.

Conclusion: DeepCORD enables adaptive distributed optimization over matrix Lie groups, robust to communication regime and improves performance over baselines in practical distributed SLAM-like tasks.

Abstract: Modern robotic perception increasingly involves large-scale geometric optimization problems distributed across multiple robots or sessions. However, existing distributed solvers often depend on brittle hand tuning and primarily target rigid body pose graphs. To address this, we present DeepCORD, a learning-augmented framework for distributed factor graph optimization on general matrix Lie groups. By unfolding a parallel and accelerated Riemannian optimizer into differentiable iterations, DeepCORD learns a self-supervised feedback policy that dynamically adapts solver parameters according to the optimization phase and communication status. The resulting method enables adaptive distributed optimization over matrix Lie groups under both synchronous and asynchronous communication regimes. Extensive experiments on real-world $\mathrm{SE}$(3) pose graph optimization and $\mathrm{SL}$(4) projective submap alignment show that our method achieves lower objective values than existing distributed baselines on most benchmarks across realistic operating scenarios.

</details>


### [191] [ContactMimic: Humanoid Object Interaction via Contact Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08742)
*Xinyao Li,Xialin He,Runpei Dong,Saurabh Gupta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CONTACTMIMIC introduces explicit part-level contact commands alongside keypoint trajectories to enable contact controllability in manipulation tasks. It uses contact-following rewards and trajectory augmentation to decorrelate contact from geometry, achieving precise contact and controllable contact behavior in sim and sim-to-real, outperforming keypoint-only trackers on contact-relevant tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Keypoint tracking alone fails for contact-rich object interactions where physical contact drives the task outcome. A method that explicitly models and controls contact, decoupled from pose, can improve manipulation performance and safety.

Method: A learning framework that jointly tracks part-level binary contact commands with keypoint trajectories. It employs contact-following rewards and a trajectory augmentation scheme to break correlations between keypoint trajectories and contact labels, enabling decoupled contact behavior from geometry. Training and evaluation include 10 diverse human-object interaction motions in simulation and 5 motions in sim-to-real deployment.

Result: The policy achieves precise physical contact and contact controllability (ability to produce or suppress contact during deployment). It outperforms keypoint-only trackers on contact-relevant tasks, with ablations showing the necessity of trajectory augmentation. Real-world validation confirms contact controllability across five motions.

Conclusion: CONTACTMIMIC provides a robust framework for contact-aware manipulation by decoupling contact from geometry, enabling controllable contact during manipulation without task-specific rewards, validated in simulation and real-world deployments.

Abstract: Keypoint tracking alone is insufficient for object interaction tasks such as sitting on a chair, wiping a board, or pushing furniture, where the robot can reach the correct pose without making meaningful physical contact with the object. We present CONTACTMIMIC, a learning framework that tracks explicit partlevel binary contact commands alongside keypoint trajectories. CONTACTMIMIC is made possible through the use of contact-following rewards and a trajectory augmentation scheme aimed at breaking the correlations between keypoint trajectories and contact labels. The resulting policy successfully decouples contact behavior from keypoint geometry, and achieves precise physical contact as well as contact-controllability (produce or suppress contact during deployment as desired). Simulation experiments across 10 diverse human-object interaction motions confirm that CONTACTMIMIC exhibits contact controllability that enables it to complete manipulation tasks without task-specific rewards, while also outperforming keypoint-only trackers on contact-relevant tasks. Ablations confirm the necessity of the proposed trajectory augmentation scheme and sim2real deployment validates contact controllability in the real world across 5 different motions. Video results are available on https://lixinyao11.github.io/contactmimic-page/.

</details>


### [192] [DexVerse: A Modular Benchmark for Multi-Task, Multi-Embodiment Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08751)
*Yunchao Yao,Zhuxiu Xu,Tianqi Zhang,Zixian Liu,Sikai Li,Zhenyu Wei,Feng Chen,Dihong Huang,Kechang Wan,Chenyang Ma,Shuqi Zhao,Shenghua Gao,Masayoshi Tomizuka,Yi Ma,Mingyu Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexVerse is a large-scale, modular benchmark for dexterous manipulation, with 100 tasks, multiple embodiments, configurable visuals, VR teleoperation, and 3,180 multimodal demonstrations, enabling evaluation of cross-task and cross-embodiment generalization; initial benchmarks show notable generalization and visuomotor robustness challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of benchmarks that systematically test dexterous manipulation across diverse interaction modes, sensory conditions, and robot embodiments, hindering studies of cross-task and cross-embodiment generalization.

Method: DexVerse introduces 100 tasks spanning diverse manipulation skills, supports 3 robot arms and 6 dexterous hands, is extensible to new tasks/assets/embodiments, provides configurable visual variation, a VR-based teleoperation interface, and 3,180 synchronized demonstrations with proprioceptive, RGB, depth, point-cloud, and state observations; benchmarks methods (Diffusion Policy, DP3, OpenVLA, π0.5) across 19 tasks.

Result: Evaluation across 19 tasks shows substantial challenges in task generalization and visuomotor robustness, underscoring the value of DexVerse as a general-purpose dexterous manipulation testbed.

Conclusion: DexVerse offers a scalable, extensible platform for studying general-purpose dexterous manipulation and facilitates research into cross-task and cross-embodiment generalization.

Abstract: Building general-purpose dexterous manipulation policies requires benchmarks that go beyond isolated tasks to systematically evaluate policies across diverse interaction modes, sensory conditions, and robot embodiments. However, existing benchmarks remain limited in task and data diversity, embodiment coverage, or controllable visual variation, hindering studies of cross-task and cross-embodiment generalization. We present DexVerse, a large-scale and modular benchmark for dexterous manipulation. DexVerse includes 100 tasks spanning a broad range of manipulation skills, including object grasping and relocation, articulated-object interaction, functional tool use, bimanual coordination, non-prehensile control, contact-rich behaviors, multi-goal execution, and long-horizon multi-stage task completion. It supports 3 robot arms and 6 dexterous hands, and is extensible to new tasks, assets, and embodiments. To evaluate visuomotor generalization, DexVerse provides configurable visual variations in textures, background, lighting, and camera viewpoints. We further provide a VR-based teleoperation interface and 3,180 demonstrations with synchronized proprioceptive, RGB, depth, point-cloud, and state observations. We benchmark representative methods, including Diffusion Policy, DP3, OpenVLA, and $π_{0.5}$, across 19 tasks. Results reveal substantial challenges in task generalization and visuomotor robustness, establishing DexVerse as a promising testbed for general-purpose dexterous manipulation. Project page: https://ycyao216.github.io/DexVerse.site

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [193] [Context Graphs for Proactive Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07721)
*Avinash Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proactive enterprise agents using a Context Graph and Delta Detection Engine surface timely, grounded insights with high precision, significantly reducing time to surface compared to reactive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current enterprise AI for decision support is largely reactive; to unlock productivity gains, systems must proactively surface relevant information to workers before queries.

Method: Introduce a live Context Graph modeling enterprise entities, relationships, and state transitions over time; implement a Delta Detection Engine to monitor changes; develop a Proactivity Scorer to rank insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit; deploy a Surfacing Layer powered by an LLM to present ranked notifications with grounded explanations; formalize components and a unified Proactivity Score; provide an end-to-end Python implementation using NetworkX and the Anthropic Claude API.

Result: Evaluation across three enterprise scenarios (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, sales pipeline hygiene) shows Precision@5 = 0.83, false positive rate = 0.11, and mean time to surface reduced from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 seconds.

Conclusion: Context-graph-driven proactivity can markedly improve enterprise productivity by delivering timely, grounded insights; the work provides a complete end-to-end architecture and empirical validation, with implications for generalization and future enhancements.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic frameworks have advanced enterprise AI considerably, yet agents remain fundamentally reactive: they wait for a human query before acting. This paper argues that genuine enterprise productivity gains require proactive agents: systems that surface relevant, actionable information to workers before they ask. We propose the Context Graph, a live relational data structure that models enterprise entities, their relationships, and state transitions over time. Built on this graph, we define a Delta Detection Engine that continuously monitors state changes, a Proactivity Scorer that ranks candidate insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit, and a Surfacing Layer powered by an LLM that delivers ranked notifications with grounded explanations. We formalize each component, derive a unified Proactivity Score function, and provide a complete end-to-end Python implementation using NetworkX and the Anthropic Claude API. Evaluation across three generic enterprise case studies (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, and sales pipeline hygiene) demonstrates that context-graph-driven proactivity achieves Precision@5 of 0.83, a false positive rate of 0.11, and reduces mean time to surface from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 second.

</details>


### [194] [AI-integrated models for assessing agricultural resilience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07759)
*Joshua R. Waite,Dana Golden,Brett Indelicato,Kevin Camp,Mojdeh Saadati,Shannon Regan,Patrick Schnable,Baskar Ganapathysubramanian,Carlos Messina,Suzanne Thornsbury,Soumik Sarkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI-powered integration of GTAP (economic model) and APSIM (biophysical model) to assess agricultural supply chain shocks via natural-language queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agricultural supply chains are vulnerable to disruptions from coupled biophysical and economic systems; there is a need for cross-disciplinary analysis and a user-friendly, queryable decision-support tool for policymakers and market participants.

Method: Integrate GTAP and APSIM within an AI-powered tool; enable natural-language queries and responses to analyze cross-disciplinary impacts of supply-chain shocks.

Result: Not reported in the abstract; the work claims the tool enables cross-disciplinary analysis, but empirical results are not presented.

Conclusion: The framework offers an integrative, queryable decision-support platform for analyzing supply-chain disruptions and their cross-domain impacts.

Abstract: Agricultural supply chains are vulnerable to disruptions through linked biophysical and economic systems. We develop an AI-powered tool that integrates economic models (GTAP) with biophysical models (APSIM) to analyze supply chain shocks, enabling policymakers and market participants to assess cross-disciplinary impacts through queries and responses written in natural language.

</details>


### [195] [Adversarial Social Epistemology for Assemblies of Humans and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07760)
*Mihnea C. Moldoveanu,Joel A. C. Baum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces adversarial social epistemology (ASE) to model trust subversion in dense, chain-based public communication, beyond epistemic bubbles, with auditing mechanisms and inferentialist semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing accounts (epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, misinformation) do not explain how agents exploit commitments that render scaffolded assertions trustworthy; a framework is needed to diagnose, audit, and remediate breaches of trust in inferential chains.

Method: Develops ASE language and conceptual apparatus; identifies mechanisms by which agents subvert trust in testimonial-inferential chains; augments epistemic networks with inferentialist semantics; designs auditing/redress machinery for trust breaches.

Result: A conceptual toolkit for diagnosing trust subversion in scaffolded public communications; explicit mechanisms for subversion, auditing procedures, and redress strategies grounded in an inferentialist interpretative framework.

Conclusion: ASE provides a robust framework to analyze and counter adversarial manipulation of testimony-based knowledge in densely interactive settings, with practical implications for auditing and governance of epistemic networks.

Abstract: We outline an adversarial social epistemology (ASE) for densely interactive communicative landscapes in which public assertions are scaffolded by chains of testimony, inference, institutional certification, and tacit trust. In such landscapes, agents have incentives and affordances to distort, color, omit, fabricate, or strategically under-specify information for private, reputational, rhetorical, or material gains. We argue that these phenomena are not adequately captured by familiar descriptions of epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, or misinformation diffusion. What requires explanation is how communicative agents exploit the commitments and entitlements that normally make scaffolded assertions trustworthy. We provide language that delivers the requisite analysis, outline mechanisms that subvert trust in scaffolded public communications, and outline machinery for auditing and redressing trust breaches arising from subverting the auditability of inferential chains, drawing on epistemic networks, enriched with an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions.

</details>


### [196] [Aligning Clinical Needs and AI Capabilities: A Survey on LLMs for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07761)
*Qi Peng,Jiatong Li,Sirui Huang,Yiyang Jiang,Kaisong Gong,Ronger Ding,Shijie Ye,Changmeng Zheng,Yi Cai,Xiaobo Yang,Jin Huang,Xiao-Yong Wei,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of medical LLMs connecting clinical practice with computational reasoning; introduces Miller's Pyramid-based competency framework, maps deductive/inductive/abductive reasoning to medical tasks, and presents a five-level medical reasoning benchmark evaluated on 18 models; specialist models excel in diagnosis-centric tasks while general models lead in decision support and dialogue; highlights data, hallucination, and grounding challenges with directions toward safer, workflow-ready systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify how LLMs can support clinical reasoning and patient care, establish a framework linking clinical competencies with computational reasoning, and evaluate current models to identify strengths and gaps for safer deployment.

Method: Dual-view framework aligning Miller's Pyramid levels with clinical competencies; categorize reasoning patterns as deductive, inductive, abductive and map to medical goals; develop a benchmark spanning five levels of medical reasoning; evaluate 18 state-of-the-art models on the benchmark; analyze performance by model type.

Result: Specialist medical models outperform in diagnosis-centric tasks; general models perform better in decision support and dialogue; the benchmark reveals progress and remaining gaps; common issues include data limitations, hallucination, and grounding problems.

Conclusion: Progress is evident but open challenges remain; emphasis on data quality, grounding, safety, and reliability; call for workflow-ready, safer AI systems in healthcare.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as important tools in healthcare, showing growing potential for clinical reasoning and patient care. This survey examines recent progress in medical LLMs, focusing on reasoning applications and requirements. We present a dual-view approach that connects clinical practice with computational methods. On the clinical side, we establish a five-level competency scheme following Miller's Pyramid, progressing from knowledge recall to dynamic case management. On the computational side, we link deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning patterns to common medical goals and tasks. We also introduce a benchmark dataset spanning five levels of medical reasoning capability and report results on 18 state-of-the-art models, revealing that medical specialist models excel in diagnosis-centric tasks while general models lead in decision support and dialogue. We conclude by discussing current progress and open challenges, including data limitations, hallucination, and grounding issues, and outline directions toward safer, more reliable, and workflow-ready systems.

</details>


### [197] [Alignment Plausibility: A New Standard for Assuring AI in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07766)
*Gwydion Williams,Sara Zannone,Bilal A Mateen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces alignment plausibility for AI in health, a 3-level framework (value specification, training, oversight) to ensure safe, beneficial use of LLMs in mental health; presents a regulatory lens to assess trust and prevent harm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs in mental health risk being shaped by an attention economy that prioritizes engagement; current safety responses are reactive and fail to address long-term risks like dependency, boundary erosion, and distorted beliefs. A structural safety framework anchored in clinical norms is proposed.

Method: Conceptual framework aligning AI safety with clinical practice: explicit value specification, embedding values in training, and deployment oversight to detect drift and harm; defines alignment plausibility and its regulatory analogy to biological plausibility.

Result: Proposes alignment plausibility as a structured construct showing that a system's values, training, and oversight are collectively aligned with safe health outcomes; outlines how this could be demonstrated and used as a regulatory reference.

Conclusion: Alignment plausibility offers a principled regulatory construct for AI in health, enabling scrutiny of whether systems will cause no harm and deliver patient benefits, even when capable of more harmful actions.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become significant providers of mental health support, yet they remain products of an attention economy whose operational and commercial targets favour sustained engagement over the friction that effective psychological support often requires. Developers' safety responses have been largely reactive, addressing the most visible and acute harms while subtler, longer-term patterns of risk (e.g., dependency, boundary erosion, the amplification of distorted beliefs) receive less attention. We contend that making LLMs structurally safe requires alignment organised at three levels that mirror how society assures the safety of human clinical practice: 1) explicit value specification grounded in the codified normative commitments of clinical practice; 2) training that embeds those values in the model; and 3) oversight that detects drift and longer-term harm during deployment, much as clinical supervision does for human practice. Organising alignment in this way yields a construct we call alignment plausibility - a structured demonstration that a system's values, training regime, and oversight mechanisms are together consistent with safe and positive outcomes. We propose alignment plausibility as a regulatory construct (by drawing analogy to the established construct of biological plausibility) for AI in health: a principled way to argue for, or against, trust that systems are aligned to positive health outcomes, will cause no harm even where capable of doing so, and will ultimately lead to patient benefit.

</details>


### [198] [Idiobionics: The Unification of Privacy and Intelligent Robotic Prostheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07775)
*Kwesi Afari Darfoor,Patrick M. Pilarski,Bailey Kacsmar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces idiobionics, a privacy-centric research line at the intersection of intelligent bionic limbs, defining the concept, grounding it in literature, presenting preliminary adversarial risks, and outlining open research questions to guide future work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As robotic prostheses become perceptive and autonomous, privacy risks emerge from sensors and AI control. Understanding these threats is essential for user adoption and safe deployment.

Method: Conceptual proposition: define idiobionics, ground it in prior literature on privacy and wearable robotics, provide preliminary evidence of potential adversarial attacks, and curate open research questions for the field.

Result: Definition of idiobionics, a grounded theoretical framework, initial evidence hinting at privacy-attacking vectors, and a structured list of open research questions for researchers in wearable robotics and autonomous human-facing systems.

Conclusion: Idiobionics could unlock the full potential of intelligent bionic limbs by addressing privacy challenges and guiding future research and design towards user trust and adoption.

Abstract: The human body is at the center of a growing family of technologies designed to tightly and persistently couple biological and digital systems. Robotic prostheses are a representative example of this tight coupling. Also referred to as bionic limbs, robotic prostheses are devices that support people who have lost limbs in pursuing daily life activities such as walking and grasping objects. Bionic limbs are now perceptive and responsive owing to their integration with advanced sensors and artificial intelligence-based control approaches. Consequently, such robotic prostheses can now be viewed as semiautonomous wearable robotic systems that can co-adapt with their users. However, the same sensing and control advancements that increase the capability of robotic prostheses also introduce threat vectors that could be exploited by malicious entities to violate the privacy of users. To fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs, we maintain it is important to directly understand and address these privacy risks and the barriers they might present to user adoption. This paper therefore introduces a new line of inquiry we term idiobionics to holistically investigate issues at the intersection of privacy and intelligent bionic limbs. As the main contribution of this paper, we define idiobionics, ground it in related literature, and provide preliminary evidence showing and discussing potential adversarial attacks that could exploit intelligent bionic limb designs. We then contribute a curated list of open research questions within idiobionics that are relevant to researchers in wearable robotics and other human-facing autonomous systems. We expect that idiobionics research will help unlock the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices.

</details>


### [199] [Infinity-Parser2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07836)
*Zuming Huang,Jun Huang,Kexuan Ren,Baode Wang,Weizhen Li,Jianming Feng,Yu Wang,Yichen Yao,Shijun Lin,Yige Tang,Cheng Peng,Weidi Xu,Wei Chu,Yinghui Xu,Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large multimodal document parser, Infinity-Parser2, with a scalable synthesis pipeline to create Infinity-Doc-5M bilingual corpus and a multi-task reinforcement learning objective across eight tasks; two variants (Flash and Pro) for low-latency and precision; state-of-the-art results on olmOCR-Bench (87.6%) and ParseBench (74.3%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address scarcity of faithfully annotated parsing data and unify perception, structure, and reasoning in end-to-end document parsing across diverse document types and modalities, with strong generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and VQA.

Method: 1) Scalable data synthesis: controllable rendering with iterative refinement to build Infinity-Doc-5M, a bilingual Chinese/English corpus with element bounding boxes, canonical content forms, and full-page reading order. 2) Verifiable, multi-task reinforcement learning: eight co-trained objectives (document parsing, layout analysis, table parsing, math parsing, chart parsing, chemical formula parsing, document VQA, multimodal understanding) under a single optimization signal. 3) Dual-model variants: Infinity-Parser2-Flash for low latency (3.68x throughput gain vs Infinity-Parser-7B) and Infinity-Parser2-Pro for precision-critical tasks, sharing architecture.

Result: Infinity-Parser2-Pro achieves state-of-the-art 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, outperforming DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, MinerU2.5, with robust generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

Conclusion: A unified, scalable approach combining synthetic data generation and multi-task RL yields a high-performance, generalizable document parsing model, offering both speed and precision variants and strong cross-task performance.

Abstract: We present Infinity-Parser2, a large multimodal model that couples a controllable data-synthesis pipeline with multi-task reinforcement learning for end-to-end document parsing, addressing the persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated parsing corpora. Our contributions are threefold. First, we build a scalable synthesis engine, pairing a controllable rendering framework with an iterative refinement loop, and use it to construct and open-source Infinity-Doc2-5M: a 5-million-sample bilingual (Chinese/English) corpus spanning diverse document types, annotated with element bounding boxes, canonical content forms (Markdown, HTML, LaTeX, SMILES, structured charts), and full-page reading order. Second, we introduce a verifiable, multi-task reward system that enables Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives (document parsing, layout analysis, table parsing, math formula parsing, chart parsing, chemical formula parsing, document VQA, and general multimodal understanding), unifying perception, structure, and reasoning in a single optimization signal. Third, we release two variants under a shared architecture: Infinity-Parser2-Flash, optimized for low-latency inference with a $3.68\times$ throughput gain over Infinity-Parser-7B, and Infinity-Parser2-Pro, engineered for precision-critical settings. Infinity-Parser2-Pro reaches state-of-the-art 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5, with strong generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

</details>


### [200] [VectorizationLLM: Smart Vectorization Based AI Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07846)
*Ryan Duke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A specialized LLM (VectorizationLLM) built on Google open-weight LLMs to teach vectorization, time/wave analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations in MATLAB for a specific ECE course, using RAG-based instructional prompts to provide detailed explanations with examples while avoiding direct answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for guided, illustrative MATLAB education in vectorization and analysis topics; align with course objectives; enable scalable, non-direct-answer tutoring through retrieval-augmented generation.

Method: Leverages Google open-weight LLMs as the base model, integrates a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) knowledge base and system prompt architecture, and delivers responses with code, text, and images; emphasizes instructive explanations derived from in-class notes without giving direct answers.

Result: The abstract outlines a design and deployment plan rather than empirical results; expected outcomes include improved student understanding through detailed explanations and worked examples, with scalable tutoring via RAG, but no concrete metrics are reported.

Conclusion: VectorizationLLM presents a pedagogical LLM architecture for engineering education, combining open-weight LLMs with RAG to support MATLAB-based topics in vectorization and analysis within a specific course context.

Abstract: VectorizationLLM is a specialized Large Language Model based on Google open-weight LLMs. The model is designed to assist students to learn smart vectorization, time/wave vector analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations in MATLAB. The course application is CTEC 247: Applied Computational Analysis II by the Department of Electrical & Computer Engineering Technology at New York Institute of Technology Old Westbury. The LLM model is designed to be an instructive assistant, providing detailed explanations of concepts with examples from in-class notes without providing direct answers to questions. The model is designed with a RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation) knowledge base and system prompt architecture. Examples in both code, text, and images are provided in the LLM responses.

</details>


### [201] [A Graph Neural Network Model for Real-Time Gesture Recognition Based on sEMG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07850)
*Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan,Kamal Premaratne,Manohar Murthi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Graph-based sEMG gesture recognition using graph neural networks achieves near-perfect real-time accuracy on 8-channel forearm data (Myoband), with ~99% accuracy and ~48 ms latency on an M1 Pro CPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accurate and immediate hand gesture recognition for prosthetics and AR by exploiting the relational structure of forearm muscle activity; standard methods may neglect inter-muscle interactions accessible via graph representations.

Method: Construct graph representations from sEMG signals captured by an 8-electrode forearm sensor (Myoband) and apply a graph neural network to perform real-time hand gesture classification; evaluate across 8 healthy subjects.

Result: Average classification accuracy of 99% across subjects; outperforms state-of-the-art methods; mean latency for graph construction and prediction is 48 ms on a MacBook Pro M1 Pro CPU.

Conclusion: Graph-based sEMG representations with GNNs provide highly accurate, real-time hand gesture recognition, suitable for seamless control in prosthetics and augmented reality.

Abstract: For seemless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality, accurate and immediate hand gestures recognition is essential. Surface electromyography (sEMG) signals obtained from the forearm are commonly employed for this purpose. In this paper, we present a novel approach for sEMG representation that utilizes graph networks which contain information about muscle activation patterns in the forearm. Based on these graph networks, we have developed a machine learning algorithm capable of real-time hand gesture recognition using a graph neural network. The algorithm's performance was evaluated using sEMG signals acquired from myoband, which has 8 electrodes placed around the forearm, involving 8 healthy subjects. The proposed method demonstrated an average classification accuracy of 99\%, surpassing the performance of state-of-the-art techniques. The average time for both graph construction and prediction stood at 48ms utilizing a M1 pro CPU, rendering the approach well-suited for real-time applications.

</details>


### [202] [Agentic AI and Retrieval-Augmented Models in Straight-Through Underwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07858)
*Robert Richardson,Josh Meyers,Brian Hartman,David Sandberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic RAG framework for straight-through underwriting of small commercial BOPs; among three pipelines, agentic RAG best handles multi-step and missing-information scenarios, improving transparency and governance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address automation in actuarial practice amid unstructured data, heterogeneous sources, and regulated decision workflows; balance automation with transparency and human-in-the-loop governance.

Method: Experimental comparison of three pipelines in a synthetic but realistic underwriting environment: (i) single-LLM baseline, (ii) naive retrieval-augmented generation, (iii) agentic RAG combining targeted retrieval, third-party checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation; evaluation focuses on straight-through underwriting performance, especially in multi-step and missing information contexts.

Result: Agentic RAG yields best overall performance, with largest gains in multi-step and missing-information scenarios due to structured retrieval and reflection that reduce unsupported decisions.

Conclusion: Agentic architectures with retrieval, checks, and rule-based evaluation can improve transparency, auditability, and governance in regulated underwriting; supports human-in-the-loop governance and straight-through decision processes.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is beginning to reshape actuarial practice, particularly in domains that require reasoning over unstructured documents, heterogeneous data sources, and regulated decision workflows. Actuaries now face a design space that ranges from traditional rule-based automation to large language models (LLMs), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and multi-agent ``agentic'' systems that plan, retrieve, call tools, and reflect. This paper examines how these emerging architectures can support actuarial priorities such as transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance, with a focus on straight-through decision processes. To make these ideas concrete, we develop and analyze an agentic AI framework for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs). We construct a synthetic but realistic experimental environment and compare three underwriting pipelines: (i) a single-LLM baseline, (ii) a naive RAG system, and (iii) a multi-agent ``Agentic RAG'' pipeline that combines targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation. The agentic system performs best overall, with the largest gains in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection help the model avoid unsupported straight-through decisions.

</details>


### [203] [Feedback Manipulation Regularization: Enabling Offline Agent Alignment for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07859)
*Benjamin Poole,Minwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR), an algorithm-agnostic method that uses evaluative feedback to regularize imitation learning for better alignment in sequential offline RL; shows large reductions in misalignment (up to 98%) and robustness under limited data in Safety Gymnasium tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the misalignment between RL agents and human values by leveraging evaluative feedback in a single-stage offline, sequential decision-making setting, rather than multi-stage pipelines typical of contextual bandits.

Method: Propose FMR, an evaluative-feedback-based regularization technique that can be applied across imitation learning algorithms; adapt Safety Gymnasium as a principled alignment evaluation testbed; focus on improving alignment with limited aligned/noisy demonstrations.

Result: Demonstrates improved aptitude and up to 98% reduction in misalignment across various imitation learning algorithms; robust to data scarcity and noisy demonstrations.

Conclusion: FMR effectively leverages evaluative feedback to align imitation policies in sequential offline settings, offering a robust, algorithm-agnostic approach with strong performance in a principled testbed.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) research has increasingly shifted focus towards alignment, ensuring agents learn behaviors adhering to human values. While human demonstrations and feedback have proven crucial for alignment, existing approaches predominantly combine these signals using multi-stage pipelines designed for the contextual bandit framing of language generation. Yet little work explores how these complementary inputs can serve as a richer, interconnected signal for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments. We propose Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR), an algorithm-agnostic method that harnesses evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to improve the alignment of imitation learning policies. We adapt Safety Gymnasium environments to be a principled testbed for alignment evaluation, demonstrating improved aptitude and up to a 98\% reduction in misalignment across a range of imitation learning algorithms. FMR remains robust in limited data regimes, even when learning from scarce aligned and uninformative noisy demonstrations.

</details>


### [204] [Nigeria Machinery: A Low-Resource Industrial Dataset with a Domain-Grounded Reasoning Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07883)
*Gospel Bassey,Vincent Fakiyesi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Release of two resources: Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset (89 records, 28 indicators, 2006-2025) and a method to generate chain-of-thought prompts from sparse numeric data; results show improved domain grounding and provenance; dataset is a seed/resource under CC-BY-4.0.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the scarcity of public, model-ready, domain-grounded data on industrial machinery for African economies to enable quantitative analysis and numeric tasks for language models; provide provenance and a reproducible CoT prompting resource.

Method: Provide a public dataset (Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures) with 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators (2006–2025), each linked to a public source and decoded via a codebook; develop a method to build chain-of-thought reasoning examples from sparse numeric values, yielding 94 prompt–completion–reasoning-trace rows where prompts name the real indicator, subsector, year, and source; include data adaptation by Adaption Labs and a per-row provenance file; fix a known issue where prompts could mirror real numbers without domain grounding to improve grounding.

Result: New data and reasoning resources: Nigeria dataset and 94 CoT prompts; grounding improved from 1/78 to 94/94 domain-grounded prompts; retrieval answers match source values in 84/84 cases; per-row provenance and CC-BY-4.0 licensing; clear on limitations (small seed dataset; many indicators with single observations; most reasoning is retrieval, not multi-step).

Conclusion: The work provides a seed, domain-grounded dataset and a CoT prompt resource for numeric tasks in a sparsely sampled African industrial context; valuable for researchers seeking domain-grounded prompts and provenance, but not a substitute for large-scale training data; emphasizes transparency and licensing, and highlights common data-generation pitfalls in LLM dataset construction.

Abstract: There is relatively little, public, and model-ready data on industrial machinery for African economies. This makes it hard to do quantitative analysis or to train language models on numeric tasks grounded in that setting. We release two things to help with part of this problem. The first is the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset: 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators, covering Nigeria's manufacturing and oil and gas sectors from 2006 to 2025. Every record names a public source and is decoded by a codebook. The second is a method for building chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning examples from these sparse numeric values. The result is 94 prompt, completion, and reasoning-trace rows. In every row, the prompt names the real indicator, subsector, year, and source of the record it comes from. The data adaptation work was carried out by Adaption Labs. Along the way we describe a problem that is common when language models are used to build datasets. The prompts can match the real numbers while saying nothing about the real domain. We show that fixing this raises the share of domain-grounded prompts from 1 out of 78 in an earlier release to 94 out of 94, and that every retrieval answer now matches its source value (84 out of 84). We release the data, the reasoning layer, and a per-row provenance file under CC-BY-4.0. We are clear about the limits. With 89 records and 17 indicators that have only one observation, this is a reference and seed dataset, not a large training set. Most reasoning rows are retrieval rather than multi-step computation.

</details>


### [205] [Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916)
*Luke Baines,Anton Gonzalvez Hawthorne,Mariia Koroliuk,Irakli Shalibashvili,Clément Dumas,Konstantinos Voudouris,David Demitri Africa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Treats LLM personas as positions in the OCEAN trait space and uses low-rank adapters to modulate traits. Found monotonic scaling, additive trait interactions, and preserved capabilities at moderate scales; demonstrated safety-relevant effects via trait axes and introduced an unsupervised pipeline that extracts four interpretable behavioral factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of reliable methods to decompose, measure, and control model personas; leverage the OCEAN framework to yield interpretable, controllable, and safety-aligned personality manipulation in LLMs.

Method: Train low-rank adapters to amplify/suppress individual OCEAN traits across 4B–32B models (six models from three families). Evaluate with an LLM-judge calibrated against human panels, trait-specific MC benchmarks, and standard capability tests. Examine monoticity, additivity, and safety-related behavior changes across trait axes. Develop an unsupervised psychometric pipeline to recover factors from rollouts.

Result: Adapters move target traits largely monotonically with scale and combine approximately additively to form mixed personas. Capability performance remains stable at moderate scales. Neuroticism and Agreeableness axes influence safety-relevant behaviors (e.g., frustration, sycophancy). The unsupervised pipeline reveals four interpretable factors—tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution—from model outputs.
