Conclusion: Persona control can be framed as learning, scaling, and composing trait dimensions in weight space, creating a bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and safety.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit recurring behavioural patterns -- personas -- that shape generalisation and safety, but we lack reliable tools for decomposing, measuring, and controlling them. Our central insight is to treat personas as positions in a space of behavioural traits, using the OCEAN framework to describe model personas in terms of Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, and Neuroticism. We train low-rank adapters to amplify or suppress individual traits, and evaluate their effects using an LLM-judge calibrated against a human-validated panel, trait-specific multiple-choice benchmarks, and standard capability evaluations. Across six models from three families (4B-32B), we find that each adapter moves its target trait largely monotonically with scale, combines approximately additively with other adapters to construct mixed personas, and preserves performance on capability benchmarks at moderate scales. We further show that the induced trait axes affect safety-relevant behaviour in downstream evaluations: for example, moving along neuroticism and agreeableness axes affects frustration and sycophancy respectively. We also introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline that recovers four interpretable behavioural factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution) from model rollouts. Persona control can then be considered in terms of learning, scaling, and composing traits in weight space, providing a bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and safety.

</details>


### [206] [Evaluating the Effect of Frame Rate in Sequence-Based Classification of Autism-Related Self-Stimulatory Hand Idiosyncrasies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07957)
*Raunak Mondal,Peter Washington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-objective study on video-based autism screening: (1) compare LSTM vs GRU on pose features across sampling rates, (2) examine data augmentation strategies for small datasets using I3D, with per-subject models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve scalable remote ASD screening by identifying architecture, sampling, and augmentation choices that maximize performance under data scarcity.

Method: Evaluate LSTM and GRU on pose-derived features from SSBD at sampling intervals of 1, 5, 15, 30, 45, 90 frames; compare to CNN baselines. Apply ten data augmentation strategies to an I3D transfer-learned pipeline with ablation. Also test per-subject temporally split models.

Result: Peak accuracies: LSTM 97.5% and GRU 98.75% at 15-frame sampling; CNN baselines 62-76%. Horizontal flip standalone accuracy 48.78%. Up-sampling in augmentation is crucial. Per-subject models show consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79).

Conclusion: Provides data-driven guidance for architecture, sampling rate, and augmentation in video-based behavioral classification for data-scarce clinical domains, and highlights personalized models as a means to stabilize predictions.

Abstract: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) affects over 75 million individuals worldwide, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening remain limited. This study addresses two complementary challenges in automated detection of autism-related self-stimulatory behaviors from video: (1) identifying the optimal sequence-based neural network architecture and temporal sampling rate, and (2) characterizing data augmentation strategies for training on small behavioral datasets. For the first objective, long short-term memory (LSTM) and gated recurrent unit (GRU) models were trained on pose-derived features from the Self-Stimulatory Behavior Diagnosis (SSBD) dataset at frame sampling intervals of 1, 5, 15, 30, 45, and 90 frames. Both architectures exceeded prior convolutional neural network (CNN) baselines (62-76% accuracy), with peak accuracies of 97.5% (LSTM) and 98.75% (GRU) at a sampling interval of every 15 frames. For the second objective, ten data augmentation strategies were applied to an I3D transfer learning pipeline, with an ablation study quantifying the marginal contribution of each technique. Horizontal flip achieved the highest standalone accuracy (48.78%), while exclusion of upsampling from the augmentation pipeline produced the largest performance degradation, indicating its necessity for complex behavioral video augmentation. A personalized machine learning approach, in which per-subject models were trained and tested on temporally split segments of each video, produced consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79). These results provide practitioners with concrete guidance on architecture selection, sampling rate, and augmentation strategy for video-based behavioral classification in data-scarce clinical domains.

</details>


### [207] [Agentic Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07984)
*Seokhoon Jeong,Mijung Kim,Taehwan Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentNAS merges LLM-generated seed architectures with a bounded, slot-based NAS search space, enabling efficient, task-specific neural architecture search and achieving state-of-the-art results on 11 of 17 tasks across NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce reliance on manually engineered search spaces in NAS and leverage LLMs to produce high-quality seed designs, then constrain search to a modular, interchangeable slot-based scaffold for conventional NAS to explore.

Method: A three-phase pipeline where an LLM designs a seed architecture, decomposes it into a slotted architecture with named module slots, defines a bounded task-specific search space, and then runs conventional NAS within that space. The approach is instantiated in AgentNAS and evaluated across 17 tasks spanning classification, regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS). Ablations assess the seed quality and the NAS search over slots, across three LLMs.

Result: AgentNAS sets a new state-of-the-art on 11 of 17 tasks. The LLM-generated seed alone surpasses many published baselines, while NAS-based recombination across slots provides additional gains in most tasks. The two search mechanisms are complementary, robust across three LLMs of varying capability.

Conclusion: Dividing labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search yields robust, scalable improvements, enabling high-quality seeds and bounded, recombinable search spaces that outperform task-specific baselines while reducing manual engineering. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) methods have grown increasingly efficient, yet they remain bounded by manually engineered search spaces that require substantial domain expertise and must be rebuilt for every new task. Large language models (LLMs) can generate architectures in an open-ended space, but how to optimally divide the labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search remains unexplored. We propose a mechanism that bridges these two paradigms: an LLM produces a high-quality seed architecture, then decomposes it into a "slotted architecture", a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that automatically defines a bounded, task-specific search space for conventional NAS to explore, without manual engineering. We instantiate this mechanism in AgentNAS, a modular three-phase pipeline in which each component's contribution can be measured independently. On 17 tasks spanning classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging across diverse modalities (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS), AgentNAS establishes a new state of the art on 11 tasks, outperforming published baselines including task-specific expert designs. Ablation studies show that the two search mechanisms are broadly complementary: the LLM-generated seed already surpasses published baselines on the majority of tasks, and NAS delivers additional gains in most cases through combinatorial recombination across slots, a mode of search that independent LLM samples cannot replicate. These patterns hold across three LLMs of different capability levels, confirming that the division of labor is robust. Our code is available at https://github.com/alroimfebruary/AgentNAS.

</details>


### [208] [Concretized Proposition Prompting Resolves Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08018)
*Changhun Lee,Minguk Jeon,Jongkyung Shin,Chiehyeon Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP) to bridge compositionality and knowledge in LLMs by concretizing propositions relevant to questions; CPP improves reasoning, especially in medical domains, and scales across models, suggesting a foundational paradigm to unify composition- and knowledge-based reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often struggle to balance compositionality (logical structure) with knowledgeability (factual accuracy), leading to a Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy. A method to explicitly ground reasoning in concrete propositions may reconcile these aspects.

Method: Introduce Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP) that explicitly formulates and grounds propositions relevant to the task. Evaluate CPP across medical and math benchmarks using multiple foundation models of varying sizes to assess performance gains and scalability.

Result: CPP significantly improves reasoning performance, notably on medical benchmarks requiring precise knowledge, while remaining competitive on math benchmarks driven by deductive reasoning. Demonstrates scalability across different foundation models and parameter counts, suggesting CPP as a general paradigm bridging composition and knowledge-based reasoning.

Conclusion: CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a foundation for logically organized and factually grounded reasoning, scalable across models and domains.

Abstract: LLMs often struggle to balance compositionality with knowledgeability, a challenge we define as Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy. To address this, we propose Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), a framework that explicitly concretizes propositions relevant to questions. The results demonstrate that CPP significantly enhances reasoning performance, particularly in medical benchmarks where precise knowledge is paramount, while being competitive on math benchmarks where deductive reasoning is prioritized. Additional experiments reveal that CPP is scalable to various foundation models and parameter sizes, being a fundamental paradigm that bridges the gap between composition- and knowledge-based approaches. Consequently, CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a solid foundation for logically organized and factually grounded reasoning.

</details>


### [209] [From Prompts to Contracts: Harness Engineering for Auditable Enterprise LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08028)
*Joongho Ahn,Moonsoo Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A reusable harness-engineering pattern converts exploratory LLM prototypes into auditable, source-grounded applications by moving deterministic behavior into code, schemas, and validators around a replaceable composition boundary; demonstrated on a public-data slice of five Korean corporate groups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of traceability, reproducibility, and governance in prototype LLM apps by enforcing fixed contracts, source-based authority, and validated boundaries.

Method: Architect a traceable LLM‑agent with code-owned enforcement, source-backed claims as runtime authority, and validation artifacts; instantiate on a real data slice and evaluate three research questions via fault injection, multi-model substitution, and ablation of enforcement layers; compare internal vs external guards.

Result: The harness preserves grounding, routing, traceability, output hygiene, and language contracts across validation runs; validators flag broken contracts when faults injected. Under model substitution, all 270 runs passed; failures were on the model side. Code-owned guarantees were essential; prompt-only approaches degraded safety/utility; external guardrails helped but decreased utility at times; only code-owned enforcement maintained both safety and utility.

Conclusion: Provides a reusable pattern for turning prototypes into auditable, versioned applications with verifiable source, control, and validation artifacts.

Abstract: Enterprise large language model (LLM) applications often begin as prototypes whose behavior is carried by prompts and retrieval context. Productization adds requirements for source boundaries, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces. We present a harness-engineering approach that reconstructs this pattern into a traceable, auditable LLM-agent architecture: deterministic behavior moves into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts around a replaceable composition boundary, while source-backed claims remain the authority for runtime answers. We instantiate it on a public-data slice of five Korean corporate groups (25 listed companies) and evaluate three research questions. (1) The harness preserves its source-grounding, entity-routing, trace, output-hygiene, and recommendation-language contracts across the fixed validation scenarios; a fault-injection control confirms the validators flag deliberately broken contracts. (2) The checks the harness enforces held under model substitution: across three hosted models, they passed on all 270 composition-boundary runs; failures were confined to the model-composed side and were caught and recorded. (3) The code-owned guarantees are load-bearing, not reproducible by prompting alone: holding the model fixed and varying only the enforcement layer, prompt instructions alone let recommendation-language and internal-trace-leakage violations reach the reader, which the harness blocks entirely. A bolt-on external guardrail prevents such violations too but over-refuses, dropping utility to 88/120 where the harness preserves full utility (120/120); in this ablation, only code-owned enforcement preserves both safety and utility. The result is a reusable engineering pattern for turning exploratory prototypes into auditable applications with versioned source, control, and validation artifacts.

</details>


### [210] [A safety-oriented hypothetico-deductive framework for AI-assisted differential diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08038)
*Fan Ma,Mauro Giuffrè,Donald Wright,Kent McCann,Mark Iscoe,Lingfei Qian,Mingyang Jiang,Chi Wing Ng,Na Hong,Huan He,Cathy Shyr,Qingyu Chen,Lee Schwamm,Lucila Ohno-Machado,Hua Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AegisDx is a safety-focused, hypothetico-deductive diagnostic AI framework that improves coverage of high-risk diagnoses and reasoning verification, outperforming standalone LLMs on multiple datasets and showing improved safety scores in physician evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often perform single-shot diagnoses without safeguards against missed dangerous alternatives or rigorous reasoning verification. A safety-oriented framework could improve bedside diagnostic safety and transparency in acute care.

Method: AegisDx uses role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates to generate broad differentials, explicitly screen for must-not-miss conditions, verify reasoning against medical evidence, and provide actionable next steps.

Result: On literature-derived JAMA/NEJM cases, Top-3 accuracy: 59.9% vs 52.1% (JAMA) and 62.7% vs 51.4% (NEJM). On Annals of Emergency Medicine cases, Top-3 accuracy 85.7% vs 68.6%. Must-not-miss capture in top-3: 78.0% vs 52.0%. Blinded physician evaluation (43 notes) vs GPT-5: safety score 4.55 vs 4.31 (p=2.1e-4).

Conclusion: Engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework can offer safer, more transparent bedside decision support beyond raw predictive accuracy.

Abstract: Diagnostic error is a major threat to patient safety, yet current large language model (LLM) systems often treat diagnosis as a one-shot prediction task, lacking safeguards against missed high-risk alternatives or rigorous verification of their reasoning. Here, we present AegisDx, a safety-oriented framework for hypothetico-deductive clinical reasoning. AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components through role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates to generate broad differential diagnoses, enforce explicit screening for dangerous "must-not-miss" conditions, verify reasoning against grounded medical evidence, and structure actionable next steps. We evaluated AegisDx across three layers. On literature-derived case reports from NEJM and JAMA, with GPT-oss-120B as the shared backbone, Top-3 diagnostic accuracy was 59.9% versus 52.1% for the standalone LLM on JAMA cases and 62.7% versus 51.4% on NEJM cases. On cases from Annals of Emergency Medicine, Top-3 accuracy was 85.7% versus 68.6%; against physician-consensus must-not-miss diagnosis sets, AegisDx captured at least one such condition among its top three diagnoses in 78.0% of cases versus 52.0%. In a blinded physician evaluation of 43 real-world emergency department notes from the Yale New Haven Health System compared against GPT-5, AegisDx improved the physician-rated composite safety score from 4.31 to 4.55 on a 5-point scale (adjusted p = 2.1x10^-4), with qualitative gains in must-not-miss identification and reasoning safety. Our findings suggest that engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework, rather than optimizing raw predictive accuracy alone, can provide a safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful layer of bedside decision support for acute care workflows.

</details>


### [211] [When LLMs Agree, Are They Right? Auditing Self-Consistency and Cross-Model Agreement as Confidence Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08065)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agreement among judges is not a reliable standalone indicator of accuracy in LLM evaluation; it is a weak, regime-dependent proxy with risks of overconfidence, especially for frontier models; data released for further analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Question the common assumption that consensus among evaluators (or model samples) implies correctness in AI-system evaluation, and assess when agreement can be a useful proxy in large-scale, multi-runner settings.

Method: Large-scale cross-runner study with 53 runners, K=50 samples per overlapping case, across model tiers, prompts, and scales on GPQA Diamond and AIME. Used hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap and majority-correctness as deployment label. Also conducted exploratory cross-family check on Claude tiers. De-identified per-run rows and answer distributions publicly released.

Result: Agreement with truth is positive but weak (rho ~0.20–0.59); reliable only in specific regimes (best for unsaturated mid-tier models and compute allocation). Frontier models show overconfidence; high agreement does not imply correctness (77% of GPQA case-result entries had agreement ≥0.8, 48% of those were wrong). Cross-family checks show consistent frontier over-confidence. Self-consistency is a conditional proxy, not a standalone confidence score.

Conclusion: Self-consistency should be used cautiously as a proxy for correctness; it is not sufficient for deployment decisions. The study provides nuanced guidance on when agreement is informative and emphasizes transparency by releasing per-run data.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge (Zheng et al., 2023) is increasingly the default for evaluating AI systems in enterprise pipelines, often scaled to ensembles (Verga et al., 2024) or "mixture-of-experts" (Shazeer et al., 2017) panels of judges. These systems share a key assumption: that consistency -- agreement among judges, or among a model's own samples -- indicates correctness. We show this assumption is unreliable. Agreement is not accuracy: a model can agree with itself, and different models can agree with each other, out of shared bias, a memorized heuristic, or an option-position prior rather than truth. We ask when agreement is nonetheless a usable proxy, in a large-scale cross-runner study: 53 runners drew K=50 samples for assigned overlapping cases across comparisons of model tier, prompting, and scale on GPQA Diamond and AIME -- 265,000 samples. Using majority-correctness as the deployment label and a hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap, agreement is a positive but weak predictor (rho 0.20-0.59, all positive under item-clustered resampling) whose usefulness is regime-dependent: best for unsaturated mid-tier models and for allocating compute, and worst -- over-confident yet no more accurate -- for the most consistent frontier model (agreement >=0.8 on 77% of GPQA case-result entries, 48% of those wrong). An exploratory cross-family check on three Claude tiers shows the same frontier over-confidence, with confident errors recurring across providers above a marginal-preserving null. Self-consistency is thus a conditional proxy for correctness, not a standalone confidence score. We publicly release the de-identified per-run rows and answer distributions.

</details>


### [212] [Persuasion Attacks Can Decrease Effectiveness of CoT Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08066)
*Jennifer Za,Julija Bainiaksina,Nikita Ostrovsky,Tanush Chopra,Victoria Krakovna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoT monitoring can be vulnerable to adversarial persuasion; cross-model fact-checking with model-diverse pairing mitigates risk, reducing approvals of policy-violating actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the reliability of chain-of-thought based monitoring against persuasion-based jailbreaks and identify effective mitigations for safeguarding AI agent behavior.

Method: Create a 40-task evaluation framework and simulate thousands of agent–monitor interactions where agents advocate for policy-violating proposals; measure how often monitors approve; then implement a fact-checking monitor using model-diverse pairings (e.g., Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor with GPT-4.1 fact-checker) and compare against a same-model setup.

Result: Under adversarial conditions, CoT monitoring increases approval of harmful actions by about 9.5% on average due to the scratchpad as an additional persuasion channel; model-diverse fact-checking pairing reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45% (versus only 6% when both roles use the same model).

Conclusion: CoT monitoring alone is insufficient to counter adversarial persuasion; employing model-diverse fact-checking provides a robust mitigation strategy.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising safety mechanism for AI agents, based on the premise that visible reasoning traces can surface misaligned or deceptive behavior. While effective in standard scenarios, recent work highlights that LLMs remain vulnerable to persuasion-based jailbreaks, where natural-language arguments override model constraints. We stress-test whether this vulnerability extends to monitoring LLMs: can an adversarial agent persuade its CoT monitor to approve proposed actions that violate the monitor's policy? We design an evaluation framework with 40 tasks and analyze thousands of agent-monitor interactions, where agents are instructed to argue for policy-violating proposals. We find that in such adversarial settings, monitor access to the agent's CoT reasoning increases rather than decreases approval of harmful actions on average by 9.5%, as the scratchpad provides an additional persuasion channel. To address this, we introduce a fact-checking monitoring framework. We find that a fact-checker and monitor pairing from different model families, for example a Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor paired with a GPT-4.1 fact-checker, reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45%, compared to only 6%, when using the same model for both fact-checking and monitoring roles. Our results demonstrate that CoT monitoring alone may be insufficient against adversarial persuasion, and that model-diverse fact-checking provides a robust mitigation.

</details>


### [213] [PARA-PV: Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented PV Prediction Based on Frozen Foundation Model and Distribution Shift Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08079)
*Hang Fan,Weican Liu,Ying Lu,Dunnan Liu,Long Cheng,Wei Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PARA-PV is a physics-aware retrieval-augmented framework for photovoltaic forecasting that combines patch-level encoding, physics-consistent analog retrieval, a Chronos foundation-model calibrated base, distribution-shift correction, and a regime-aware physics-constrained loss to improve accuracy across diurnal and weather regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PV power is shaped by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and physical constraints; existing models struggle to incorporate physics and long-term memory for robust forecasts.

Method: Encode multivariate PV observations into patch representations. Use a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner to fetch historical patches/analog trajectories consistent with current window in temporal shape, power level, PV operating state, and intra-day period to form a physically grounded base forecast. Calibrate this base with a frozen Chronos time-series foundation-model prior via a lightweight residual adapter to inject general temporal regularities without overriding physical grounding. Apply a physics-aware distribution shift correction module that adjusts forecasts based on power, weather, timestamp, and day/night conditions using gated mean-shift and scale corrections. Employ a physics-constrained loss that partitions samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes and adaptively reweights errors to prevent dominance of the regular regime.

Result: The abstract presents the architecture and training strategy but does not report quantitative empirical results; it emphasizes achieving a physically grounded base forecast, temporal generalization via Chronos, and regime-aware error handling.

Conclusion:  PARA-PV fuses physics-aware retrieval, foundation-model calibration, distribution-shift correction, and regime-aware loss to deliver robust PV forecasts; code is available at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: Accurate photovoltaic (PV) power forecasting is essential for reliable grid dispatch and renewable energy integration, yet it remains challenging because PV generation is jointly shaped by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and strict physical constraints. We propose PARA-PV, a Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented framework that embeds physical knowledge throughout the forecasting process. The framework first encodes multivariate PV observations into patch-level representations and, through a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner, retrieves historical patches and analog trajectories that are consistent with the current window in temporal shape, power level, PV operating state, and intra-day period; this yields a physically grounded base forecast. To supplement local memory with broader temporal knowledge, the base forecast is then calibrated against a frozen Chronos time-series foundation-model prior through a lightweight residual adapter, so that general temporal regularities are adapted to PV-specific dynamics without overriding the physically grounded prediction. Because residual conditional distribution shifts persist when weather and diurnal regimes change, a physics-aware distribution shift correction module subsequently adjusts the preliminary forecast using power, weather, timestamp, and day/night conditions, applying gated mean-shift and scale corrections selectively. Finally, a physics-constrained loss function partitions the samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes and adaptively reweights their error contributions, preventing the dominant regular regime from suppressing learning of operationally critical states. Our code is available at https://github.com/weican1103/PARA-PV.

</details>


### [214] [CausalDS: Benchmarking Causal Reasoning in Data-Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08093)
*Andrej Leban,Yuekai Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces CausalDS, a synthetic but empirically grounded benchmark to evaluate causal reasoning in agentic data-science workflows, spanning Pearl's rungs and incorporating tool use, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and coding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks either focus on symbolic causal reasoning without realistic data analysis or on data-analysis tasks without a principled causal data-generating structure. They also rely on limited, templated variations and are prone to “causal parrot” artifacts. A diverse, systematically generated, synthetic yet grounded benchmark is needed to evaluate LLMs in integrated data-science settings.

Method: Sample structural causal models (SCMs) to generate observational data; create synthetic NL stories grounded in realistic domains; optionally ground components in empirical distributions from real-world data; derive tasks across Pearl's three rungs, with data-science prediction tasks and routine coding/tool-use steps; incorporate imperfect observations via an observation model; include abstention as a first-class scored outcome.

Result: A unified benchmark framework that evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use in agentic workflows, offering diverse, realistic, and systematically generated scenarios beyond template-based datasets.

Conclusion: CausalDS fills a gap by integrating causal reasoning with practical data-science workflows, enabling robust, cross-cutting evaluation of LLM-based data-science agents and guiding future improvements.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as integrated data-science agents, combining abstract reasoning with advanced tool use. Yet the relevant benchmark landscape largely divides into symbolic causal reasoning benchmarks without realistic data analysis or data analysis benchmarks without a principled causal data-generating structure. Furthermore, existing causal evaluation datasets are often restricted to curated examples from existing sources, with diversity coming from limited templatized variations rather than from systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures. We introduce CausalDS, a benchmark for evaluating causal reasoning in agentic data-science workflows. Each benchmark instance is a scene consisting of a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data and an accompanying synthetic natural-language story grounded in a realistic domain. We optionally ground the composition of the benchmark components in empirical distributions obtained from real-world datasets, thus retaining empirical structure while reducing the "causal parrot" risk through completely synthetic generation. From each scene, we then derive tasks spanning all three of Pearl's rungs, with typical data-science prediction tasks appearing as Rung 1. Most tasks include a data science coding component, where the model typically needs to use several tools to arrive at the final answer due to the frequent presence of imperfect observations, which are generated by an observation model. Additionally, recognizing when a question admits no warranted answer and abstaining is treated as a first-class scored outcome. The benchmark thus jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use/coding.

</details>


### [215] [Answer Set Programming Energised! End-to-End Neurosymbolic Reasoning and Learning with ASP and Energy Based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08136)
*Jakob Suchan,Julius Monsen,Salim Baloch,Mehul Bhatt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neurosymbolic framework integrating answer set programming with energy-based models for end-to-end training in dynamic domains, enabling joint continuous latent-space optimization with declarative ASP semantics; demonstrated on MNIST, CLEVR, and MOT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge symbolic reasoning and continuous perception by combining ASP's non-monotonic, background-knowledge-driven reasoning with differentiable energy-based models to enable joint optimization and robust inference in dynamic tasks.

Method: Modular integration of ASP with an energy-based model substrate; explicit declarative semantics in a continuous latent space; ASP-centric end-to-end training; support for non-monotonic reasoning and background knowledge; generalized model at the interface of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and theory modulo ASP; practical platform for end-to-end training in perception and interaction.

Result: Empirical demonstrations on MNIST; evaluations on visual question answering benchmark CLEVR and multi-object tracking benchmark MOT.

Conclusion: Proposes a general, practical neurosymbolic framework enabling robust end-to-end training in perception-plus-reasoning tasks, with a flexible platform for incorporating background knowledge and constraints; potential for improved generalization in dynamic domains.

Abstract: We present a general neurosymbolic reasoning and learning methodology based on a modular integration of answer set programming with an energy based model substrate. Key contributions are: (1) supporting joint optimisation in the continuous latent space through explicit ASP-based declarative semantics fully incorporating background knowledge, constraints, non-monotonic inference; and (2) advancing recent works at the interface of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories by providing a generalised model and practical platform for ASP-centric robust, end-to-end training for applications in dynamic domains (e.g., involving perception and interaction). We provide a practical implementation, and demonstrate basic use and application (with MNIST), and evaluate with the visual question-answering benchmark Clevr and the multi-object tracking benchmark MOT.

</details>


### [216] [Overthinking: Amplifying Reasoning Weights to Extract Learned Secrets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08173)
*Jack Hopkins,Dipika Khullar,Fabien Roger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes 'overthinking' by linearly interpolating between a non-reasoning and a reasoning model to amplify reasoning, adds layer-wise attenuation, and reports that this amplification reveals hidden information up to 10x more often across 2B-32B models; effectiveness depends on the secret type.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve black-box auditing of language models by eliciting hidden information and misalignment that standard reasoning prompts may miss, through deliberate amplification of reasoning processes.

Method: Define overthinking model θ_Oα = θ_M + α(θ_R − θ_M) with α > 1 to push beyond the pure reasoning model R. Introduce layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning while preserving output quality. Evaluate across four experimental settings and four models ranging from 2B to 32B parameters.

Result: Overthinking models more frequently reveal hidden information than the baseline reasoning model, with up to about 10× increase in surface occurrences. The surface mechanism depends on the secret type: some secrets require perturbation along the reasoning direction, others respond to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

Conclusion: Reasoning amplification via overthinking is a promising approach for surfacing secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training, though its effectiveness is contingent on secret type and perturbation pathway; careful design of perturbations is crucial.

Abstract: Black box auditing of language models is an essential pre-deployment tool, but it may miss subtle forms of misalignment and hidden information. To better elicit hidden information during an auditing process, we introduce \emph{overthinking}: the process of using reasoning task vectors to amplify the propensity to think out loud of reasoning models. Given the parameters of a non-reasoning instruct model $M$ and reasoning-distilled model $R$, we define the \emph{overthinking model} as $\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{O}_α} = \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}} + α(\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{R}} - \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}})$, where $α> 1$ amplifies reasoning beyond the pure reasoning model $R$. Additionally, we introduce new layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning without losing quality and coherence of model outputs. We demonstrate that overthinking models are more likely to reveal hidden information across four experimental settings, across 2B-32B models. Our findings suggest that reasoning amplification may surface secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training up to $10\times$ more frequently than the original reasoning model. How secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation along the reasoning direction, while others yield to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

</details>


### [217] [ASMR: Agentic Schema Generation for Ship Maintenance Report Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08177)
*Sohrab Namazi Nia,Amogh Dalal,Ning Sa,Peter Ly,Marti Zentmaier,Tomek Strzalkowski,Jay Miller,Rishi Singh,Senjuti Basu Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASMR: a two-agent framework for automatic schema generation from historical ship reports, combining adaptive clustering for field discovery and RL-based optimization for compact, informative schemas; preliminary results are promising with open research challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce manual schema engineering by automatically discovering compact, informative schemas that capture essential information requirements across diverse report types, improving consistency, completeness, and actionability.

Method: Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from narratives and uses adaptive multi-granularity clustering to propose candidate schema fields. Structural Optimizer Agent applies reinforcement learning to select and prune fields to form compact, non-redundant schemas.

Result: Preliminary results indicate the approach can produce useful schemas that guide authors toward more complete and consistent reports; highlights open challenges at the intersections of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Conclusion: ASMR is a promising direction for automated schema design in multi-form domains; further work needed to validate on broader datasets, refine reward signals, and integrate human-in-the-loop for deployment.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the automatic schema generation problem: given a collection of historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, automatically discover compact and informative schemas that capture the essential information requirements of each report type. To address this challenge, we propose ASMR, a modular agentic framework consisting of two specialized agents. A Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from historical narratives and generates candidate schema fields through adaptive multi-granularity clustering, while a Structural Optimizer Agent employs reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations. The resulting schemas can guide report authors toward producing more complete, consistent, and actionable reports. Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the proposed approach and highlight several open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

</details>


### [218] [A First-Principles Theory of Slow Thinking and Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08196)
*Hongkang Yang,Zhi-Qin John Xu,Feiyu Xiong,Weinan E*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes 'active lifting' as a first-principles framework to model thinking and perception, deriving slow thinking and active perception, and outlining a design/training/inference pipeline for slow-thinking LLMs within representation and sampler hierarchies; includes a three-stage improvement pathway and a unified, multi-modal encoder/generator approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize cognitive functions (thinking and perception) from probabilistic lifting/projection of distributions, enabling slow thinking and intrinsic agency in AI systems, and to provide a unified theory for encoders/gen generators across modalities while addressing issues like policy collapse.

Method: Introduce active lifting: sampling latent sequences with an intrinsic drive to minimize uncertainty at maximal rate; formalize lifting/projection between observable and latent spaces; position slow-thinking models within static theory subspace on representation and sampler hierarchies; derive an internal-time inference process and a minimum-length coding-like training objective; propose a three-stage pathway and a unified, cross-modality encoder/generator framework.

Result: The theory yields a large design space for slow-thinking models, situates them within static theory hierarchies, derives internal-time inference, and links training objectives to coding efficiency; it also predicts the emergence of languages and provides by-products including a three-stage improvement pathway, a universal encoder/generator construction across modalities, a priori human-like representations, and potential mitigation of policy collapse.

Conclusion: The active lifting framework offers a principled route to modeling and engineering slow-thinking, active perception, and autonomous representation-building in AI systems, with concrete design pathways and cross-modal unification, while inviting empirical validation and careful assessment of practical implications such as alignment and safety.

Abstract: As part of a series on first-principles modeling of cognitive functions, this paper attempts to provide a mathematical formulation of thinking and perception. It formally derives slow thinking or more generally, active perception, and encompasses the design, training and inference of slow thinking large language models. Our starting point is the lifting and projection of probability distributions on the observable and latent spaces, with the objective of representing complex data distributions by simple function families such as neural networks. A theory called "active lifting" is proposed, based on the sampling of latent sequences and an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty with maximum rate. It derives a large design space, containing the slow thinking models in a subspace that we call the static theory. These models are positioned on the representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy induced by the static theory, and can be upgraded by climbing the two hierarchies. Active lifting further derives an inference process with an internal time axis, and a training objective that resembles minimum-length coding as well as the invention of languages. Thus, it characterizes the agency of perception, including the emergence of the slow thinking formats. Technical by-products of this theory include a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models, a unified approach to constructing encoders and generative models for all data modalities, a priori formation of human-like visual representations, and a possible solution to policy collapse.

</details>


### [219] [Playing ZendoWorld: Challenging AI Agents on Active Visual Concept Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08233)
*Sophia Koehler,Antonia Wüst,Inga Ibs,Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Wolfgang Stammer,Constantin Rothkopf,Kevin Ellis,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZendoWorld is a controlled testbed for joint perception, hypothesis induction, and active experimentation to infer logical visual rules; key findings show label accuracy does not guarantee rule recovery, perception and induction bottlenecks vary by agent class, and VLM-based agents propose uninformative experiments, with humans showing stronger inductive reasoning for simpler rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how agents integrate perception, hypothesis formation, and active data gathering to discover hidden generative rules from visual environments; illuminate bottlenecks and guide improvements for intelligent systems and scientific discovery.

Method: Introduce ZendoWorld: a controlled interactive environment where agents observe scenes, propose new scenes to acquire information, and update hypotheses about a hidden logical rule based on feedback. Evaluate diverse agent families (pure vision-language model reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic approaches) and compare with human data on the same task.

Result: Agent performance reveals three main insights: (1) high accuracy on observed labels does not imply correct rule recovery; (2) perception and inductive inference bottlenecks differ across agent classes; (3) VLM-based agents generate near-informative experiments that fail to reduce hypothesis uncertainty. Human data shows inductive reasoning gaps, especially for complex rules.

Conclusion: ZendoWorld advances the evaluation of intelligent agents in tasks requiring belief formation and active experimentation, and highlights concrete avenues for improvement in domains like scientific discovery, such as better integration of perception and symbolic induction and more informative experimental design.

Abstract: A central challenge in building intelligent systems is enabling agents to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test them. To study this problem, we propose ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment in which agents must infer a logical rule about visual game observations, acquire information by proposing new scenes, and refine their hypotheses based on feedback from the game environment. We evaluate several agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods. Our main findings are: (1) high accuracy in predicting labels for observed examples does not imply recovery of the underlying rule; (2) perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; and (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to actively reduce hypothesis uncertainty. To compare these results, we collect human data on the task, which reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly for more complex rules. Overall, ZENDOWORLD takes an important step toward evaluating intelligent agents and identifies concrete avenues for improvement, particularly in domains like scientific discovery.

</details>


### [220] [AutoPersonas: A Multi-Timescale Loop Engine for Open-Ended Persona Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08252)
*Mengchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoPersonas proposes a bounded, multi-timescale life-environment engine to curb self-locking in long-running persona agents by separating Occurrences, Observations, and State and enforcing evidence-guided absorption before state reachability changes. In multi-model, long-horizon and A/B tests, the approach reduces macro-theme repetition and increases theme diversity while preserving identity continuity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address runtime self-locking in continuing persona-life loops, where locally plausible events accumulate and push agents toward stale environments and decisions, threatening identity continuity and adaptability.

Method: Introduce AutoPersonas with an OSO loop that separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Permit divergent future material but require evidence-guided absorption before State or reachability changes. Conduct a three-year compressed simulation to reveal environment-watermark mechanisms; run eight models for 40 days (≈1,600 events) with 5-day rolling action-category repetition metrics; perform a same-runtime 40-day A/B with context-slice masking and per-sample divergence targeting; also validate with a juvenile-goblin fictional-world run.

Result: Findings include environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. Eight-model 40-day stress test generated 1,600 events; mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition = 95.2–97.6% (all models >90% by day 11). Semantic macro-theme repetition = 79.0–88.0% across direct loops. In the A/B, context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. Juvenile-goblin world run demonstrated anti-fixation without real-world intrusions. Conclusion: the separation of controlled divergence from evidence-guided absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity.

Conclusion: Separated divergence and evidence absorption constitute a bounded mechanism to maintain identity while allowing adaptation, mitigating self-locking in long-running persona systems.

Abstract: Long-term persona agents must remain identifiable while adapting to new events, relationships, evidence, and social conditions. We identify self-locking as a runtime failure mode in continuing persona-life loops: locally plausible events keep appearing while the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages. We trace this failure to model-level convergence toward high-probability behavioral channels and system-level context gravity from State, memory, history, and environment summaries. We introduce AutoPersonas, a multi-timescale life-environment engine for bounded persona-level recursive self-evolution. It separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Its OSO loop admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes. A three-year compressed simulation exposed environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation failures, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. An eight-model 40-day stress test generated 1,600 events and found mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition of 95.2%-97.6%, with all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping found 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition across all direct-loop runs. In a same-runtime 40-day A/B, context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. A juvenile-goblin fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without hard real-world intrusions. These results support a bounded claim: separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity.

</details>


### [221] [Compete Then Collaborate: Frontier AI Teachers Build a Verifiable Curriculum to Improve a Coding Student Beyond Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08255)
*Miseong Shawn Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Four frontier LLMs compete via an execution-based judge and then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student, revealing that ranking accuracy matters little for student outcomes, SFT on verified solutions can harm learning, while reinforcement-learning-with-verifiable-rewards (RLVR) improves performance; a reproducible on-prem pipeline is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address bias in LLM judges and inefficiencies in multi-teacher distillation by separating competitive assessment from collaborative curriculum construction, ensuring verifiability and robust student learning under a controlled environment.

Method: Four frontier teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head using an execution-based judge with fairness controls; the best teachers collaborate to design a verifiable curriculum for the student Qwen2.5-Coder; evaluate student performance on MBPP and competition problems; compare SFT on verified solutions versus RLVR; release an on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with GRPO patches.

Result: (1) Execution-verified problems yield near-perfect student solutions (99-100%) due to saturation; harder problems show separation (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%), but student results do not depend on teacher ranking. (2) Imitation (SFT) on verified solutions degrades performance for 7B/32B students (MBPP: 76.7%→72.7%; competition: 5.9%→2.9%). (3) RLVR using the same collaborative curriculum improves competition performance (5.9%→8.8%, +49% relative). The value lies in a verifiable, learning-by-doing environment rather than pooling answers. A reproducible on-prem pipeline is released.

Conclusion: The study argues that AI-teacher collaboration is most valuable when it constructs a verifiable environment for learning by doing, rather than merely pooling teacher outputs for imitation; ranking among teachers is less critical for the student, and RL-based verifiable rewards can surpass SFT in this setting. A reproducible deployment pipeline is provided.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as teachers generating training data for smaller students. Prior multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods merge outputs without determining which frontier model teaches best, often relying on an LLM judge biased toward its own outputs. We introduce a compete-then-collaborate framework where four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head by an execution-based judge (unit tests and stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls, and then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student (Qwen2.5-Coder). We report three findings. (1) Under execution verification, all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly after self-correction (99-100%) due to a saturation effect, but harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%); however, the robust student-side results do not depend on teacher ranking. (2) Imitation (SFT) on verified solutions does not improve, and can degrade, an already-competent student at 7B and 32B (e.g., from 76.7% to 72.7% on MBPP-test, and 5.9% to 2.9% on competition problems). (3) Using the same collaborative curriculum as a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) environment improves the student (from 5.9% to 8.8% peak on competition problems, a +49% relative gain), reversing SFT's direction. The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers to imitate, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing. We release a reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with framework patches for running GRPO on a bleeding-edge stack.

</details>


### [222] [MentalHospital: A Virtual Environment for Evaluating Psychiatric Clinical Encounters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08257)
*Yuming Yang,Xiao Sun,Yuanwei Zou,Zhengxiao Wu,Yun Chen,Jiang Zhong,Haoyang Zeng,Jingwang Huang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces MentalHospital and MentalEval, a dual-track, standardized-patient benchmark for LLM-based psychiatric encounters, revealing current AI gaps vs clinicians and highlighting mental-status assessment as a key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in holistic, scalable benchmarks for AI in psychiatry by simulating complete clinical encounters and measuring both objective performance and clinical process quality.

Method: Builds skill-augmented standardized patients from 1,193 de-identified EHR cases spanning ICD-11 categories and 76 disorders to instantiate S.O.A.P. encounters. Uses a dual-track evaluation: objective comparison to EHR-derived references plus subjective assessment of process quality. Develops MentalEval with five domain-specific evaluators (communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, treatment appropriateness), trained via rubric-grounded supervised fine-tuning and expert-guided Direct Preference Optimization. Includes survey of 22 clinicians for fidelity.

Result: Clinician fidelity: 3.88/5. MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with average Cohen’s Kappa (QWK) of 0.944. Benchmarking shows strongest LLMs lag clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment identified as a primary bottleneck.

Conclusion: MentalHospital and MentalEval offer a scalable, clinically faithful benchmark for AI in psychiatry; despite high evaluator alignment, current LLMs substantially underperform clinicians, particularly in mental status assessment, indicating clear directions for improvement and standardization of evaluation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on isolated psychiatric tasks, including dialogue, diagnosis, and treatment planning, yet existing benchmarks rarely simulate complete psychiatric clinical encounters. We introduce $\textbf{MentalHospital}$, a virtual evaluation environment for LLM-based psychiatric clinical encounters. MentalHospital instantiates the Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, and Treatment Planning (S.O.A.P.) workflow, using skill-augmented standardized patients constructed from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric electronic health record (EHR) cases spanning all major ICD-11 categories and 76 disorders. Each encounter is assessed through a dual-track protocol that combines objective comparison against EHR-derived references with subjective assessment of clinical process quality. To scale specialist judgment, we develop $\textbf{MentalEval}$, five domain-specific evaluators covering communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, and treatment appropriateness, trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO. Survey responses from 22 clinicians support MentalHospital's clinical fidelity (3.88/5), while MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with an average QWK of 0.944. Benchmarking shows that even the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.

</details>


### [223] [Different Teachers, Different Capabilities: Sub-1B On-Device Distillation for Structured Text Enrichment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08268)
*Vinay Kumar Chaganti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distilling an 8B reasoning teacher into a 0.6B on-device student yields large speedups with partial performance recovery; evaluation by blinded panel shows ~58% gap closure on summary quality, with per-subtask and per-field routing insights rather than a single superior model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-volume structured extraction incurs high latency. The work evaluates whether task-level distillation yields comparable outputs on sub-tasks when constrained to on-device inference, and how teacher characteristics (reasoning, size, control) affect outcomes.

Method: Distill an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B; QLoRA, three seeds). Introduce two teacher controls: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. Use blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel to score each arm against full article; compare to two baselines (few-shot prompting, constrained decoding). Dataset: each news article mapped to a JSON with a short summary and five categorical labels. Measure on-device latency and per-task performance, and analyze subgroups to identify sources of gains.

Result: The 0.6B student runs ~0.8s per article versus the 8B teacher’s ~39s, recovering 58% of the base-to-teacher gap in summary quality. It outperforms constrained decoding by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher yields no improvement, suggesting gains stem from the reasoning aspect rather than model size. The reasoning teacher enhances writing quality; the managed pipeline improves label diversity. Grounding effects vary by subgroup; the overall results support a per-field routing map as the practical deliverable rather than a single best model.

Conclusion: No single engine dominates across fields. The study yields a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment, highlighting how different teacher signals (reasoning vs diversity) contribute to different sub-tasks. The evidence points toward task-level customization and diagnosis over a universal distillation win.

Abstract: High-volume structured extraction pays a large model's latency on every item, so distilling the task into a small on-device model is attractive: comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost. We measure what that distillation actually delivers, per sub-task. Each news article is mapped to one JSON object with a short summary and five categorical labels. We distill an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B; QLoRA, three seeds), and add two teacher controls: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. A blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scores every arm against the full article, alongside two non-distillation baselines, few-shot prompting and constrained decoding. The student runs at about 0.8 s per article against the teacher's 39 s, and recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality, beating its primary baseline (constrained decoding) by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by a secondary +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher trains a student no better than the untuned base, so the summary gain follows from the teacher's reasoning nature rather than its scale. Capabilities then split by teacher: the reasoning teacher transfers writing quality and the managed pipeline transfers label diversity, while a same-size instruction teacher's students stay more grounded on the 22 short, thin-source articles in the 93-item test set (74 versus 55 faithful), where the reasoning-lineage student fabricates. That grounding difference is a consistent ordering rather than a significant aggregate effect, and the subgroup is small, so we report it as a direction. Because no single engine wins every field, the deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment.

</details>


### [224] [INTENT: An LSTM Framework for Vehicle Intention Prediction in Intersection Scenarios with Comprehensive Ablation Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08316)
*Logine M. Zaki,Catherine M. Elias*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes INTENT, an LSTM-based framework for predicting vehicle intention at intersections 2 seconds ahead (straight/left/right) with 99.71% accuracy on the IN D dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve human-like interpretation of driver intent in complex driving scenarios (intersections, roundabouts, emergencies) to improve safety and enable real-time evasive actions and intention-conditioned trajectory prediction.

Method: INTENT framework using an LSTM model to predict intersection vehicle intention 2 seconds before the event; classification into straight, left turn, or right turn; extensive experiments and ablation studies on the InD dataset.

Result: Achieves 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset; multiple model experiments and ablation analyses support the robustness of the approach.

Conclusion: The INTENT framework demonstrates high-accuracy early prediction of intersection intentions, facilitating safer real-time decision-making and contributing to intention-conditioned trajectory prediction.

Abstract: Vehicle intention prediction is a pivotal aspect in the agility and safety of autonomous vehicles in all driving scenarios; if genuine enhancement of autonomous vehicles are required, we need to make them adopt human interpretation of driver's intention especially in cases that require a lot of human interaction as well as complex driving behaviors like the ones at intersections, roundabouts and emergency cases such as sudden stops where vehicle intention prediction helps in taking the correct evasive action within a real time period where every second of action makes an impact and can prevent a catastrophe from taking place. In the worst case, it helps minimize the damage and make safety a priority. Intention prediction can also be used to enhance trajectory prediction (intention conditioned trajectory prediction). In this study, The INTENT framework is proposed using LSTM model to predict the vehicle's intention at intersections 2 seconds ahead of the event occurrence to predict whether the cars in intersections are going straight, turning left, or turning right. Various model experiments and ablation study are thoroughly tested on InD dataset achieving 99.71% accuracy.

</details>


### [225] [PolyUQuest: Verifiable Structure-Aware Web RAG over Heterogeneous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08269)
*Ying Liu,Yi Ye,Quanyu Feng,Mingxi Ye,Mingtao Zhang,Haoyang Li,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyUQuest introduces a structure-aware web RAG framework built on a heterogeneous graph that unifies hyperlinks, DOM blocks, and cross-page entity relations, with a two-tier router and fully verifiable evidence for each answer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preserve structural and semantic signals in HTML when performing retrieval and ensure verifiability, addressing limitations of flat-text RAG.

Method: Construct a heterogeneous graph integrating page hyperlinks, DOM hierarchy, and entity-relation data; implement a two-tier router routing queries to direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, or multi-hop entity reasoning; provide traceable citations (source page, heading path, entity links).

Result: Outperforms existing RAG baselines in accuracy, coverage, and faithfulness while reducing LLM token usage; evaluated on PolyU's official website (4,240 pages; 31,086 DOM blocks; 29,119 entities; 37,680 relations) plus a multi-type evaluation benchmark; includes an interactive demonstration and prep for deployment as a student QA service at PolyU.

Conclusion: Structure-aware, verifiable web RAG over a heterogeneous graph improves retrieval quality and traceability, with scalable deployment potential for university-scale web corpora.

Abstract: Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat web pages as flat text, losing the structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML. We present PolyUQuest, a verifiable, structure-aware web RAG framework built on a heterogeneous graph that unifies hyperlink topology between pages, DOM hierarchy within pages, and entity-relation knowledge across pages. A two-tier router dispatches each query to one of three retrieval modes matched to its structural need, including direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, and multi-hop entity reasoning. Every answer is fully verifiable, as each cited block carries its source page, heading path, and entity links so that users can trace any claim back to its structural evidence. We evaluate on the official websites of the Hong Kong Polytechnic University (PolyU), comprising 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations, together with a multi-type evaluation benchmark. PolyUQuest outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness, while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query. The demonstration provides an interactive interface for inspecting cited answers, comparing retrieval traces across routing modes, and exploring evidence graph paths. PolyUQuest is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

</details>


### [226] [Understanding Axes of Difficulty For Long Context Tasks Via PredicateLongBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08284)
*Siddhartha Jain,Ameya Velingker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PredicateLongBench is a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning by finding the longest subsequence satisfying predicates, with synthetic and real-world pipelines, revealing scaling limits of frontier LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current long-context benchmarks focus on average-case performance and saturate; there is no systematic, axis-driven probe of how difficulty scales with task complexity.

Method: Two generation pipelines (synthetic random word-like strings and real-world natural documents) generate tasks requiring identifying the longest contiguous subsequence meeting given predicates; multiple axes of difficulty; no LLM-based generations or judges.

Result: Frontier models show degraded performance as task difficulty increases along the axes, highlighting current long-context limitations; benchmark proves useful for diagnosing capabilities.

Conclusion: The tasks are conceptually simple, computationally tractable, and do not rely on generated labels, providing a robust tool to understand long-context reasoning and its scaling limits.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated rapidly improving long-context capabilities, prompting a wave of benchmarks designed to evaluate them. However, existing long-context evaluations - from Needle-in-a-Haystack (NIAH) tests to more recent multi-hop reasoning and summarization tasks - predominantly measure average-case performance, and many are either saturated or lack robustness. Notably absent is a systematic way to probe how models perform as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along various axes. We address this gap by proposing PredicateLongBench, a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning by asking models to identify the longest contiguous subsequence of words in a long input that satisfies given predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering), drawn from a broader predicate class. The central innovation of our benchmark is the identification and systematic exploration of multiple different axes of difficulty which test multiple aspects of long context understanding. We provide two complementary generation pipelines - a fully synthetic setup using random word-like strings, and a real-world setup that samples words from natural documents while preserving their distributional properties. We find that frontier models struggle to perform well as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along our axes, demonstrating the utility of our benchmark in understanding the limitations of current long-context capabilities. Furthermore, the tasks in PredicateLongBench, though challenging, are conceptually simple and do not require LLM-based generations or judges.

</details>


### [227] [Psychological Competence as a Missing Dimension in AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08285)
*Marcos Economides,Paul M. Sacher,Samuel Salzer,Alexis Michelle Abellar,Fendi Tsim,Antoine Ferrère*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes psychological competence as a missing dimension in AI evaluation for human-facing systems; defines the construct, outlines a framework, and suggests assessment methods; advocates integration into practice by providers, deployers, researchers, and regulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation focuses on technical metrics (accuracy, robustness, etc.) and neglects the social-psychological effects of interactions with users. For systems acting as advisors, tutors, or companions, evaluation must account for how responses shape user cognition, emotion interpretation, trust, and decision-making.

Method: Theoretical/conceptual work drawing on behavioral science and human–AI interaction literature. Defines core domains of psychological competence, and proposes assessment approaches (scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, model-assisted evaluation) rather than a fixed benchmark.

Result: A defined construct and boundary for psychological competence, plus a framework of core domains and concrete avenues for evaluation. It refrains from presenting empirical results or a benchmark but outlines how such assessments could be conducted.

Conclusion: Psychological competence should be a core consideration for model developers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators to understand and manage the broader real-world effects of human-facing AI.

Abstract: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus primarily on technical performance, including accuracy, robustness, reasoning ability, and policy compliance. These measures remain essential, but they are not sufficient for systems that interact directly with users through natural language. Human-facing AI systems are increasingly used as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. In these roles, their responses can shape how users reason, interpret emotions, form beliefs, calibrate trust, and make decisions. The relevant unit of evaluation is therefore not only the model, but the human-AI interaction. This paper introduces psychological competence as a missing dimension in AI evaluation. We define psychological competence as the capacity of a human-facing AI system to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making in ways that are appropriate to the user, context, and purpose of the interaction. This includes interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance. Existing evaluation approaches capture parts of this problem but rarely assess these psychological effects directly. Drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, we outline a conceptual framework for psychological competence and its core domains. Rather than proposing a specific benchmark, we define the construct, clarify its boundaries, and describe how it may be assessed through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. We argue that psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems.

</details>


### [228] [Blind-Spots-Bench: Evaluating Blind Spots in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08317)
*Matteo Santelmo,Xiuying Wei,Israa Fakih,Felix Bauer,Juan Garcia Giraldo,Chengkun Li,Etienne Bamas,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces blind-spots-bench, a 235-sample benchmark to expose human-like but AI-challenging tasks; uses automated grading across open-weight and closed-source models; finds closed-source models outperform open-weight by ~10% gap; no model dominates across all tasks; some tasks remain hard for all.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots that humans find trivial; need a diagnostic stress test to reveal weaknesses in current models across modalities.

Method: Collect raw questions from AI course students; clean and annotate with structured reference solutions; develop a task taxonomy tailored to 235-sample dataset; implement automated grading pipeline; evaluate models spanning open-weight/closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation.

Result: Closed-source frontier models substantially outperform open-weight models with ~10% accuracy gap, even when on existing benchmarks they are comparable; no single model dominates across all task types; some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models.

Conclusion: blind-spots-bench provides a diagnostic tool to identify concrete weaknesses in current AI systems and serves as a stress test beyond traditional benchmarks.

Abstract: Modern AI models achieve strong performance on many established benchmarks, yet they still fail on tasks that humans find almost trivial, such as manipulating a string or drawing a dog with five legs. These examples suggest that existing benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots in current systems. We introduce $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$, a benchmark designed to expose such blind spots through tasks that appear simple for humans but remain challenging for modern AI. We collect raw questions from students in an AI course, clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions, and propose a task taxonomy tailored to the resulting dataset of 235 samples. We further develop an automated grading pipeline to evaluate a wide range of models, including open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models. Our analysis on $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ reveals that closed-source frontier models can substantially outperform open-weight models with even $\approx10\%$ gap, even when they attain comparable performance on existing benchmarks. A more fine-grained analysis shows that no single model dominates across all task types, and that some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models. These results highlight the value of $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ as a diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in current modern models.

</details>


### [229] [MobiDiff: Semantic-Aware Multi-Channel Discrete Diffusion for Human Mobility Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08357)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Taichi Liu,Desheng Zhang,Yuan Tian,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Discrete diffusion framework MobiDiff for synthetic mobility data using multi-channel semantic skeletons, achieving fidelity and speed advantages over latent-continuous diffusion methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy and data collection costs in mobility data; existing diffusion methods rely on continuous or latent traces, hindering native modeling of discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures.

Method: End-to-end discrete diffusion that denoises multi-channel semantic skeletons. Each check-in is decomposed into spatial, activity, and temporal channels. Uses structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to capture both trajectory-level patterns and within-event dependencies. Avoids interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine pipelines. Evaluated on three large-scale city datasets (Atlanta, Boston, Seattle).

Result: MobiDiff preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions and remains competitive on broader mobility statistics. Demonstrates substantial inference speedup (about 5.3x faster than GeoGen). Overall, shows discrete diffusion can effectively generate realistic, privacy-friendly mobility data.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data. MobiDiff validates its viability, particularly in preserving key mobility semantics while delivering notable efficiency. Potential extensions include richer semantic events, enhanced masking strategies, and explicit privacy guarantees.
